# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 18

## Page 9141

Part Eighteen
The Book of Hadith

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

All praise belongs to Allah alone, and blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

A question submitted to the shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him.

The questioner said:

All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

O you who have mastered the science of hadith and transmitted … the practices of the Prophet, the chosen and elect,
You have become a steadfast mountain in Islam, … through which people find guidance, and are counted among the learned.
These questions have proved difficult; generously grant us … their explanation, O transmitters of reports.
For help in difficult matters is sought from those versed in them … as has been related in the transmitted reports.
[And yours is a reward like that of those who follow his Sunnah … when you are consulted, O people of insight.]

(*)

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (p. 142):
> This verse is in the kāmil meter, but its meter is defective. Perhaps it should read:
> “Yours is a reward like that of those who follow a sunnah … when consulted, O people of insight.”
> Or something similar. Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 9142

First:
What is the definition of a Prophetic hadith? Is it whatever he said during his lifetime, what he said after his prophetic mission began, or what he said by way of legislation?

Second:
What constitutes a single hadith? Is it comparable to a surah, a verse, or a sentence?

Third:
If a hadith is sound, must it necessarily be true, or not?

Fourth:
Is dividing hadiths into sound, good, and weak a valid classification, or do these categories overlap?

Fifth:
What constitutes a repeated hadith, retold in different wording, its meaning conveyed without addition or omission? Is it like the accounts repeated in the magnificent Quran?

Sixth:
How many hadiths are there in Sahīh al-Bukhārī, including repetitions, and how many excluding them? How many are there in Muslim’s collection, with and without repetitions? How many hadiths do both collections share, and how many does each contain that the other does not?

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-ʿAbbās Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—replied:

All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. When used without qualification, the term “Prophetic hadith” refers

---

## Page 9143

to what is reported from him after he became a prophet: his sayings, actions, and tacit approvals. His sunnah is established through these three means. If what he says conveys information, it must be believed; if it prescribes a ruling—an obligation, prohibition, or permission—he must be followed in it. The signs establishing the prophethood of the prophets also establish that they are protected from error in what they report from Allah, Mighty and Majestic; thus, what they report can only be true. This is the meaning of prophethood: Allah informs the prophet of the unseen, and he informs people of it. A messenger is commanded to call people to Allah and convey his Lord’s messages to them.

For this reason, every messenger is a prophet, but not every prophet is a messenger, although a prophet may be described as being sent in a qualified sense, as in His words:

“We never sent before you any messenger or prophet but that, when he recited, Satan cast something into his recitation. But Allah abolishes what Satan casts, then makes His verses firm. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

The Muslims have agreed that no falsehood can remain in what he conveyed, whether it is held that none of these satanic suggestions which Allah abolishes ever passed his lips, or that something did pass his lips which Allah abolished. In either case, Allah has abolished what Satan cast and made His verses firm; and Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. Thus, everything he says is true.

The following account has been narrated: “ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr used to write down what he heard from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Some people told him, ‘The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, speaks when angry, so do not write down everything you hear.’ So he asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace

---

## Page 9144

about this, and he replied, ‘Write, for by Him in whose hand is my soul, nothing but truth has come from between them’—meaning his two noble lips.”

It is authentically reported that Abū Hurayrah said: “None of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, retained more in memory than I did, except ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr. He would write with his hand and retain it in his heart, whereas I retained it in my heart but did not write with my hand.”

The family of ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀs possessed a written collection that he had recorded from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. On this basis, some people challenged the reports transmitted by ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father Shuʿayb, from his grandfather, saying: “It is a written collection.” Shuʿayb is Shuʿayb ibn Muhammad ibn ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀs. They said: “If ‘his grandfather’ means his nearer grandfather, Muhammad, then the report is disconnected, since he did not live to meet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. If he means his more distant grandfather, then its chain is interrupted, since Shuʿayb did not live to meet him.”

The leading scholars of Islam and the majority of scholars, however, accept the reports of ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather, as evidence when the transmission back to him is authentic. These include Mālik ibn Anas, Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah and others like them, as well as al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh and others. They said: “The grandfather is ʿAbdullāh, for he is explicitly named in some transmissions, and Muhammad lived during his lifetime.” They also said: “If it is a collection written during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, this lends it greater authority and provides stronger evidence of its authenticity.” For this reason, the written collection of ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb contained

---

## Page 9145

legal hadiths specifying prescribed amounts that Muslim scholars generally needed.

The point is that the term “hadith of the Messenger,” may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when used without qualification, includes accounts of what he said after becoming a prophet and what he did. His actions that Allah did not disallow constitute authoritative evidence, especially when he commanded us to follow them, as in his sayings:

“Pray as you have seen me pray,” and, “Learn your rites of pilgrimage from me.”

Likewise, whatever Allah made lawful for him is lawful for the Muslim community unless evidence establishes that it was exclusive to him. Thus, Allah said:

“So when Zayd had ended his marriage to her, We married her to you, so that there should be no restriction on believers marrying the wives of their adopted sons once their marriages to them have ended.”

When Allah permitted him to marry a woman who offered herself to him, He said:

“And a believing woman if she offers herself to the Prophet and the Prophet wishes to marry her—a privilege for you alone, not for the other believers.”

For this reason, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked about an action, he would tell the questioner that he himself did it, thereby making clear that it was permissible. When he was told, “Allah has forgiven your past and future sins,” he would say:

“I fear Allah more than any of you and know His limits better than any of you.”

Also included within the meaning of his hadith is whatever he tacitly approved: the profit-sharing partnerships they customarily practiced; his approval of ‘Ā’ishah playing with dolls; his approval, during the festivals, of such things as the two girls’ singing and the Abyssinians’ playing with spears in the mosque; and other such things. This also includes his approval of their eating spiny-tailed lizard at his table, even though

---

## Page 9146

it had been authentically reported from him that it was not forbidden. There are other similar examples. All of this falls within the meaning of hadith and is the subject of hadith scholarship, which seeks only what can serve as evidence in religious matters: his words, actions, or tacit approvals.

This may also include some accounts of him and aspects of his life before prophethood, such as his devotions in the Cave of Hirā and his exemplary conduct. Such accounts reveal the noble character and admirable deeds that distinguished him before prophethood, as expressed in Khadījah’s words to him:

“Never! By Allah, Allah will never disgrace you. You maintain ties of kinship, bear the burdens of the helpless, entertain guests, provide for the destitute, and help others meet rightful needs in times of adversity.”

Likewise, this includes knowing that he was unlettered, neither writing nor reading, and that no learned person had compiled anything comparable, although he was known for truthfulness and trustworthiness. Other such information provides evidence about his circumstances that helps establish his prophethood and truthfulness. These matters are therefore of considerable use in demonstrating the proofs of prophethood. For this reason, such accounts appear in books about his life, alongside his lineage, relatives, and other information through which his circumstances become known. This, too, may fall within the meaning of hadith.

The books containing accounts of him include works of Quranic commentary, works on his life and military expeditions, and collections of hadith. Hadith collections are more particularly concerned with what occurred after prophethood, although they also contain accounts of events before prophethood. Those earlier events are not recounted so that his conduct before prophethood should be adopted and prescribed as religious practice. Rather, Muslims unanimously agree that what

---

## Page 9147

Allah has required His servants to believe in and act upon is what the Prophet brought after prophethood. Thus, in their view, anyone who abandons the Friday and congregational prayers and withdraws into caves and mountains where neither is held, claiming to follow the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—because he used to devote himself to worship in the Cave of Hirā before prophethood, is mistaken. Such a person abandons the prescribed acts of worship that Allah commanded His Messenger to perform, instead following what he did before prophethood.

After Allah honoured him with prophethood, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—no longer practised his earlier devotions in the Cave of Hirā or anything similar. He remained in Mecca for over ten years after prophethood and returned there after the migration for the make-up umrah, the conquest of Mecca, and the umrah from al-Ji‘rānah, yet he did not seek out the Cave of Hirā. Likewise, none of his Companions after him would visit the Cave of Hirā, nor would they withdraw to remote places and abandon the Friday and congregational prayers. None of them undertook a forty-day retreat, as some later people do. Rather, they worshipped Allah through the prescribed acts of worship instituted for them by the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in whom Allah required them to believe and whom He required them to follow. These included the five daily prayers and other prayers, fasting and devotional retreats in mosques, various forms of remembrance and supplication, recitation, and jihad.

As for the questioner’s words, “what he said during his lifetime, or after prophethood, or by way of legislation”: everything he said after prophethood that was allowed to stand and was not abrogated constitutes legislation. However, legislation

---

## Page 9148

encompasses obligation, prohibition, and permission. This includes the medical benefits to which he directed people, for this establishes the permissibility of using and benefiting from that remedy. It is thus legislation establishing its permissibility, and it may also establish that it is recommended. People have differed over whether seeking treatment is permissible, recommended, or obligatory.

The correct assessment is that some forms of treatment are forbidden, some are disliked, some are permissible, and some are recommended. Some may even be obligatory: namely, treatment known to be the only means of preserving life. This is comparable to the obligation to eat carrion when compelled by necessity, which is obligatory according to the four imams and the majority of scholars. Masrūq said, “Whoever is compelled by necessity to eat carrion but refuses to eat it until he dies will enter the Fire.” Sometimes, when an illness becomes severe, a person may reach a condition in which he will die without treatment, whereas the customary treatment will preserve his life—as with nourishing someone who is weak, or drawing blood in certain cases.

The point is that a religious ruling can be derived from all his statements. When he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—saw them pollinating date palms, he said to them, “I do not think this…”—meaning, that it achieves anything. He then said to them:

“I merely expressed an opinion, so do not hold me to an opinion. But when I tell you something from Allah, I will never speak falsely about Allah.”

He also said:

“You know best about your worldly affairs; as for matters of your religion, refer to me.”

He did not forbid them to pollinate the trees. Rather, they were mistaken in thinking that he had forbidden them, just as some mistakenly thought that “the white thread” and “the black thread” meant white and black cords.

---

## Page 9149

Section

As for “a single hadith,” this means the connected discourse narrated by a Companion, even if it consists of many sentences, such as the hadith concerning the repentance of Kaʿb ibn Mālik, the hadith concerning the beginning of revelation, the hadith concerning the slander, and other lengthy hadiths. Each of these is called a hadith. Likewise, what a Companion narrates consisting of one sentence, two sentences, or more, connected to one another, is called a hadith, such as his sayings:

“No prayer is valid without reciting the Opening Chapter of the Quran.”

“A neighbor has a greater claim by virtue of his proximity.”

“Allah does not accept the prayer of any of you who has invalidated his state of ritual purity until he performs ablution.”

And his saying:

“Actions are judged only by intentions, and each person shall have only what he intended,”

and the rest of that narration: this is called a hadith.

Likewise, his saying:

“Do not sever ties with one another, turn your backs on one another, hate one another, or envy one another. Be brothers, O servants of Allah.”

And his saying concerning the sea:

“Its water is purifying, and its dead creatures are lawful to eat.”

A hadith may be composed of statements of different kinds, but generally they share a common meaning, as in his saying:

“A man must not propose marriage where his brother has already proposed, interfere with his brother’s sale, or bid against his brother’s bid, nor

---

## Page 9150

should a woman ask for her sister’s divorce so as to empty her bowl and marry her husband, for she will have only what has been decreed for her.”

This prohibits competing with a Muslim in matters of sale and marriage. In sales, one must neither bid against him nor interfere with his sale. If bidding against him is forbidden, then interfering with his purchase is all the more forbidden. He also forbade proposing marriage where another Muslim has already proposed. This implies, all the more, a prohibition on depriving him of his wife. He likewise forbade a woman to seek her sister’s divorce so that she alone might have the husband. Although these prohibitions concern both sale and marriage, they share a common meaning.

Likewise, his saying:

“There are three to whom Allah will not speak, at whom He will not look on the Day of Resurrection, and whom He will not purify; theirs will be a painful punishment: an elderly adulterer, a lying king, and an arrogant pauper.”

These three share this threatened punishment, and they also share the fact that they commit these sins despite having little incentive to do so. An elderly man’s urge to commit adultery is weak. Likewise, a king has little incentive to lie, since he has no need of it, and a poor man has little incentive to be arrogant. When they commit these sins despite such weak incentives, this indicates an evil within their souls for which they deserve a threatened punishment that others do not deserve.

A single hadith rarely contains several statements unless there is some connection between them, although that connection may sometimes escape some people. Thus, connected discourse is called a single hadith.

---

## Page 9151

But if a Companion narrates a statement, finishes it, and then narrates another, separating the two by saying, “And the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said,” or by leaving a long interval between them, these are two hadiths. This is comparable to continuity in a person’s speech, admissions, and testimony, and in the wording of marriage contracts, sales, admissions, and endowments. When the wording follows continuously in the customary manner, it constitutes a single, interconnected whole. But if he finishes speaking and, after a long interval, begins another statement that is not subject to the same ruling as the first, this constitutes a second statement.

A single hadith is therefore not the same as a single sentence, since it may comprise several sentences. Nor is it like a single surah, since some parts of a surah may have been revealed before or after others and may be unrelated to them. Rather, it resembles a single verse or a series of interconnected verses, such as the four verses revealed at the beginning of al-Baqarah describing the believers, the two describing the disbelievers, and the ten-odd verses describing the hypocrites. Another example is His saying:

“Indeed, We have sent down to you the Book in truth so that you may judge between people by what Allah has shown you. And do not be an advocate for the treacherous.”

This passage, through to the end of the discussion, forms a connected whole and was revealed on account of the incident involving Banū Ubayriq.

A hadith may also be called a single hadith even when it contains several accounts, if the Companion narrates them continuously. It is then considered one hadith because of its continuity in the Companion’s narration. An example is the lengthy hadith of Jābir in which he says:

“We were with the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace).”

In it, he mentioned what related to his miracles and what

---

## Page 9152

related to prayer and other matters. In this respect, it is called a single hadith. A hadith may also be lengthy, and some narrators may divide it into several hadiths, as al-Bukhārī did with Abū Bakr’s letter concerning zakat. This is permissible provided that it does not alter the meaning.

Section

As for the questioner’s words, “If a hadith is authentic, does that mean it is true?” the answer is that authentic hadiths are of several types, and two things may be meant by their being true.

Some authentic hadiths are mass-transmitted in their wording, such as his saying:

“Whoever deliberately tells a lie about me, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

Others are mass-transmitted in meaning, such as the hadiths concerning intercession, the vision of Allah, the Cistern, water flowing from between his fingers, and other matters. Such reports yield certain knowledge, and their truth is affirmed with certainty, because they are mass-transmitted either in wording or in meaning.

Among authentic hadiths are also those that the Muslims have accepted and acted upon, such as the hadith concerning the prescribed compensation for a fetus, the hadiths concerning pre-emption, those concerning the prostrations for forgetfulness, and similar reports. These yield certain knowledge, and their truth is affirmed with certainty, because the Muslim community has accepted them as true and acted in accordance with them. The Muslim community does not agree upon misguidance. Were such a report actually false, the community would have agreed upon believing a falsehood and acting

---

## Page 9153

in accordance with it, which is impossible for the community.

Other authentic hadiths are those that hadith scholars have accepted and affirmed as true, such as the majority of the hadiths in al-Bukhārī and Muslim. All hadith scholars are certain of the authenticity of the majority of the hadiths in these two collections, and everyone else follows their lead in knowledge of hadith. The consensus of hadith scholars that a particular report is true is like the consensus of jurists that a particular act is lawful, unlawful, or obligatory. When the scholars agree on something, the rest of the community follows them. Their consensus is protected from error: they cannot agree upon something mistaken.

Among the reports that may be called authentic are those that some hadith scholars grade as authentic while others dispute their assessment, saying, “It is weak, not authentic.” Examples include certain wordings narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh whose authenticity other scholars disputed, whether those scholars were his equals, below him, or above him in rank. Such reports cannot be affirmed as true with certainty without evidence.

One example is the hadith narrated by Ibn Waʿlah from Ibn ʿAbbās that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Any hide that has been tanned has become pure.”

Muslim alone, rather than al-Bukhārī, narrated this report. Imam Ahmad and others judged it weak, although Muslim narrated it.

Another example is Muslim’s report that:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the eclipse prayer with three bowings and with four bowings.”

Muslim alone, rather than al-Bukhārī, narrated this. The most discerning scholars judged it weak and said that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the eclipse prayer only once, on the day his son Abraham died. In these very hadiths, which describe the prayer as having three bowings

---

## Page 9154

and four bowings, it is stated that he performed that prayer only on the day Abraham died. It is well known that Abraham did not die twice, nor did the Prophet have two sons named Abraham. Mass-transmitted reports establish that he performed the eclipse prayer that day with two bowings in each unit of prayer, as narrated from him by ʿĀ’ishah, Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn ʿAmr and others. For this reason, al-Bukhārī narrated only these hadiths. This is an omission on Muslim’s part. Accordingly, al-Shāfiʿī and others judged the hadiths mentioning three and four bowings to be weak and did not regard that practice as recommended. This is the sounder of the two positions reported from Ahmad. It is also reported that he used to permit it before the weakness of these hadiths became clear to him.

Another example is the hadith narrated by Muslim:

“Allah created the soil on Saturday, the mountains on Sunday, the trees on Monday, harmful things on Tuesday, and light on Wednesday. He spread the animals throughout the earth on Thursday and created Adam on Friday.”

This hadith was challenged by scholars more knowledgeable than Muslim, such as Yahyā ibn Maʿīn, al-Bukhārī and others. Al-Bukhārī stated that these were the words of Kaʿb al-Ahbār. Some scholars considered it authentic, including Abū Bakr ibn al-Anbārī, Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī and others. Al-Bayhaqī and others agreed with those who judged it weak. This is the correct view, for it is established through mass transmission that Allah created the heavens, the earth and everything between them in six days. It is also established that creation was completed on Friday, which necessarily means that it began on Sunday. This is also the account held by the People of the Scripture, and the names of the days indicate this. It is likewise what is reliably transmitted in other hadiths and reports;

---

## Page 9155

and if creation had begun on Saturday and ended on Friday, it would have taken seven days, contrary to what the Quran states. Moreover, expert hadith scholars establish that this hadith is defective on other grounds, showing that a particular narrator’s version is mistaken for reasons they explain. This is what is called knowledge of hidden defects in hadith: a hadith’s chain of transmission may appear sound, yet other evidence reveals that its narrator mistakenly attributed it to the Prophet when it actually stopped at a Companion, supplied a connected chain when it was in fact disconnected, or confused one hadith with another.

This is a distinguished discipline. Yahyā ibn Saʿīd al-Ansārī, then his associate ʿAlī ibn al-Madīnī, and then al-Bukhārī were among its most knowledgeable scholars, as were Imam Ahmad, Abū Hātim, al-Nasāʾī, al-Dāraqutnī and others. Well-known works have been written on it.

Even in al-Bukhārī’s own collection, there are three hadiths whose authenticity some have disputed. One example is the hadith of Abū Bakrah, who reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said of al-Hasan:

“This son of mine is a leader, and through him Allah will reconcile two great groups of Muslims.”

A number of scholars, including Abū al-Walīd al-Bājī, disputed al-Bukhārī’s judgment, claiming that the narrator al-Hasan had not heard this hadith from Abū Bakrah. But al-Bukhārī is correct: al-Hasan did hear it from Abū Bakrah, as has been explained elsewhere. This has also been established elsewhere. Al-Bukhārī is more skilled and experienced in this discipline than Muslim; for this reason, they never agree on

---

## Page 9156

a hadith unless it is unquestionably authentic and the scholars have agreed upon its authenticity. Muslim may then include particular wordings that al-Bukhārī omits and that some hadith scholars judge weak. Those who judge them weak may be correct, as with the reports of the eclipse prayer involving three or four bowings. Or Muslim may be correct—and this is more often the case—as with the wording in the hadith of Abū Mūsā:

“The imam has only been appointed to be followed. When he says ‘Allah is greatest,’ say the same, and when he recites, listen silently.”

Muslim judged this additional wording authentic, and Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others accepted it, whereas al-Bukhārī judged it weak. This addition accords with the Quran. Even if no authentic hadith had been reported concerning it, acting upon the Quran would still be obligatory. Regarding Allah’s words:

“When the Quran is recited, listen to it attentively and remain silent, so that you may be shown mercy,”

there is unanimous agreement that this verse was revealed concerning prayer and that recitation during prayer is intended by this text.

Thus, the most balanced view concerning recitation behind the imam is that when a person praying behind him hears his recitation, he should listen silently, reciting neither al-Fātihah nor anything else. If he does not hear the imam’s recitation, he should recite al-Fātihah and additional passages. This is the view of the majority of earlier and later scholars. It is the position of Mālik and his followers, and of Ahmad ibn Hanbal and the majority of his followers. It is also one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two views, adopted by a number of the discerning scholars of his school, and is the view of Muhammad ibn al-Hasan and others among the followers of Abū Hanīfah.

As for the view held by a group of scholars, such as Abū Hanīfah and Abū Yūsuf, that one

---

## Page 9157

should not recite behind the imam, neither al-Fātihah nor anything else, in either silent or audible prayers—this stands in contrast to the view that reciting al-Fātihah is obligatory even when one hears the imam’s recitation. The latter is al-Shāfiʿī’s other view, his later position, and is also the view of al-Bukhārī, Ibn Hazm, and others.

There is a third view: that reciting al-Fātihah is recommended when one hears the imam’s recitation. This is reported from al-Layth and al-Awzāʿī, and was the position preferred by my grandfather, Abū al-Barakāt. However, the strongest view is that of the majority, because the Quran and Sunnah indicate that a person praying behind the imam must listen silently when he hears the imam’s recitation.

Scholars have differed over whether a person’s prayer is invalidated if he recites while hearing the imam’s recitation. There are two views, which Abū ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hāmid mentioned as two positions within Ahmad’s school. They have unanimously agreed that, for passages beyond al-Fātihah, listening to the imam’s recitation is better than reciting along with him. It follows that the listener gains more than someone who recites along with the imam. Accordingly, listening to the imam recite al-Fātihah achieves the purpose of recitation and more, making it sufficient in place of reciting along with him, which has been forbidden.

The situation is different when he cannot hear the imam. Reciting the Book of Allah, for which he receives ten good deeds for every letter, is better than remaining silent without benefit, leaving himself open to distracting whispers and inner thoughts for which there is no reward. Recitation for which he is rewarded is better than inner thoughts for which he receives no reward. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 9158

The point here is to illustrate the case of a hadith reported in an authentic collection whose authenticity some scholars dispute: the stronger assessment may sometimes favor its authenticity and sometimes oppose it. Such cases allow scope for scholarly judgment in authenticating hadith, just as legal rulings allow scope for scholarly judgment. As for reports whose authenticity the scholars unanimously accept, these are comparable to legal rulings on which the scholars agree. Such reports can only be true. Most of the hadith texts in the authentic collections belong to this category.

These texts are generally transmitted from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, through several routes: one Companion and another relate them without having colluded. This yields certain knowledge. If a narrator relates a lengthy hadith that he heard, and another relates it, stating that he too heard it, and it is known that they did not collude to fabricate it, then it is known to be true. Were it not true, it would be false, whether deliberately or by mistake. For when a narrator reports something contrary to the truth, he is either deliberately lying or making an error. If he neither deliberately lies nor makes an error, his report can only be true.

In the ordinary course of events, it is impossible for two people to happen upon the same fabricated lengthy account without colluding. This occurs frequently in hadith: a report is narrated by Abū Hurayrah and Abū Sa‘īd, or by Abū Hurayrah and ‘Ā’ishah, or by Abū Hurayrah and Ibn ‘Umar or Ibn ‘Abbās, when it is known that neither took it from the other. One example is the lengthy hadith concerning the divine manifestation on the Day of Resurrection. Abū Hurayrah related it while Abū Sa‘īd remained silent, objecting to not a single word. Indeed, he agreed with Abū Hurayrah on the entire report except for a single expression at its end.

---

## Page 9159

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, may have related it on different occasions, with each of the two hearing it on a separate occasion. Thus, one related what he heard from him on one occasion, and the other what he heard on another. All of it is included in the report containing the additional wording. And Allah knows best.

Section

As for dividing hadith into authentic, good, and weak, the first person known to have made this classification was Abū ‘Īsā al-Tirmidhī. This classification is not known from anyone before him. Abū ‘Īsā explained what he meant by it.

He stated that a good hadith is one transmitted through several routes, none of whose narrators is suspected of lying, and which is not anomalous. It ranks below an authentic hadith, whose transmitters are known for their integrity and accuracy.

He also stated that a weak hadith is one whose transmitter is known to be suspected of lying and to have a poor memory. When an unknown narrator relates a report, there is reason to fear that he may be a liar or have a poor memory. If another narrator agrees with him without having taken the report from him, it is known that he did not deliberately fabricate it. For two narrators to coincide in the same lengthy wording may sometimes be impossible and sometimes improbable. Since such coincidence remains conceivable, however, the report falls below the rank of authentic.

Some people have objected to al-Tirmidhī’s classification, saying that he uses the designation “good and singular.” A singular report is one transmitted by a single narrator, and a hadith may

---

## Page 9160

be authentic and singular, such as the hadith, “Actions are judged only by intentions”; the hadith, “He forbade selling or giving away the right of patronage”; and the hadith, “He entered Mecca wearing a helmet.” These are authentic reports that have been accepted. The first is not authentically established from anyone other than ‘Umar; the second is not known from anyone other than his son ‘Abd Allāh; and the third is known only through al-Zuhrī’s narration from Anas. However, those who criticized al-Tirmidhī did not understand what he meant by much of what he said.

Hadith scholars may say, “This hadith is singular,” meaning through this particular route. They sometimes state this explicitly, saying, “Singular through this route.” Thus, a hadith may be authentic and well known to them through one route, but when transmitted through another route, it is singular through that route, even though its text is authentic and well known. Accordingly, when al-Tirmidhī says, “Good and singular,” he may mean that it is singular through that particular route, while its text has corroborating reports that bring it into the category of good hadiths.

Other scholars dispute some of al-Tirmidhī’s assessments of authenticity, just as they may dispute some of his assessments of reports as weak or good. He may judge a hadith weak while al-Bukhārī judges it authentic. One example is the report of Ibn Mas‘ūd: when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to him, “Find me stones with which to cleanse myself,” he said, “I brought him two stones and a piece of dung.” He said, “He took the two stones, left the dung, and said, ‘It is impure.’” This report was transmitted in differing versions from Abū Ishāq al-Sabī‘ī, and al-Tirmidhī regarded this variation

---

## Page 9161

as a defect, and favored the version he narrated from Abū ‘Ubayda, from his father, although Abū ‘Ubayda had not heard from his father. Al-Bukhārī, however, judged it authentic through another route, because Abū Ishāq would have the same hadith from several narrators, relating it sometimes from one and sometimes from another. Likewise, al-Zuhrī would sometimes narrate a hadith from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib, sometimes from Abū Salama, and sometimes from both together. Someone unfamiliar with this practice might suppose that narrating it sometimes from one and sometimes from the other was an error, although both versions are authentic. This subject would require a lengthy discussion.

As for the scholars before al-Tirmidhī, this threefold classification is not known to have been used by them. Rather, they divided hadiths into authentic and weak. For them, weak hadiths were of two kinds:

Those whose weakness did not preclude acting upon them, resembling what al-Tirmidhī termed good; and those whose weakness required their rejection—the very weak reports. This is comparable to a sick person’s illness: it may be life-threatening, so that his gifts are charged against one third of his estate, or it may not be life-threatening. This distinction is found in the statements of Imam Ahmad and others.

Hence they say, “This is somewhat weak,” or, “There is weakness in it.” Such descriptions occur in their assessments of hadith.

---

## Page 9162

Among hadith scholars are masters of precision, such as Shu‘ba, Mālik, al-Thawrī, Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Qattān, and ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī. They attained the highest degree of precision and retention, unlike those below their rank. These scholars may consider a narrator weak because of the many errors in his reports. Yet, if his reports are predominantly sound, they can serve for comparison and corroboration. Multiple chains of transmission strengthen one another and may even yield certain knowledge, even if the transmitters are wicked and sinful. How much more so when they are upright scholars whose reports nevertheless contain many errors.

An example is ‘Abd Allāh ibn Lahī‘a, who was among the leading Muslim scholars. He served as a judge in Egypt and narrated many hadiths. However, his books were burned, so he began narrating from memory, and many errors entered his reports, although most remained sound. Ahmad said, “I may record a man’s reports for comparison with other reports—as in the case of Ibn Lahī‘a.”

As for someone known to lie deliberately, some scholars would transmit nothing from him. This was the approach of Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others. In his Musnad, Ahmad did not narrate from anyone he knew to lie deliberately. He did, however, narrate from those he knew to make mistakes, for comparison and corroboration.

Other scholars would listen to the reports of narrators who lied, saying that they

---

## Page 9163

could distinguish what was false from what was not. It is reported that al-Thawrī used to accept reports from al-Kalbī while forbidding others to do so, and that he could tell the difference. Something similar may occur when someone knows a person well: when that person relates various things to him, he can distinguish what he has told truthfully from what he has lied about, through contextual indicators that cannot be precisely defined.

A report from a single narrator may be accompanied by indicators showing that he has spoken truthfully, or by indicators showing that he has lied (1).

## Page 9164

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section: Types of Transmission and Their Designations

These include “He narrated to us,” “He informed us,” “He notified us,” “I heard,” “I read,” oral communication, handing over written material, written correspondence, authorization to transmit, transmission from a discovered manuscript, and similar forms.

We say: the discussion concerns two matters. The first is the means by which transmission is valid and continuity of the chain is established. The second is the wording used to express this.

There are several types. The first is hearing the narrator’s own words, whether one sees him or not, just as the Companions heard the Quran, as well as hadith, from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; and as he used to recite it to them and recited Sūrat Lam Yakun (“They Were Not”) to Ubayy. In this matter, people have made no distinction between the two situations, unlike the distinction some jurists have made regarding testimony. Then that speaker

---

## Page 9165

sometimes intends to narrate to that individual alone, or to a particular group of which he is a member; sometimes he intends to narrate generally to anyone who hears him, and that individual is among the listeners; and sometimes he intends to narrate to someone else, but that individual hears him. In all these cases, if the listener says, “I heard so-and-so say,” he has spoken accurately. If he says, “He narrated to us” or “He narrated to me”—and the narrator intended to address him, whether specifically or as part of a general audience—he has likewise spoken accurately. This is comparable to a witness saying, concerning a judgment, acknowledgment, or testimony that he was called upon to witness, “He called upon me to bear witness” or “He called upon us to bear witness.”

If, however, the narrator intended to address someone else and he overheard him, this is comparable to someone being entrusted with testimony while another person hears it. The latter’s testimony is valid, but his use of “He called upon me to bear witness” or “He narrated to us” is questionable. Rather, it would be appropriate for him to say, “He narrated while I was listening.”

If the speaker was not narrating to anyone, but the listener merely heard him speaking about the hadith, this resembles bearing testimony without having been entrusted with it, or testifying to an acknowledgment without having been called upon to witness it. Testimony concerning a judgment, unlike testimony concerning the establishment of a fact, through hearing and the like, is valid by agreement without being formally entrusted with it. As for testimony concerning statements, such as testimonies and acknowledgments, there is disagreement about it, which is not for discussion here.

The rules governing narration are broader. Nevertheless, someone who intends to narrate to another is not like someone speaking to himself. A man may speak to himself about various matters and speak freely; once he knows that someone else is receiving his words for transmission, he becomes more careful. For this reason, they did not readily transmit hadiths exchanged in informal scholarly discussions.

---

## Page 9166

Imam Ahmad would discuss matters from memory, but when a listener asked to receive them for transmission, he would bring out his book and narrate from it.

There are thus three levels: intending to entrust the listener with the hadith so that he may narrate it on the speaker’s authority; intending to tell it to him, but not for him to narrate it on his authority; and intending only to speak of it to oneself.

(The second type)

This is when someone reads to the hadith scholar, who confirms what is read, just as a student recites the Quran to a teacher. The scholars of the Hijaz call this “presentation,” because the person receiving the hadith presents it to the person transmitting it. This resembles presenting a recitation, or presenting an acknowledgment, judgment, contract, or testimony for confirmation to the person whose words or actions are to be attested: the judge, witness, person making the acknowledgment, or contracting party.

Dimām ibn Thaʿlabah presented to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, what his messenger had conveyed, and he would answer, “Yes.” According to Mālik, Ahmad, and the majority of the early generations, this is equivalent to an explicit verbal statement.

For this reason, we have said that if the officiant asks the guardian, “Have you given her in marriage?” and he answers, “Yes,” and asks the husband, “Have you accepted?” and he answers, “Yes,” the marriage is contracted, and these are explicit declarations. For “yes” takes the place of uttering the complete statement about which the question was asked.

Thus, when people are asked, “Have you found what your Lord promised to be true?” or “Did Allah command you to do that?” or “Did so-and-so narrate such-and-such to you?” or “Have you given so-and-so in marriage on such terms?” and the answer is “Yes,” this is equivalent to saying, “I have found what my Lord promised to be true,” “Allah commanded me to do such-and-such,” “So-and-so narrated such-and-such to me,” or “I have given so-and-so in marriage on such terms.” The difference is that the former is an answer to a question, whereas the latter is a statement made independently. “Yes” is a concise word that dispenses with spelling out the details.

---

## Page 9167

The person presenting the hadith may also say, “He narrated it to you,” as a statement rather than a question, and the scholar replies, “Yes.” Some scholars of Medina and elsewhere prefer this method of presentation because the recipient has mastered the hadith, while the transmitter corrects him and sets it right for him. This view is reported from Mālik and others. Others prefer hearing the hadith directly, a position similar to that of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī. Some, such as the scholars of the Hijaz, permit both “he informed us” and “he narrated to us” when reporting a hadith received through presentation. Others, including various groups of scholars, use only “he informed us.” Two positions are reported from Ahmad.

Some say, “There is no linguistic difference; those who distinguish between them do so only by convention.” Thus, regarding testimony obtained by presenting a judgment, acknowledgment, or contract for confirmation, one says, “He made me a witness to such-and-such.”

It may also be said that khabar (information) originally concerns hidden matters. From this comes khibrah (expert knowledge) of things, meaning knowledge of their inner aspects. Thus one says, “So-and-so has expertise in such-and-such.” A khabīr (expert) in a matter is someone acquainted with its inner aspects. From this too comes khabīr in the sense of a farmer who brings what lies beneath the soil to the surface, and khabār, soft soil that is turned over. Mukhābarah (sharecropping) is also derived from this.

The transmitter’s reply, “Yes,” does not convey the statement in question through its outward wording alone. Rather, it conveys it through its underlying meaning: the word signifies agreement with the questioner or the person making the statement. If someone asks, “Did he narrate to you?” or “Have you given her in marriage?” and the reply is “Yes,” this amounts to saying, “He narrated to me” or “I have given her in marriage.” This meaning arises from the combination of the word “yes” and the question asked, just as demonstratives and pronouns identify the object indicated and the explicit antecedent

---

## Page 9168

through their wording and the accompanying indications pointing to the referent and to the explicit antecedent explaining the pronoun.

A better formulation when presenting a hadith for confirmation is, “He narrated to me that So-and-so said,” or “He informed me that So-and-so said,” rather than, “So-and-so informed us, saying, ‘He informed us,’” or “So-and-so narrated to us, saying, ‘He narrated to us.’” This is also the formulation used in testimony: “I testify that So-and-so, son of So-and-so, made an acknowledgment,” or “that he issued a judgment,” or “that he established an endowment.” Likewise, some hadith masters distinguish transmission by authorization from other methods. For authorization, they say, “So-and-so informed us (anā) that So-and-so narrated to them,” unlike their wording for direct hearing.

Some have held that there is no difference between the two formulations. They have even preferred anna (“that”), supposing that it adds emphasis. But the matter is not as they imagined: anna, with its initial a, together with its predicate, functions as a verbal noun. Thus, “He narrated to me that he said” amounts to “He narrated his statement to me.” Hence the grammarians agree that inna, with its initial i, occupies the position of a complete sentence, whereas anna occupies the position of a single nominal expression.

Thus, His words:

“The angels called to him while he stood praying in the sanctuary, that Allah gives you glad tidings”

—according to the reading with anna—amount to saying, “They called to him with His glad tidings.” This conveys the meaning of what they called out to him, without reproducing the wording. Whoever reads inna Allāha (“Indeed, Allah”), however, reproduces the actual wording. The same distinction applies between saying, “The first thing I say is, ‘I praise Allah,’” and “The first thing I say is, ‘Indeed, I praise Allah.’”

---

## Page 9169

And since the form with the initial a functions as a verbal noun, when you say, “He related his statement and his report to me,” you do not reproduce the wording of that statement or report; rather, you describe his utterance as a whole as a statement and a report. This is like saying, “I heard So-and-so’s speech and So-and-so’s sermon,” without quoting their wording. But when you say, “He said such-and-such,” you report his actual words. For this reason, no one should require the exact wording in this case, unlike the former, which is permissible only according to those who allow transmission by meaning.

Thus, if you hear his wording and say, “So-and-so narrated to me, saying, ‘So-and-so narrated such-and-such to me,’” you have reproduced the wording, for you heard him say, “So-and-so narrated such-and-such to me.” If, however, you present it to him for confirmation, asking, “Did So-and-so narrate such-and-such to you?” and he replies, “Yes,” and you then say, “He narrated to me that So-and-so had narrated such-and-such to him,” you are speaking truthfully according to both views. For you have stated that he informed you of So-and-so’s having narrated such-and-such to him. “Narrating” is a general expression that encompasses this, just as his reply, “Yes,” is a concise expression encompassing it. His saying “Yes” thus informs you that the other person narrated it to him.

But if you say, “He narrated to me, saying, ‘He narrated to me,’” you did not hear him say, “He narrated to me”; you only heard him say, “Yes.” It conveys the same meaning, however, and this is a commonly understood equivalence in meaning.

This discussion of presentation for confirmation applies when the transmitting teacher knows what the reader is reading to him, just as a Quran teacher knows what is being recited to him. If he does not know, then hearing the teacher narrate is undoubtedly better, as the later scholars agreed, because the reader of the hadith plays the more active role, rather than the person to whom it is read. In presentation for confirmation, a distinction must also be made between whether the transmitting teacher intends to convey information or not, as discussed earlier concerning narration and hearing.

---

## Page 9170

(The Third Type)

“Handing Over a Written Text and Correspondence”

In both cases, he gives the recipient a written text rather than addressing him orally. Handing it over, however, is direct, whereas correspondence involves an intermediary. Thus, handing over is preferable when the two methods are otherwise equivalent—for example, when he hands over specific hadiths known to him, or writes to the recipient conveying those same hadiths. Handing over is presentation for confirmation in summary form, for his statement concerning what he has with him … (1).

If, however, he writes to the recipient conveying specific hadiths, but hands him a book without specifying its contents, correspondence is preferable. In correspondence, it is sufficient to know that the handwriting is his. This was not disputed even by those who disputed the validity of a judge’s letter to another judge and of written testimony. In those cases, the jurists differed: Is testimony concerning the letter required? If so, must the testimony concern its actual contents, or is testimony concerning the letter itself sufficient? Those who required testimony made reliance rest upon the witnesses testifying concerning the judge who wrote it, so that someone other than the judge to whom it was addressed could act upon the letter.

Furthermore, “correspondence” involves the intention to convey the information contained in the letter. If that intention concerns its recipient, he may truthfully say, “He wrote to me,” or, “He showed me his letter.” If, however, the writer addressed someone else and this person read the letter, it is like his narrating to someone else while this person hears

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A lacuna in the original.

---

## Page 9171

the words spoken. Even if he did not correspond with anyone, but merely wrote something in his own hand, reading his handwriting is like hearing his words. This is what they call wijādah (finding a written narration). As noted previously, the narrator has neither narrated this nor rejected it, even though he may have said and written it. Not everything a person says or writes is something he considers fit to narrate, communicate to others, or have transmitted from him.

(The Fourth Type)

Authorization to Narrate

When authorization concerns specific material known to the person granting it, it is like handing over a written text. It is a “presentation of a presentation.” In presentation, the person presenting the material recites it in detail, and the shaykh says, “Yes.” The person seeking authorization, however, asks, “Have you authorized me to narrate what is in this book?” and the person granting authorization says, “Yes.” The difference between the two is that, in presentation, the shaykh hears the entire hadith, whereas here he hears words referring to it, having learned their content by examining what is in the book or by some similar means.

Such authorization constitutes narration and communication. Reports from some of the early scholars of Medina and elsewhere state that they regarded authorization as equivalent to hearing directly, and treated the expressions “He narrated to us,” “He informed us,” “He told us,” and “I heard” as equivalent. What they intended—and Allah knows best—was this kind of authorization: for example, someone coming to Mālik and saying, “This is al-Muwatta’; authorize me to narrate it,” whereupon he grants him authorization.

As for authorization that leaves the material unspecified, it resembles authorization that leaves the recipient unspecified. If someone says, “I authorize you to narrate whatever you establish as belonging to my hadiths,” narration on that basis becomes dependent upon knowing that the material belongs to his hadiths. If this is learned directly from him, authorization is unnecessary. If it is learned from someone else, then that other person is the one who has narrated it to him on his authority

---

## Page 9172

and the authorization has not acquainted him with the hadith or imparted knowledge of it to him as hearing it directly from him or presenting it to him would have done. Hence, nothing comparable to this exists in testimony.

As for practices corresponding to correspondence and handing over a written text, the jurists have differed over their permissibility in testimony. However, I have explained elsewhere that narration serves two purposes: knowledge and the chain of transmission. Knowledge is not acquired through authorization, whereas a chain of transmission is established through it. Someone who recites the Quran to a shaykh today is, in terms of knowledge, on the same footing as someone who recited it five hundred years ago. In terms of the chain of transmission, however, reciting to a teacher whose chain is closer to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, affords a higher chain.

The same applies to hadiths mass-transmitted from Mālik, al-Thawrī, and Ibn ʿUlayyah, as with the mass transmission of al-Muwatta’ from Mālik, Sunan Abī Dāwūd from its author, and Sahīh al-Bukhārī from its author. In terms of knowledge and understanding, it makes no difference whether there is one intermediary between a person and al-Bukhārī or two, because the book is mass-transmitted from him. As for the chain of transmission, a higher chain is more distinguished than a lower one. The benefit of unrestricted authorization is therefore akin to the benefit of a higher chain over a lower one when it adds nothing to one’s knowledge.

Is pursuing this aim a recommended religious practice? This must be determined from scriptural evidence. Ahmad said:

“Seeking a higher chain of transmission is a practice handed down from our predecessors. The companions of ʿAbd Allāh would travel from Kufa to Medina to speak directly with the Companions.”

We therefore say: the shorter the chain, the less burdensome and demanding it is, and the easier narration becomes. If a hadith is known to be authentic, and it is known that

---

## Page 9173

so-and-so narrated it, and that narrating from him serves to maintain continuity of transmission, then a shorter chain is better than a longer one. This, then, is the benefit of authorization.

The crucial point is to distinguish between a chain of transmission that establishes authenticity and narration that yields knowledge, on the one hand, and a chain that enables narration and narration that supplies a chain of transmission, on the other. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 9174

He was also asked:

“What is meant when a hadith is described as fair, mursal (disconnected), or singular? How does al-Tirmidhī combine the descriptions ‘singular’ and ‘authentic’ for a single hadith? Are there hadiths mass-transmitted in both wording and meaning? Do most authentic hadiths yield certainty or probability? What are the criteria of al-Bukhārī and Muslim, given that scholars distinguish between their respective criteria and say, ‘According to the criteria of al-Bukhārī and Muslim’?”

He replied:

A mursal hadith is one transmitted by someone other than a Companion without naming the Companion from whom he received it; he may, however, have received it from someone other than a Companion. Some scholars apply the term mursal only to reports transmitted in this manner by a Successor, while others also apply it to reports transmitted in this manner by someone other than a Successor.

Likewise, when a narrator is omitted from a chain of transmission, some scholars reserve the term munqatiʿ (interrupted) for it, while others include it under the term mursal. Some also call every mursal report interrupted. All these usages are linguistically permissible.

---

## Page 9175

As for a singular hadith, it is one known through only a single route of transmission. It may nevertheless be authentic, as with the hadith:

“Actions are only according to intentions”; 

the report of “his prohibition of selling or giving away the right of patronage”; and the hadith that “he entered Mecca wearing a mail coif.”

These are authentic hadiths recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, yet hadith scholars classify them as singular. The first is established only through Yahyā ibn Saʿīd al-Ansārī, from Muhammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Taymī, from ʿAlqamah ibn Waqqās al-Laythī, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb. The second is known only through the narration of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dīnār from Ibn ʿUmar. The third is known only through Mālik’s narration from al-Zuhrī, from Anas. Most singular hadiths, however, are weak.

As for a fair hadith in al-Tirmidhī’s terminology, it is one transmitted through two routes, none of whose narrators is accused of lying, and which does not anomalously contradict authentic hadiths.

These are the conditions al-Tirmidhī stipulated for a fair hadith. Some, however, object that he has described hadiths as fair even though they do not meet these conditions. For example, he may describe a hadith as “fair and singular”: it has been transmitted through only one route, yet he has called it fair. The response given is that it may be singular because it has been transmitted from only one Successor, while being transmitted from that Successor through two routes. It thus becomes fair because of the multiple routes from that individual, although it is singular at its origin.

Likewise, a hadith may be described as “authentic, fair, and singular” because it has been transmitted through an authentic, singular chain, and then transmitted from the original narrator through one authentic route and another route

---

## Page 9176

and thus becomes fair, even though it is authentic and singular. For a fair hadith is one transmitted through multiple routes, none of whose narrators is accused of lying. If it is authentic through both routes, it is authentic without qualification; but if the authenticity of one of the two routes is not known, it is fair. Its chain may be singular, in that it is known through that chain only by that particular route, while its text is fair because it has been transmitted through two routes.

This is why he says, “On this subject, there are also reports from so-and-so and so-and-so.” Its meaning thus has corroborating reports showing that its text is fair, even though its chain is singular.

If he also calls it authentic, it will have been established through an authentic route and transmitted through a fair route, thus combining authenticity and fairness. It may be singular through that particular route, being known through that chain only by that route. Even if it is authentic through that route, it may still be authentic and singular. There is no difficulty in this; the difficulty lies only in combining fairness and singularity. As explained above, it may be singular and fair, then become fair; it may also be fair and singular in the two senses mentioned.

As for mass-transmitted reports, the correct position, held by the majority, is that mass transmission requires no fixed number of transmitters. Rather, whenever the reports of those conveying them yield certainty, the report is mass-transmitted. The majority likewise hold that the certainty gained varies according to the circumstances of those conveying the report. A small number of people may convey a report that yields certainty because of factors establishing their truthfulness, whereas a report conveyed by many times their number may not yield certainty. Hence, the correct view is that a solitary report may yield certainty when accompanied by supporting indications that yield certainty.

---

## Page 9177

Accordingly, many of the texts in the two Sahīh collections are mass-transmitted in their wording among hadith scholars, even if others are unaware that they are mass-transmitted. Thus, most of the texts in these two collections are known by hadith scholars with absolute certainty to have been spoken by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—sometimes because they are mass-transmitted among them, and sometimes because the Muslim community has received them with acceptance.

A solitary report received with acceptance yields certainty according to the majority of scholars among the followers of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. This is also the position of most followers of al-Ashʿarī, such as al-Isfarāyīnī and Ibn Fūrak. For although such a report, considered on its own, yields only probability, when hadith scholars unanimously accept it as true, it becomes comparable to a consensus of jurists on a ruling based on the apparent meaning of a text, an analogy or a solitary report. According to the majority, that ruling then becomes certain, even though it would not be certain without the consensus. This is because consensus is protected from error: just as scholars of Islamic rulings do not unanimously declare something forbidden to be lawful, or something lawful to be forbidden, so too hadith scholars do not unanimously affirm a falsehood or deny a truth.

At other times, a scholar’s certainty arises from indications accompanying the reports that yield certainty. Whoever knows what these scholars know attains the same certainty as they do.

---

## Page 9178

Section

As for “the criteria of al-Bukhārī and Muslim,” each transmits from certain narrators whom the other does not use, while they also share other narrators. The hadiths included by both centre on these shared narrators.

One of them may transmit from a narrator through supporting chains and corroborating reports, without relying on him for the principal report. He may transmit from him reports already known to him through other narrators, but not those he alone transmits. He may also omit reports from a trustworthy narrator when he knows that the narrator erred in them. Someone without expertise may therefore suppose that the compilers of the Sahīh collections accept everything that such a narrator transmits as authoritative evidence, but that is not the case.

Recognizing hidden defects in hadith is a noble discipline understood by the leading authorities in the field, such as Yahyā ibn Saʿīd al-Qattān, ʿAlī ibn al-Madīnī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, al-Bukhārī—the compiler of the Sahīh—al-Dāraqutnī and others. These are branches of knowledge understood by their specialists. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 9179

He was asked:

What do some scholars mean when they say, “This hadith is weak,” or, “It is not sound”? If an issue has two transmitted opinions or two juristic views, is a person permitted to follow either on scholarly authority? Or how should one determine what to rely upon?

He replied:

A scholar may say, “It is not sound,” meaning that the evidence for this opinion is weak, even though some scholars have held it. A weak hadith is, for example, one transmitted by someone who is not trustworthy, either because of his poor memory or because he lacks moral uprightness.

When there are two opinions on an issue, either a person can discern that one is stronger, or else he follows scholars whom he trusts to explain which of the two opinions is stronger.

---

## Page 9180

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

A report may be known to be true, known to be false, or neither.

The first category:

Reports known to be true. This is generally established through accompanying evidence: either transmission by people whom reason cannot conceive of deliberately lying or colluding in falsehood, or other corroborating circumstances surrounding the report. This knowledge is of two kinds. One is immediate knowledge, requiring no effort on the mind’s part to acquire it, and … (1)

This category includes reports that the Muslim community has accepted and unanimously acted upon, or relied upon in practice. For had such a report been false, [they would not have acted upon it, since it is impossible for] (2) them to agree upon error. It is … (3)

It does not detract from this that the report does not, by itself, yield certain knowledge. This resembles a ruling established by consensus but based on analogy, independent juristic reasoning, and considered opinion. And … (4) the disputed report is, in itself, only probable; how, then, can it become certain? Yet he has not understood that probability and certainty are qualities of the investigator’s belief, according to the evidence apparent to him; the report itself has acquired no new attribute.

The second category:

Reports known to be false, or refuted by sound reason, the Book, or

> (2) Blank in the original.
> I, Usāmah ibn al-Zahrā’, note: The text in square brackets is taken from Harf Company’s electronic edition; I have included it for its usefulness.

---

## Page 9181

the Sunnah, consensus, or other grounds, according to the categories of those interpretations—and such cases are numerous—or by circumstantial evidence. In both categories, such evidence can be firmly established only by those versed in this discipline; others are ignorant of it.

The third category:

Reports that may be either true or false. These are divided into widely transmitted reports and others, with varying degrees of strength. A report transmitted by al-Siddīq and al-Fārūq does not carry the same weight as one transmitted by other, younger Companions or those whose companionship was brief.

Section:

An error in a report may arise from the narrator either deliberately or inadvertently. Narrators are therefore required to possess upright character, to guard against deliberate lying, and sound retention and alertness, to guard against inadvertent error.

Ease has several causes:

The first is being occupied with other pursuits rather than this discipline, so that one fails to master it accurately, as is the case with many ascetics and devoted worshippers.

The second is a lack of knowledge of this discipline.

---

## Page 9182

The third is narrating from memory, for not everyone can do this accurately.

The fourth is that someone inserts into his narrations material that does not belong to them and falsely attributes it to him.

The fifth is relying on students and consequently narrating what he supposes to be among his own reports.

The sixth is transmitting with an incomplete chain, for the omitted narrator may not be acceptable.

The seventh is narrating from a written text, because discrepancies may occur in it. For these and other reasons, a narrator is required to possess sound retention and accuracy, together with the qualities that safeguard against his conveying falsehood unwittingly. A narrator may have been free of lapses, yet subsequently lapse in another of his reports. Glory be to Him who neither errs nor forgets! Specialists in this discipline recognize such errors by comparing the narrations of his counterparts and contemporaries. An inattentive narrator’s report may nevertheless be accompanied by evidence establishing its accuracy, such as indications showing that he retained what he narrated and that not everything he transmitted was confused.

Deliberate lying has several causes:

The first is heresy and deviation in Allah’s religion:

“But Allah refuses to do anything but perfect His light, however much the disbelievers may detest it.”

The second is championing schools of thought and sectarian inclinations. This is common in fundamental matters, subsidiary matters, and those in between.

---

## Page 9183

The third is encouraging good and warning against evil, on the part of those who consider such fabrication permissible.

The fourth is pursuing worldly aims by amassing fleeting riches.

The fifth is seeking prominence through singular hadith reports.

Section

A narrator’s reports may be accepted either unconditionally or subject to qualifications. For unconditional acceptance, he must be reasonably considered trustworthy against lying. This requires upright character and freedom from motives and corrupt beliefs that might lead him to regard fabrication as permissible. He must also be safeguarded against inadvertent error by sound retention, accuracy, and mastery. Qualified acceptance, however, varies according to the accompanying evidence. Each hadith calls for its own critical discernment and a particular examination unlike that required for another.

Section

Many a hadith has an unbroken chain of transmission, yet contains additions or omissions. The addition of a single word may alter its meaning, as may the omission of another. Anyone experienced in this discipline will seldom fail to recognize where this occurs. In judging a hadith authentic or weak, there are gateways to enter, paths to follow, and routes to explore.

---

## Page 9184

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

As for the number of mass-transmitted hadiths in the two Sahīh collections, the term “mass-transmitted” is used in several senses. What is intended by mass transmission is that which yields certainty. Some, however, apply this term only to reports transmitted by so many narrators that certainty results from their number alone. They say that any number sufficient to yield certainty in one matter must likewise yield certainty in every matter. This is a weak view.

The correct view, held by the majority, is that certainty sometimes results from the large number of transmitters. It may also result from their qualities—their religious integrity and accuracy—or from corroborating circumstances surrounding the report, with these factors together yielding certainty. Certainty may also be attained by one group but not another.

Furthermore, a report that the leading scholars have accepted, either by affirming its truth or by acting upon its implications, yields certainty according to the overwhelming majority of earlier and later scholars. This falls within the meaning of mass transmission. Some, however, call it mashhūr (well-known) and mustafīd (widely circulated), and divide reports into mass-transmitted

---

## Page 9185

, well-known, and solitary reports. Accordingly, most of the texts in the two Sahīh collections are known with certainty and reliably preserved. Hadith scholars have accepted them, affirmed their truth, and unanimously agreed on their authenticity. Their consensus is protected from error, just as the jurists’ consensus on legal rulings is protected from error. If the jurists unanimously agree on a ruling, their consensus constitutes authoritative proof, even if an individual jurist bases his view on a solitary report, an analogy, or a text of general scope. Likewise, when hadith scholars unanimously agree on the authenticity of a report, it yields certainty. Although each of them individually may err, their consensus is protected from error.

Furthermore, these hadiths, whose authenticity they unanimously affirm, may be mass-transmitted and widely circulated among some scholars but not others. Some may attain certainty of their truth because they know the qualities of the transmitters and the corroborating circumstances accompanying the report that yield certainty. This is like someone hearing a report from al-Siddīq or al-Fārūq as he relates it among the Emigrants and the Helpers, who had witnessed what he had witnessed, affirm his truthfulness, and endorse his account.

His saying,

“Actions are judged only by intentions,”

is among the reports that scholars have accepted and affirmed as true. It was not mass-transmitted at its origin; rather, it is among the singular authentic reports. Yet, because they accepted it and affirmed its truth, its authenticity became established with certainty.

The Sunan collections also contain hadiths that scholars have accepted and affirmed as true, such as his saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“No bequest is to be made to an heir.”

This is among the reports that the Muslim community has accepted and acted upon. It is found in the Sunan collections, not in the Sahīh collections.

---

## Page 9186

As for the number of transmitters required for mass transmission, some have stipulated a fixed number, but they disagree over what it is. Some say more than four; others say twelve, forty, seventy, three hundred and thirteen, or some other number. All these views are invalid, for each is no more than an assertion.

The correct view, held by the majority, is that mass transmission has no fixed number. The certainty produced by a report arises directly in the heart, just as satiety follows eating and thirst is quenched by drinking. There is no fixed quantity that satisfies everyone’s hunger or quenches everyone’s thirst. Satiety may result from the quantity of food, from its quality—as with meat—or because the person eating needs only a little. It may also result from the mind’s preoccupation with joy, anger, sorrow, or something similar.

Likewise, certainty arising from a report may result from the large number of those conveying it. When they are numerous, their report may yield certainty even if they are unbelievers. At other times, it results from their religious integrity and accuracy. The reports of two or three men may yield a degree of certainty not attained through the reports of ten or twenty whose religious integrity and accuracy cannot be trusted.

At other times, certainty may arise because each reporter relates the same account as the other, while it is known that the two have not colluded and that such agreement cannot ordinarily occur by coincidence. An example is someone narrating a lengthy hadith containing several sections, which is also narrated by another person who has never met him.

At other times, certainty through a report is attained by someone endowed with discernment, intelligence, and knowledge of the reporters’ circumstances and of what they report

---

## Page 9187

but not by someone else with similar qualities. At other times, certainty arises from a report because it was related in the presence of a large group who shared the reporter’s knowledge, and none of them contradicted him. A large group may be unable to collude in concealing the truth, just as they may be unable to collude in lying. Once it is understood that certainty arising from reports has causes other than sheer numbers, it becomes clear that anyone who makes certainty depend on a fixed number and treats all reports alike in this respect has made a grave error.

For this reason, mass transmission is divided into two kinds: general and particular. Scholars of hadith and jurisprudence may possess mass-transmitted knowledge of matters of the Sunnah that have not reached the general public through mass transmission. Examples include the prostrations for forgetfulness, the binding right of pre-emption, the payment of blood money by the group collectively liable for it, and the stoning of an adulterer who has consummated a valid marriage; also the hadiths concerning seeing Allah, punishment in the grave, the Cistern, intercession, and similar matters.

Since a report may reach some people through mass transmission but not others, and some may attain certainty of its truth while others do not, whoever attains such certainty must believe it and act upon its implications, just as in comparable cases. Whoever does not attain such certainty must defer to the scholars who have unanimously agreed on its authenticity, just as people must defer, concerning rulings established by consensus, to the scholars who have agreed upon them. Allah has protected this community from agreeing upon error. Its consensus is realized only when the non-scholar defers to the scholar, for the non-scholar has no authoritative say; that belongs only to the scholar. Just as the opinion of someone unfamiliar with the evidence for rulings is not taken into account, neither is the opinion of someone unfamiliar with the means of establishing a hadith’s authenticity. Rather, everyone who is not a scholar must follow the consensus of the scholars.

---

## Page 9188

He also said: (*)

In refuting some leading scholars of speculative theology who criticized later scholars of hadith, disparaging their limited understanding, claiming that they neither understood the meanings of hadith nor distinguished sound reports from weak ones, and boasting of their own expertise and the subtlety of their learning, he—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

“There is no doubt that this is true of some of them. They cite fabricated hadiths as evidence in matters of religious fundamentals and subsidiary rulings, along with invented reports and unreliable accounts. They quote passages from the Quran and hadith whose meaning they do not understand. I have witnessed astonishing examples of this.

“Yet in this respect their position relative to others is like that of Muslims relative to the followers of other religions. Every evil found among some Muslims is more prevalent among others, and every good found among others is greater among Muslims. The same is true of the scholars of hadith in relation to others. While those scholars discuss hadiths whose meanings they do not understand, these theologians venture into speaking without knowledge to a far greater extent and with far graver consequences. How excellent are the words of Imam Ahmad: ‘A weak hadith is better than personal opinion.’ Shaykh Abū ‘Amr ibn al-Salāh ordered that a well-known school be taken away
