## Page 9189

from Abū al-Hasan al-Āmidī, saying: “Taking it from him is better than taking Acre.” Yet in al-Āmidī’s time no one was more deeply versed in the disciplines of speculative theology and philosophy than he, and he was among the best of them in adherence to Islam and the soundest in belief.

It is well known that subtle matters—whether true or false, faith or unbelief—can only be grasped through intelligence and discernment. They therefore regard anyone who does not share their learning as ignorant, even if his faith is better than theirs, whenever he displays a deficiency in intelligence and eloquence. They are as Allah Most High described:

“Indeed, those who committed crimes used to laugh at those who believed, and when they passed by them, they would exchange derisive glances.”

And so on through the subsequent verses.

Thus, when they unquestioningly accept from their false authorities that whatever is not attained through these methods of logical inference is not knowledge, many of them may fail to gain through those methods the faith required of them. Such a person then becomes an unbeliever and a heretic, a hypocrite and an ignoramus, misguided and misleading, utterly unjust and ungrateful. He becomes one of the foremost enemies of the messengers and hypocrites within the Muslim community, among those of whom Allah said:

“And thus We have made for every prophet an enemy from among the criminals.”

Some of them may possess both faith and hypocrisy and be apostates—either from the very foundation of the religion or from some of its ordinances, whether their apostasy takes the form of hypocrisy or unbelief. This is frequent and widespread, particularly in times and places where ignorance, unbelief, and hypocrisy prevail. These people exhibit such astonishing ignorance, injustice, lying, unbelief, hypocrisy, and misguidance that there is no room here to recount it all.

---

## Page 9190

When it concerns subtle doctrines, it may be said that a person is mistaken and misguided in them, but that the proof on the basis of which he would be deemed an unbeliever has not been established against him. Yet among some groups of them, this occurs even in manifest matters which learned and ordinary Muslims alike know to belong to their religion. Indeed, even Jews, Christians, and polytheists know that Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent with these teachings and that whoever opposes them is an unbeliever. These include his command to worship Allah alone, without partner, and his prohibition against worshipping anyone besides Allah, whether angels, prophets, or others—for this is the most evident distinguishing feature of Islam. They also include enmity towards Jews, Christians, and polytheists, and the prohibition of sexual immorality, usury, intoxicants, gambling, and similar things.

Yet you find that many of their leaders have fallen into these kinds of unbelief and thus become apostates, although they may repent of this and return to Islam. They resemble tribal leaders such as al-Aqraʿ, ʿUyayna, and others who apostatized from Islam and then re-entered it. Some of those tribal leaders were suspected of hypocrisy and sickness of the heart, while others were not. Many of these people’s leaders are likewise: at one time you find a man openly apostatizing from Islam, and at another returning to it, but with sickness and hypocrisy in his heart. He may also have a third state in which faith prevails over hypocrisy. Yet they rarely remain free of some form of hypocrisy, and accounts of this concerning them are well known.

Ibn Qutayba mentioned some of this at the beginning of Mukhtalif al-Hadīth (Conflicting Hadiths). Writers on doctrines have also related some such accounts about one another, as recounted by

---

## Page 9191

Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī, al-Qādī Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī, Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Shahrastānī, and others.

More extreme still, some of them compose works on the religion of the polytheists and apostasy from Islam. Thus, al-Rāzī wrote his book on worshipping celestial bodies, adducing proofs of its goodness and benefit and encouraging it. This constitutes apostasy from Islam by the consensus of the Muslims, although he may have subsequently returned to Islam. All the knowledge, deeds, and moral conduct they prescribe are insufficient to save anyone from Allah’s punishment, let alone lead to the bliss of the Hereafter. Allah Most High says:

“Who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against Allah or denies His signs? They will receive their allotted share of what is decreed”—this verse and the next.

And He Most High says:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed”—to the end of the surah.

Here He tells us something similar to what He relates in al-Aʿrāf: when those who turned away from what the messengers brought saw Allah’s punishment, they acknowledged Allah’s oneness and abandoned polytheism, but this did not benefit them. Likewise, He relates concerning Pharaoh—who disbelieved in Allah’s oneness and in the divine message—that when drowning overtook him:

“He said, ‘I believe that there is no god except the One in whom…’”—to the end of the verse.

And He Most High says:

“And when your Lord took from the children of Adam, from their loins…”—this verse and the next.

This appears in several passages of the Quran, explaining that the messengers were commanded to worship Allah alone, without partner, and forbidden to worship any created being besides Him, and that

---

## Page 9192

those destined for bliss are those who affirm Allah’s oneness, while the polytheists are those destined for misery. He also makes clear that those who do not believe in the messengers are polytheists. Thus, affirming Allah’s oneness and believing in the messengers are inseparable, as is belief in the Last Day: all three are inseparable. This is why they are mentioned together in such statements as:

“Do not follow the desires of those who deny Our signs, who do not believe in the Hereafter, and who set up equals to their Lord.”

In several passages, He tells us that the divine message has extended to all the children of Adam. These three foundations—affirming Allah’s oneness, believing in His messengers, and believing in the Last Day—are therefore inseparable. This is why He, glorified be He, says:

“Thus We have appointed for every prophet enemies—devils from humankind and jinn”—through to His words: “and so that they may commit whatever they commit.”

He tells us that all the prophets have enemies: devils from humankind and jinn who inspire one another with embellished speech—that is, speech made attractive and appealing—with which they deceive. Deception means disguising and misrepresenting the truth. Such is the nature of every statement and deed that opposes what the messengers brought, whether from speculative theologians or others, in earlier or later generations. Then He says:

“And so that the hearts of those who do not believe in the Hereafter may incline towards it.”

Thus, opposing the messengers and failing to believe in the Hereafter are inseparable. Whoever does not believe in the Hereafter lends an ear to the embellished speech of their enemies and consequently opposes the messengers. This can be seen among the various kinds of disbelievers and hypocrites within this community and elsewhere. This is why He Most High says:

“We have certainly brought them a Book which We have explained in detail with knowledge”—through to His words: “Are they waiting for anything but its fulfillment? On the Day

---

## Page 9193

its fulfillment comes, those who had forgotten it before will say, ‘The messengers of our Lord certainly came with the truth.’” He thus tells us that those who abandoned the Book—that is, the divine message—will say, when its fulfillment comes—that is, when what it foretold comes to pass—“The messengers of our Lord came with the truth.”

This is like His words, Most High:

“But whoever turns away from My remembrance will certainly have a life of hardship”—and the following verse.

He tells us that those who abandon following His signs will suffer what He has described. It is thus clear that the foundation of bliss and deliverance from punishment is affirming Allah’s oneness by worshipping Him alone, without partner, believing in His messengers and the Last Day, and doing righteous deeds. These things are absent from their philosophy. It contains neither the command to worship Allah alone, without partner, nor the prohibition against worshipping created beings. Rather, every instance of polytheism in the world arose through the opinions of people of their kind. They are the ones who enjoin polytheism and practise it. Those among them who do not enjoin it do not forbid it either; rather, they accept both groups. Even if one gives some preference to those who affirm Allah’s oneness, another may prefer the polytheists, or may turn away from both altogether. Reflect on this, for it is immensely beneficial.

I have seen writings of theirs on the worship of celestial bodies and angels, and the worship of disembodied souls—the souls of prophets and others—that constitute the very foundation of polytheism. When they claim to affirm Allah’s oneness, their affirmation is only in speech, not in worship and action. Yet affirming Allah’s oneness as taught by the messengers necessarily entails devoting religion sincerely to Allah and worshipping Him alone, without partner. This is something they do not know.

---

## Page 9194

Yet the affirmation of Allah’s oneness that they claim amounts only to denying the realities of His names and attributes. It contains unbelief and misguidance that are among the greatest causes of associating partners with Him.

Even if they affirmed His oneness in speech and discourse—that is, by describing Allah as His messengers described Him—they would possess this affirmation without putting it into practice. That is not sufficient for bliss and salvation. Rather, they must worship Allah alone and take Him alone as their God, to the exclusion of all others. This is the meaning of the statement, “None is worthy of worship but Allah.” How much less can their position suffice when, in their speech and discourse, they negate and deny rather than affirming His oneness and devoting worship sincerely to Him!

Since they entirely lack that through which bliss and deliverance from misery are attained, their teachings concerning morals, deeds, and governance fall under the words of the Most High:

“They know only what is apparent of the life of this world, but of the Hereafter they are heedless.”

Allah has appointed a measure for everything. Although these people possess intelligence and discernment, and some of them practise asceticism and display moral virtues, saying this does not establish that they will attain bliss and deliverance from punishment without the foundations already mentioned. Intellectual strength is comparable to physical strength and strength of will. Someone endowed with excellences of knowledge and will without these foundations is like someone endowed with bodily strength without them. People of learning and judgment are comparable to those who wield royal and political authority. None of this benefits anyone in either group at all unless he worships Allah alone, without partner, and believes in His messengers and the Last Day.

And since both those who wield power and those who possess knowledge may oppose the messengers,

---

## Page 9195

or may follow them, Allah has mentioned this in several places. He mentions Pharaoh; the man who disputed with Abraham when Allah had granted him kingship; and the chiefs among the people of Noah, ‘Ād, and others. He also mentions the statements of their learned men, as in His words:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed.”

And He says:

“None dispute concerning Allah’s signs except those who disbelieve,”

continuing to His words:

“They argued with falsehood to refute the truth thereby,”

and then to His words:

“Those who dispute concerning Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them—how hateful this is to Allah,”

to the end of the verse.

The authority referred to is revelation sent down from Allah. In this surah, “Hā Mīm, Ghāfir,” He recounts instructive examples of the circumstances and arguments of kings and learned men who opposed the messengers, as in His words:

“Those who dispute concerning Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them have nothing in their hearts but pride, though they will never attain the greatness they seek.”

And His words:

“Have you not seen those who dispute concerning Allah’s signs? How are they turned away?”

continuing to His words:

“That is because you used to rejoice on earth without right and because you used to exult.”

The same applies to Surahs al-An‘ām and al-A‘rāf, most of the Meccan surahs, and some of the Medinan surahs. They address these people, set forth analogies and examples for them, and recount their stories and those of the prophets and their followers in their dealings with them. Hence He—glorified be He—says:

“We had certainly established them in a position in which We have not established you, and We gave them hearing, sight, and hearts,”

to the end of the verse. He thus describes how they had been endowed with various kinds of

---

## Page 9196

perception and action. He states that none of this availed them when they denied Allah’s signs and the message. In this connection, the son of the jurist Shaykh al-Khudarī (*) related to me from his father, the leading Hanafī scholar of his time, that the jurists of Bukhara used to say of Ibn Sīnā:

“They were greater than them in strength and in the traces they left upon the earth,”

to the end of the verse. Strength encompasses both the theoretical faculty of cognition and the practical faculty of action. In another verse, He says:

“They were more numerous than them and greater in strength.”

He thus describes their superiority in quantity and quality, and their greater strength both in themselves and in the traces they left upon the earth. He—glorified be He—also says concerning the followers of these leaders among the rulers and learned men who opposed the messengers:

“On the Day their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say, ‘If only we had obeyed Allah and obeyed the Messenger!’”

continuing to His words:

“And curse them with a great curse.”

And He, Most High, says:

“When they dispute in the Fire, the weak will say to those who were arrogant, ‘We were your followers, so can you relieve us of any portion of the Fire?’”

There are many such passages in the Quran, recounting the words and deeds of the messengers’ enemies and the faculties of perception and action they had been granted, which did not benefit them when they opposed the messengers.

Allah—glorified be He—has also described the hypocrisy and misguidance found among scholars, worshippers, and rulers who profess to follow the messengers, as in His words:

“O you who believe, indeed many of the rabbis and monks consume people’s wealth unjustly,”

to the end of the verse. And the verb yasuddūna (turn away) is used intransitively; one says: sadda, sudūdan

## Page 9197

meaning “to turn away,” as in His words:

“You see the hypocrites turning away from you in aversion.”

One also says sadda ghayrahu, yasudduhu, meaning “to turn someone else away.” Both descriptions apply to them. Another example is His saying, Most High:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture, who believe in superstition and false objects of worship,”

to the end of the verse. In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Mūsā reports that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The believer who recites the Quran is like a citron: its taste is pleasant and its fragrance is pleasant. The believer who does not recite the Quran is like a date: its taste is pleasant, but it has no fragrance. The hypocrite who recites the Quran is like basil: its fragrance is pleasant, but its taste is bitter. The hypocrite who does not recite the Quran is like a colocynth: its taste is bitter, and it has no fragrance.”

He thus explained that those who recite the Quran include both believers and hypocrites. Since the felicity of earlier and later generations lies in following the messengers, it is clear that those most entitled to it are those who best know the reports handed down from the messengers and follow them most faithfully. Those who know their words and deeds and follow them are the people of felicity in every time and place. They are the saved group within every religious community, and within this nation they are the people of the Sunnah and hadith. The messengers were charged with conveying the message clearly, and they did convey it clearly.

To the last of the messengers—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—[Allah] (1) sent down a Book confirming the Scriptures that preceded it and serving as a guardian over them. It is thus the trustworthy custodian of all the Scriptures. And he has

## Page 9198

conveyed the message in the clearest, fullest, and most complete manner. He was the most sincere of all creation in his concern for Allah’s servants, and was compassionate and merciful toward the believers. He conveyed the message, fulfilled the trust, strove for Allah as He deserves, and worshipped Allah until death came to him. Thus, those who enjoy the greatest felicity, the greatest bliss, and the highest rank are those who follow him most closely and conform most fully to his guidance in knowledge and deed. ## Page 9199

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section: Baseless hadiths that some jurists cite as evidence for certain positions

Among these is their claim that he “forbade a sale coupled with a stipulation.” This is a baseless hadith, found in none of the Muslims’ books; it is related only in an account with a broken chain of transmission.

Another is their claim that he “forbade the miller’s measure.” This too is baseless.

Another is the hadith concerning the muhallil (a third entrant intended to make wagering lawful), involving the entry of a horse between two horses. This is known to be a statement of Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib himself. That is how al-Zuhrī’s reliable students transmitted it: from al-Zuhrī, from Sa‘īd. Sufyān ibn Husayn erred by narrating it from al-Zuhrī, from Sa‘īd, from Abū Hurayrah, attributing it to the Prophet. Hadith scholars know that these are not the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Abū Dāwūd al-Sijistānī and other scholars have noted this.

---

## Page 9200

And they agree that this Sufyān ibn Husayn makes errors in what he narrates from al-Zuhrī, and that reports he alone transmits cannot be relied upon as evidence. The use of a muhallil in racing has no basis in Islamic law, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not instruct his community to use one. It is reported that Abū ‘Ubaydah ibn al-Jarrāh and others used to race for stakes without introducing a muhallil among them.

Those jurists who advocated this thought that it would otherwise constitute gambling. Some of them then maintained that introducing a muhallil removes its resemblance to gambling. Yet matters are not as they claim. Rather, introducing a muhallil involves … (1) risk-taking. Moreover, the arrangement is unjust: if the muhallil wins, he takes the prize, but if he loses, he pays nothing, whereas another contestant is given the prize if he wins. Introducing a muhallil is thus an injustice that Islamic law would not sanction.

This matter is discussed at length elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (pp. 151–152):
> “The wording in al-Kubrā, 1/495, is: ‘… rather, introducing a muhallil (a non-staking entrant) [leads to] risk-taking …’ This, or something to the same effect, is probably the correct reading. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 9201

Shaykh al-Islām, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

Section

Ahmad ibn Hanbal’s statement:

“When reports concern what is lawful and unlawful, we apply strict standards to their chains of transmission; when they concern encouragement and warning, we are more lenient with their chains of transmission.”

This statement, like the scholars’ practice of acting upon weak hadiths concerning the virtues of deeds, does not mean establishing that an act is recommended on the basis of a hadith that cannot serve as evidence. Being recommended is a ruling of Islamic law and can therefore be established only through evidence recognized by that law. Whoever claims that Allah loves a particular deed without such evidence has prescribed in religion something Allah has not authorized, just as if he had declared something obligatory or forbidden. This is why scholars differ over whether acts are recommended, just as they differ over other rulings. Indeed, this is fundamental to the divinely prescribed religion.

What they mean is that the deed must already have been established, through a scriptural text or scholarly consensus, as something Allah loves or dislikes. Examples include reciting the Quran, glorifying Allah, supplication, charity, freeing slaves, kindness to people, and the reprehensibility of lying, betrayal, and the like. Thus, if a hadith is narrated concerning the virtue of certain deeds

---

## Page 9202

that are recommended and their rewards, and the reprehensibility of certain deeds and their punishment, then, regarding the amounts and kinds of reward and punishment, if a hadith is narrated which we do not know to be fabricated, it is permissible to narrate it and act upon it. This means that a person hopes for that reward or fears that punishment. It is like someone who knows that trade brings profit, but then hears that it brings great profit: if this report is true, it benefits him; if false, it does him no harm.

Examples include encouragement and warning through reports from the Children of Israel, dreams, sayings of the early generations and scholars, incidents in the lives of scholars, and similar material. None of these can, by itself, establish a ruling of Islamic law, whether that something is recommended or otherwise. They may, however, be mentioned for encouragement and warning, to inspire hope and instill fear. Where something is already known to be good or bad through scriptural evidence, such material benefits and does no harm, whether it is actually true or false. But anything known to be false and fabricated must not be given any consideration, for lies are of no benefit.

If a report is established as authentic, legal rulings are established on its basis. If it could be either true or false, it may be narrated because it could be true and because its being false causes no harm. Ahmad merely said:

“When reports concern encouragement and warning, we are more lenient with their chains of transmission.”

This means that we narrate such reports with their chains of transmission even when their narrators are not among the trustworthy narrators whose reports are accepted as evidence.

The same applies to the statement of those who say, “They may be acted upon concerning the virtues of deeds.” Acting upon them means only putting into practice what they contain of

---

## Page 9203

righteous deeds, such as recitation and remembrance of Allah, and avoiding the evil deeds they describe as reprehensible.

A parallel to this is the Prophet’s statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) in the hadith narrated by al-Bukhārī from ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr:

“Convey from me, even if only a single verse. Relate reports from the Children of Israel; there is no harm in doing so. Whoever deliberately lies about me, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

This is to be considered alongside his statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) in the authentic hadith:

“When the People of the Book tell you something, neither affirm nor deny what they say.”

He permitted relating reports from them, yet prohibited affirming or denying what they say. Had there been no benefit in simply relating their reports, he would neither have permitted nor instructed it. And had it been permissible to affirm what they say merely because they reported it, he would not have prohibited affirming it. Thus, in certain circumstances, people benefit from what they think may be true.

If, however, weak hadiths concerning the virtues of deeds prescribe specific measures and particulars—such as a prayer at a specified time, with a specified recitation, or in a specified manner—acting upon those specifications is not permissible, because no scriptural evidence has established that this particular form is recommended.

This differs from a report stating that whoever enters the marketplace and says, “There is no god but Allah,” will receive such-and-such a reward. Remembering Allah in the marketplace is recommended, since it entails remembering Him among the heedless, as stated in the well-known hadith:

“One who remembers Allah among the heedless is like a green tree among dry trees.”

---

## Page 9204

As for the amount of reward mentioned in the report, it makes no difference here whether or not it is established. Concerning such cases, al-Tirmidhī narrated the following hadith:

“Whoever hears something attributed to Allah that promises a reward and acts upon it in hope of that reward, Allah will grant him that reward, even if the matter is not as reported.”

In summary, reports of this kind may be narrated and acted upon to encourage good and deter evil, but not to establish that an act is recommended. Moreover, believing what they entail—namely, the specific measures of reward and punishment—requires scriptural evidence.

---

## Page 9205

He was also asked about a group of people who had come together in various forms of wrongdoing, some of whom said: “Not a single hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has been established through mass transmission, since mass transmission means transmission by a vast multitude from another vast multitude.”

He replied:

As for anyone who denies that even a single hadith is established through mass transmission, he should be told: Mass transmission is of two kinds: transmission through the general public, and transmission through specialists, namely, scholars of hadith.

It is also divided into two categories: mass transmission of wording and mass transmission of meaning. The hadiths concerning intercession, the Bridge, the Balance, seeing Allah, the virtues of the Companions, and similar matters are established through mass transmission among scholars. Their meaning is mass-transmitted, even if no particular wording is mass-transmitted. Likewise, the miracles of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, other than the Quran are also established through mass transmission. Prostration for forgetfulness is likewise established through mass transmission among scholars, as are judicial rulings on the right of pre-emption and similar matters.

Hadith scholars receive through mass transmission reports that do not reach others through mass transmission, because they

---

## Page 9206

have heard what others have not heard and know aspects of the Prophet’s life, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that others do not know. Mass transmission does not require a fixed number of transmitters. Some scholars, however, have claimed that there is a particular number that yields certain knowledge, regardless of what is reported or who reports it. They denied that four were sufficient and withheld judgment concerning numbers greater than four. This is mistaken: certainty sometimes arises from the number of transmitters, sometimes from their qualities, sometimes from corroborating circumstances accompanying their reports, and sometimes from other factors.

Furthermore, a report narrated by one or two Companions yields certain knowledge, according to the great majority of scholars, if the Muslim community has accepted it and affirmed its truth. Some call this mustafīd (widely transmitted). Certainty here arises from the scholars’ consensus on its authenticity, for consensus cannot be upon error.

For this reason, most of the hadith texts in the two Sahīh collections are known to be authentic among the scholars of the various schools: the Hanafīs, Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, Hanbalīs, and Ashʿarīs. Only a group of speculative theologians disagreed on this matter, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 9207

Shaykh al-Islām was asked about a man who attended readings of books of hadith and Quranic commentary, but would not listen when *al-Hilya* was read to him. He was asked, “Why do you not listen to accounts of the early Muslims?”

He replied, “I will not listen to anything from Abū Nuʿaym’s book.”

He was told, “He is a leading scholar, a trustworthy transmitter, and the foremost hadith scholar of his time. Why, then, will you neither listen nor trust his transmission?”

He was then asked, “Shall the outstanding scholar of our age, Shaykh al-Islām Ibn Taymiyya, judge between us concerning Abū Nuʿaym’s standing?”

He replied, “I will listen to what Shaykh al-Islām says and defer to him.” He therefore sent this question from Damascus.

The shaykh answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Abū Nuʿaym Ahmad ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Asbahānī, the author of *The Adornment of the Saints*, *The History of Isfahan*, *al-Mustakhraj on al-Bukhārī and Muslim*, *The Book of Medicine*, *The Deeds of the Day and Night*, *The Virtues of the Companions*, *The Proofs of Prophethood*, *The Description of Paradise*, *The Path of the Confident*, and other works, is among the greatest masters of hadith and among its most prolific authors. People have benefited from his writings. He is too eminent to be described merely as a trustworthy transmitter, for his rank is higher than that. His book *al-Hilya* is among the finest

---

## Page 9208

books devoted to accounts of ascetics. Its reports are more reliably transmitted than those in al-Qushayrī’s Treatise, the writings of his teacher Abū ʿAbd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī, Ibn Khamīs’s The Virtues of the Righteous, and other such works. Abū Nuʿaym possessed greater knowledge of hadith, a larger body of narrations, and greater reliability in transmission than these authors. However, Imam Ahmad’s Book of Asceticism, Ibn al-Mubārak’s Book of Asceticism, and similar works contain more reliably transmitted material than al-Hilya.

These books, and others, inevitably contain weak hadiths and weak—even false—accounts. Al-Hilya contains some such material, but the other books mentioned contain more. The writings of Abū ʿAbd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī, al-Qushayrī’s Treatise, The Virtues of the Righteous, and similar works contain false accounts—and indeed false hadiths—the like of which is not found in Abū Nuʿaym’s writings.

However, the transmitted material in Abū al-Faraj Ibn al-Jawzī’s Safwat al-Safwa is comparable to that in al-Hilya. The material in both books is predominantly sound; nevertheless, both contain false hadiths and accounts. As for Imam Ahmad’s Book of Asceticism and similar works, they do not contain fabricated hadiths and accounts to the extent found in these other books. He does not transmit in his writings from anyone known to fabricate reports. Rather, they may contain material that is weak because of a transmitter’s poor memory.

Likewise, among the hadiths traced back to the Prophet, none is known to be a deliberate fabrication, just as none of this kind is found in his Musnad. It does, however, contain reports known to be mistaken, in which their transmitters erred. Such errors occur in most Islamic books: no book is free from error except the Quran.

---

## Page 9209

The foremost work in authenticity is al-Bukhārī’s book. It contains no hadith text known to have been reported incorrectly from a Companion, although some errors occur in the wording of hadiths. Within his Sahīh itself, al-Bukhārī has included material that makes the transmitter’s error clear, as when he explains the transmitters’ differing accounts of the price of Jābir’s camel. It also contains reports from some Companions that are said to be mistaken. For example, it reports from Ibn ‘Abbās:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, married Maymūna while in a state of pilgrimage consecration.”

The widely accepted account among most people is that he married her while not in that state. It also reports from Usāma:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not pray inside the Kaaba.”

Yet it reports from Bilāl that he did pray inside it, and scholars regard this latter account as more authentic.

As for Muslim’s collection, it contains wordings known to be erroneous, such as:

“Allah created the soil on Saturday.”

Al-Bukhārī explained that this is an error and that these are actually the words of Ka‘b. It also reports that:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the eclipse prayer with three bowings in each unit of prayer.”

The correct account is that he performed the eclipse prayer only once. It also reports that Abū Sufyān asked him to marry Umm Habība, and this is an error.

This is among the most distinguished branches of hadith scholarship, known as the study of defects in hadith.

As for Hilyat al-Awliyā’, it is among the finest works by later authors on accounts of ascetics. It contains some anecdotes that it had no need to include, and many of the hadiths transmitted in its opening sections are weak—indeed, fabricated.

---

## Page 9210

He was asked:

About someone who copies the Sahīh collections of al-Bukhārī and Muslim and the Quran by hand, intending to transcribe hadith and other material: is he rewarded if he makes copies for himself or for sale? And so on.

He replied:

As for the well-known hadith collections, such as those of al-Bukhārī and Muslim, there are no books under heaven, after the Quran, more authentic than these two and compilations combining them, such as al-Jam‘ bayna al-Sahīhayn by al-Humaydī and the corresponding work by ‘Abd al-Haqq al-Ishbīlī. Next come the Sunan collections, such as the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī, and the Jāmi‘ of al-Tirmidhī; and the Musnad collections, such as the Musnad of al-Shāfi‘ī and the Musnad of Imam Ahmad.

Mālik’s Muwatta’ contains hadiths, reports from the early generations, and other material. It is among the most distinguished books, to the extent that al-Shāfi‘ī said:

“Under heaven, after the Book of Allah, there is no book more authentic than Mālik’s Muwatta’.”

He meant works compiled according to its method. Earlier scholars used to bring together, within each chapter, reports transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the Companions, and the Successors. Works based on juristic opinion, known as “books of

---

## Page 9211

jurisprudence,” had not yet been compiled. Later, hadiths were compiled with their chains of transmission in the Sahīh collections of al-Bukhārī and Muslim. Such books are to be cherished, and a person is rewarded for copying them, whether for personal use or for sale. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah admits three people to Paradise through a single arrow: its maker, the one who shoots it, and the one who supplies it.”

The same applies to copying books: whether one does so for one’s own benefit or to benefit others, one is rewarded in either case.

---

## Page 9212

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

My Lord, grant me help.

Al-Zayn Abū Muhammad Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Imād Abū Bakr ibn Zurayq al-Hanbalī informed us in letters he sent me on more than one occasion: Abū al-Abbās Ahmad ibn Abī Bakr ibn Ahmad ibn Abd al-Hamīd al-Maqdisī informed us, as heard directly from him on Saturday, 24 Safar 797 AH.

(ح)

The three shaykhs—Abū Ishāq al-Harmalī, Abū Muhammad al-Baqarī, and Abū al-Abbās al-Raslānī—also wrote to me, saying: The hadith master Abū Abd Allāh Muhammad ibn Ahmad ibn Uthmān al-Dhahabī informed us by unrestricted authorization to transmit.

Both said: We were informed by the shaykh and imam, the learned, erudite, exceptionally accomplished and peerless scholar, the exemplary hadith master Abū al-Abbās Ahmad ibn Abd al-Halīm ibn Abd al-Salām Ibn Taymiyyah. Al-Dhahabī specified: through my reading aloud to him in Jumādā al-Ākhirah 721 AH.

He said:

“All praise belongs to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help and guidance, ask His forgiveness, and seek refuge in Allah from

---

## Page 9213

the evils of our own souls and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped but Allah alone, without partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. He sent him with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all religions, even though the polytheists dislike it. May Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon Muhammad, his family, and his Companions.

The First Hadith

The imam Zayn al-Dīn Abū al-Abbās Ahmad ibn Abd al-Dā’im ibn Niʿmah ibn Ahmad al-Maqdisī informed us, through a reading aloud to him while I listened in 667 AH: Abū al-Faraj Abd al-Munʿim ibn Abd al-Wahhāb ibn Saʿd ibn Kulayb informed us, through a reading aloud to him: Abū al-Qāsim Alī ibn Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Bayān al-Razzāz informed us, through a reading aloud to him: Abū al-Hasan Muhammad ibn Muhammad ibn Muhammad ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Makhlad al-Bazzāz informed us: Abū Alī Ismāʿīl ibn Muhammad ibn Ismāʿīl al-Saffār informed us: Al-Hasan ibn Arafah ibn Yazīd al-Abdī narrated to us: Abū Bakr ibn Ayyāsh narrated to me, from Abū Ishāq al-Sabīʿī, from al-Barā’ ibn Āzib, who said:

‘The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, set out with his Companions, and we entered ihram for Hajj.

---

## Page 9214

When we reached Mecca, he said, “Turn your Hajj into an umrah.” The people said, “O Messenger of Allah, we have entered ihram for Hajj. How can we turn it into an umrah?”

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Consider what I am commanding you to do, and do it.” They repeated their objection to him, and he became angry. He then went to Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, still angry. Seeing the anger on his face, she said, “Who has angered you? May Allah be angry with him!” He replied, “Why should I not be angry when I give a command and am not obeyed?”’

Al-Nasā’ī and Ibn Mājah reported it through Abū Bakr ibn Ayyāsh.

He was born in Safar 575 AH and died on Monday, 8 Rajab 668 AH.

The Second Hadith

The shaykh and hadith transmitter Kamāl al-Dīn Abū Nasr Abd al-Azīz ibn Abd al-Mun‘im ibn al-Khadir ibn Shibl ibn Abd al-Hārithī informed us, through a reading aloud to him while I listened, on Friday, 6 Sha‘bān 669 AH, at the congregational mosque of Damascus: The hadith master Abū Muhammad al-Qāsim, son of the hadith master Abū al-Qāsim Alī ibn al-Hasan ibn Hibat Allah ibn Asākir, informed us,

---

## Page 9215

through a reading aloud to him in Rabī‘ al-Ākhir 596 AH: Abū al-Fadā’il Nāsir ibn Mahmūd ibn Alī al-Qudsī al-Sā’igh and Abū al-Qāsim Nasr ibn Ahmad ibn Muqātil al-Sūsī informed us through a reading aloud to them. They both said:

Abū al-Hasan Alī ibn Ahmad ibn Zuhayr al-Mālikī informed us: Abū al-Hasan Alī ibn Muhammad ibn Shujā‘ al-Rab‘ī al-Mālikī narrated to us: Abū Bakr Muhammad ibn Abd al-Rahmān ibn Ubayd Allāh al-Qattān informed us: Khaythamah narrated to us: al-Abbās ibn al-Walīd narrated to us: Uqbah ibn Alqamah narrated to us: Sa‘īd ibn Abd al-Azīz narrated to us, from Atiyyah ibn Qays, from Abd Allāh ibn Amr, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I saw the pillar of the Book taken away from beneath my pillow. I looked, and there it was—a radiant light, borne toward Greater Syria. Indeed, when tribulations arise, faith will be in Greater Syria.”

He was born in 589 AH and died in Sha‘bān 672 AH.

The Third Hadith

The imam Taqī al-Dīn Abū Muhammad Ismā‘īl ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Abī al-Yusr al-Tanūkhī informed us, through a reading aloud to him while I listened, in 669 AH: Abū Tāhir Barakāt ibn Ibrāhīm al-Khushū‘ī informed us through a reading aloud to him: Abū Muhammad Abd al-Karīm ibn Hamzah ibn

## Page 9216

al-Khidr al-Sulamī informed us: Abū al-Husayn Tāhir ibn Ahmad ibn Alī ibn Mahmūd al-Mahmūdī al-Ānī informed us: Abū al-Fadl Mansūr ibn Nasr ibn Abd al-Rahīm ibn Bint al-Kāghidī informed us: Abū Amr al-Hasan ibn Alī ibn al-Hasan al-Attār narrated to us: Ibrāhīm ibn Abd Allāh ibn Umar ibn Bukayr ibn al-Hārith al-Qaysī narrated to us: Wakī‘ ibn al-Jarrāh ibn Mulayh al-Ru’āsī narrated to us, from al-A‘mash, from Abū Sālih, from Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, who said:

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Noah will be summoned on the Day of Resurrection and asked, ‘Did you convey the message?’ He will reply, ‘Yes.’ His people will then be summoned and asked, ‘Did he convey the message to you?’ They will reply, ‘No warner came to us; no one at all came to us.’ Noah will then be asked, ‘Who will testify for you?’ He will reply, ‘Muhammad and his nation.’”

This is His saying:

“And thus We have made you a justly balanced nation.”

He said: “‘Middle’ means ‘just.’”

He was born in 589 AH and died in Safar 672 AH.

The sixth hadith:

## Page 9220

The seventh hadith:

The upright shaykh Rashīd al-Dīn Abū Abd Allāh Muhammad ibn Abī Bakr Muhammad ibn Muhammad ibn Sulaymān al-Āmirī informed us through a reading to him which I heard in 669 AH, as did those previously mentioned, through their chain of transmission to al-Ansārī, who said: Al-Taymī narrated to me: Anas ibn Mālik narrated to us, saying:

“Two men sneezed in the presence of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He prayed for mercy—or prayed for mercy—for one of them but not the other—or he prayed for mercy for him but not the other.

“He was asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, two men sneezed in your presence, and you prayed for mercy for one of them but not the other—or you prayed for mercy for him but not the other.’

“He replied, ‘This one praised Allah, so I prayed for mercy for him; this one did not praise Allah, so I did not pray for mercy for him.’”

Al-Bukhārī narrated it from Muhammad ibn Kathīr, from Sufyān al-Thawrī. Muslim narrated it from Muhammad ibn Abd Allāh ibn Numayr, from Hafs ibn Ghiyāth. Both narrated it from al-Taymī. He died in Dhū al-Hijjah 682 AH.
