Indeed, in relation to the verse, this is no different from his benefiting from supplication, prayers for forgiveness, and intercession.

Elsewhere, we have presented some thirty proofs from revelation showing that a person benefits from another’s efforts. The verse merely denies that he is entitled to, or owns, another’s efforts. Yet the fact that a person neither owns something nor is entitled to it does not mean that its rightful owner cannot show him kindness by allowing him to benefit from it. These are two distinct matters. Likewise, the fact that a person does not own something does not mean that he cannot derive any benefit from it. Such a claim is false in both religious and worldly matters.

These texts that deny injustice affirm justice in recompense: no one who acts will be deprived of the due reward for his deeds. Likewise, He says concerning those whom He punished:

“We did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves. Their gods, whom they invoked besides Allah, were of no benefit to them whatsoever.”

And He says:

“We did not wrong them, but it was they who were the wrongdoers.”

He makes clear that punishing the evildoers was just because of their sins, not that He wronged them by punishing them for no sin.

The hadith recorded in the Sunan collections states:

“If Allah were to punish the inhabitants of His heavens and His earth, He would punish them without wronging them. And if He were to show them mercy, His mercy to them would be better than their deeds.”

This makes clear that, were such punishment to occur, it would be because they deserved it, not that it would be inflicted without sin. This shows that included among

---

## Page 9280

the forms of injustice He denies is punishing someone who has committed no sin. Likewise, His words, Exalted is He:

“And the man who believed said, ‘O my people, I fear for you a day like that of the earlier nations—a fate like that of the people of Noah, 'Ād, Thamūd, and those after them. And Allah wills no injustice for His servants.’”

This makes clear that this punishment was not unjust, because they deserved it, and that Allah does not will injustice. One cannot properly be praised for not willing something that cannot possibly lie within one’s power. Refraining from an action is praiseworthy only when the one praised is capable of it. Thus, we know that Allah is capable of the injustice from which He has declared Himself free, but that He does not commit it. It is in this sense that His saying holds true:

“I have forbidden injustice to Myself.”

Prohibition means restraint, and this cannot apply to something intrinsically impossible. It would therefore be inappropriate to say, “I have forbidden Myself, or restrained Myself, from creating another like Me, or from making created beings creators,” or similar impossibilities.

The most that can be offered as an interpretation of this amounts to: “I have stated of Myself that what cannot lie within My power cannot proceed from Me.” The believer knows with certainty that this is not what the Lord intends. Allah and His Messenger must be held above intending such a meaning, which is unworthy of being conveyed in speech. Besides being virtually a repetition and a statement of the obvious, it contains neither praise nor commendation, nor anything from which the listener can benefit. Thus, we know that what He has forbidden Himself is something within His power, but He does not do it because He has forbidden it to Himself. Glorified is He, exalted and holy above committing it.

---

## Page 9281

This is made clear by the fact that the definitions people have given of injustice encompass this, rather than what is intrinsically impossible. For example, some say, “Injustice is putting something where it does not belong,” as in the saying, “Whoever resembles his father has done no wrong”—that is, he has not misplaced the resemblance. It is well known that Allah, Glorified is He, is a just Judge who puts things only where they belong. Putting them elsewhere is not intrinsically impossible; rather, it is possible, but He does not do it because He does not will it. Indeed, He dislikes and hates it, having forbidden it to Himself.

The same applies to those who say, “Injustice is inflicting undeserved harm,” for Allah does not punish anyone unjustly. Likewise, some say, “It is diminishing what is due,” explaining that the word’s basic meaning is diminution, as in His words:

“Each of the two gardens yielded its produce and did not fall short in anything.”

As for the definition, “It is exercising control over another’s property,” this is neither exclusive nor exhaustive. A person may rightfully exercise control over another’s property without being unjust, and may wrongfully exercise control over his own property and thereby be unjust. The Quran frequently speaks of a servant wronging himself.

The same applies to those who define it as “a person’s acting contrary to what he has been commanded to do,” and similar formulations. Even if the validity of such a formulation is conceded, Allah, Glorified is He, has prescribed mercy for Himself and forbidden injustice to Himself. Thus, He does not act contrary to what He has prescribed, nor does He do what He has forbidden. This response is not the place to elaborate on these matters to which we have drawn attention here.

---

## Page 9282

and we merely indicate the key points. This makes clear the middle position, namely:

The injustice that Allah has forbidden to Himself includes disregarding the good deeds of the righteous and not rewarding them for those deeds; punishing an innocent person for evil deeds he has not committed; punishing someone for another’s sin; judging between people without equity; and similar acts from which the Lord is exalted above because of His equity and justice, although He has the power to do them. He deserves praise and commendation precisely because He refrains from this injustice despite having the power to commit it. Just as Allah is exalted above attributes of deficiency and imperfection, He is also exalted above acts of deficiency and imperfection.

According to the second group’s position, however, there is no act whatsoever from which Allah must be declared free. The Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars indicate otherwise. Yet when the dialectical theologians of affirmation debated the theologians of negation, they pressed them with implications from which they could extricate themselves only by countering falsehood with falsehood. This is among the things the leading scholars criticized and condemned. Thus al-Awzāʿī, al-Zubaydī, al-Thawrī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others criticized countering the Qadariyyah with excessive affirmation, and enjoined adherence to the Quran and the Sunnah. They likewise criticized those who countered the Jahmiyyah, who denied the divine attributes, with such excessive affirmation that they came to liken the Creator to His creation. We have discussed both matters at length elsewhere and cited the statements of the early generations and the leading scholars concerning them.

If someone were to say:

This is based on “the question of rational judgments of good and evil.” Whoever holds that the goodness and evil of actions can be known through reason considers the Lord above certain

---

## Page 9283

actions, whereas whoever holds that this can be known only through revelation allows all actions for Him, since no prohibition applies to Him.

The answer is: This issue does not necessarily depend on that one. Even supposing that it does, the latter requires distinctions and careful examination, which we have set out at length elsewhere. Suppose that we know through reason the goodness and evil of certain actions. Reason, however, does not regard the Creator as like the creature, such that whatever it judges good or evil for one it also judges good or evil for the other—as the Qadariyyah do—for there are many differences between the Lord and His servant. If, on the other hand, we suppose that the goodness and evil of actions can be known only through revelation, revelation has indicated that Allah has declared Himself free of certain actions and judgments—and thus it is not permissible for Him to do them—sometimes by declaring, in praise of Himself, that He does not do them, and sometimes by declaring that He has forbidden them to Himself. This clarifies the second issue.

We therefore say: Regarding Allah’s actions—what is fitting and permissible for Him and what is not—people hold three positions: two extremes and a middle position.

One extreme is that of the Qadariyyah, who restricted Him to doing only what they judged by their reason to be permissible for Him. They went so far as to lay down for Him a code defining what is just and permissible, declaring through their reason many things obligatory for Him and many things forbidden to Him. This does not mean that reason commands and forbids Him, for no rational person would say that. Rather, it means that those actions are among the things that

---

## Page 9284

reason establishes as obligatory or forbidden. Yet, [among](1) the reprehensible notions they included in this were those founded on their innovation of denying divine decree and its implications.

The other extreme is that of those who went too far in refuting them. They said that no action is to be ruled out as unworthy of the Lord, and that the only basis on which we know that He cannot perform an action is His declaration that He will not perform it, which accords with His knowledge that He will not perform it.

These people denied the reality of what He has declared: that He has prescribed mercy for Himself and forbidden injustice to Himself. Allah Most High says:

“When those who believe in Our revelations come to you, say, ‘Peace be upon you. Your Lord has prescribed mercy for Himself.’”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When Allah completed creation, He wrote a decree for Himself, which is kept with Him above the Throne: ‘My mercy prevails over My wrath.’”

These people did not realize that a mere declaration corresponding to knowledge does not explain why He acts or refrains from acting, for knowledge corresponds to what is known. His knowledge that He will do one thing and will not do another does not address His having prescribed the former for Himself and forbidden the latter to Himself. Likewise, if He were to declare of any being, whoever it might be, that it does one thing and does not do another, this would not explain its being praiseworthy and deserving of commendation for doing the former and refraining from the latter. Nor would it explain the presence of a reason prompting the former or a restraint preventing the latter. A mere declaration discloses what is being reported; it does not explain what prompts action or abstention. This is unlike His statement:

“He has prescribed

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 9285

mercy for Himself,” and “He has forbidden injustice to Himself.” For prohibition restrains an action, while His prescribing something for Himself provides a reason to perform it. This is plain and clear.

What is meant here is not merely His recording that He will act—that is, recording what has been decreed—as is established in an authentic hadith:

“He decreed the destinies of all creatures fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water.”

For He said, “He has prescribed mercy for Himself.” If this meant recording what has been decreed, He would have prescribed wrath for Himself just as He prescribed mercy for Himself, since the meaning would merely be a declaration of what will happen. Likewise, He would have forbidden Himself every act of kindness He does not perform, just as He forbade Himself injustice.

Just as, in our case, there is an established distinction between His statement [exalted is He](1), “Just retribution for those murdered is prescribed for you,” and His statements, “Everything they have done is in the records,” and “No calamity befalls the earth or yourselves without being recorded in a Book before We bring it into being,” and the Prophet’s statement [may Allah bless him and grant him peace](2):

“Then the angel is sent to him and instructed to record four things. He is told: ‘Write down his provision, his lifespan, his deeds, and whether he will be wretched or blessed.’”

So too is this distinction established with regard to Allah. Parallel to what He has mentioned about prescribing something for Himself, as discussed above, is His statement, exalted is He:

“It is a duty We have undertaken to help the believers.”

And the statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the authentic hadith: “O Muʿādh, do you know what Allah’s right over His servants is?” I said, “Allah

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I have not found it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Textual Corruptions).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 9286

and His Messenger know best.” He said:

“His right over them is that they worship Him and associate nothing with Him. Do you know what the servants’ right over Allah is if they do that?” I said, “Allah and His Messenger know best.” He said: “Their right over Him is that He not punish them.”

Another example is his statement in several hadiths: “It is incumbent upon Allah to do such-and-such for him.” This right incumbent upon Him is one He Himself has established by His own word.

Parallel to His forbidding something to Himself and making something obligatory upon Himself are His declarations that He has sworn to do something, and His word that has already gone forth, as in His statements:

“Were it not for a word that had already gone forth from your Lord”;

“I will surely fill Hell”;

“We will surely destroy the wrongdoers”;

“So those who emigrated, were driven from their homes, suffered harm in My cause, fought and were killed—I will surely absolve them of their misdeeds and admit them into gardens beneath which rivers flow”;

“Then We will surely question those to whom messengers were sent.”

These and similar oath formulas convey obligation; their import differs from that of an oath conveying a mere statement of fact. Hence the jurists say: an oath either establishes a right or a prohibition, affirms something as true, or declares it false.

It is intelligible that a human being can be both the one giving a command and the one receiving it, as in His statements:

“Indeed, the soul persistently urges evil”;

“But as for one who feared standing before his Lord and restrained his soul from desire.”

Yet the servant has someone above him who commands and forbids him. It is therefore all the more fitting to conceive of the Lord, above whom there is no one, as Himself the One who commands and prescribes mercy for Himself, and the One who forbids and prohibits injustice for Himself

---

## Page 9287

and more appropriate still. His prescribing this for Himself entails His willing it, loving it, and being pleased with it. His forbidding injustice to Himself entails His hatred of it and aversion to it. His willing and loving an act necessitate His performing it, while His hatred of it and aversion to doing it prevent Him from performing it.

As for what He loves and hates among His servants’ deeds, that is a different matter. There is a distinction between His own act and what He brings about and creates. Nothing He has created constitutes injustice on His part, even if it constitutes injustice on the part of the human being who commits it. Similarly, human actions, in relation to the human being, constitute theft, fornication, prayer, or fasting. Allah Most High creates them by His will, yet they do not bear these descriptions in relation to Him. These designations apply to the agent in whom the act occurs, just as attributes belong to the entity in which they subsist, not to the Creator who created them and made them attributes. Allah Most High created every maker and what he makes, as stated in the hadith, and He is the Creator of every being possessing attributes and of its attributes.

Yet the attributes of created things—such as colours, tastes, and smells—are not His attributes, because they do not subsist in Him. Likewise, the movements of created things are not His movements, nor, in this respect, are they His acts, since they are effects that He has created. This distinction dispels many doubts.

He deserves praise and commendation for what He has prescribed for Himself. He is far exalted above failing to fulfil it, for such a failure, were it to occur, would be a deficiency. Likewise, He deserves praise and commendation for refraining from what He has forbidden to Himself. He is far exalted above doing it, for were He to do it, that would entail a deficiency.

---

## Page 9288

All this is clear, praise be to Allah, to those who have been granted knowledge and faith. It is also firmly established in the hearts of believers generally. Yet the Qadariyyah confused people with their specious arguments, and some who opposed them responded with a kind of falsehood, such as the arguments condemned by the early generations and the leading scholars. The Muʿtazilah said:

“There is agreement that Allah is not unjust, as the Quran and Sunnah indicate. An unjust person is someone who commits injustice, just as a just person is someone who acts justly. This is what people understand this designation to mean, on the basis of transmitted knowledge and reason.” They said: “If Allah created the acts of His servants that constitute injustice, He would be unjust.”

Their opponents countered:

“An unjust person is not someone who commits injustice, but someone in whom injustice subsists.”

Some of them said:

“An unjust person is someone who acquires injustice while being forbidden to commit it.”

Others said:

“An unjust person is someone who does something forbidden to him, or something he has been prohibited from doing.”

Still others said:

“Someone who commits injustice on his own account.”

What they mean is that the one who prohibits and forbids him must be someone other than himself, whom he is obliged to obey. Thus, they considered injustice on Allah’s part intrinsically inconceivable, just as it is impossible for there to be someone above Him who commands and prohibits Him. Both groups regard it as impossible for His acts to entail any judgment pertaining to the Lord Himself. These opponents could not dispute the Muʿtazilah’s contention that a just person is someone who acts justly; rather, they conceded it, although some people disputed it out of sheer obstinacy.

---

## Page 9289

What exposes the Muʿtazilah’s deception is to say to them:

The unjust or just person, as people understand these terms, does indeed commit injustice or act justly; yet he also incurs sin thereby. People do not know anyone who is called unjust without the act by which he became unjust subsisting in him. Rather, they recognize as unjust only someone in whom the act he performed—and by which he became unjust—subsists, even if his act concerns someone else and produces an effect separate from him. They recognize someone as unjust only when that act subsists in him. Thus, when you define an unjust person as someone who commits injustice, but mean someone who produces it in another, this is deception and a corruption of revealed law, reason, and language.

You have done the same with the designation “speaker,” saying that it means someone who produces speech, even in another. You have deemed someone who brings into being speech separate from himself, subsisting in another, to be a speaker, even though no speech whatsoever subsists in him. This is among the gravest forms of falsehood, Qarmatianism, and sophistry.

For this reason, the early generations confronted them with the implications concerning the speech He brought into being in inanimate objects, and likewise what He created in animals. On this view, no distinction is made between speaking and causing another to speak. Yet the skins only said:

“Allah, who has made everything speak, has made us speak.”

They did not say that Allah Himself spoke those words. Hence some of the early generations, such as Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd al-Hāshimī and others, said, in substance, that on this view the speech created in Pharaoh, such that he said:

“I am your lord most high,”

would be like the speech created in the tree, such that it said:

“Indeed, I am Allah; there is no deity except Me.”

Thus, either Pharaoh was speaking the truth, or

---

## Page 9290

the tree was like Pharaoh.

Toward this meaning incline the unionists among the Jahmiyyah, who recite:

“All speech in existence is His speech;
To us, its prose and verse are all the same.”

This encompasses every form of disbelief. Thus, it is plainly evident to learned and ordinary people alike that anyone who says, “No speech whatsoever subsists in a speaker,” is in reality saying that he is not a speaker, for speech subsisting in someone is precisely what makes him a speaker. Hence their earliest adherents used to say:

“He is not a speaker.”

Then they said:

“He is a speaker in a figurative sense.”

This is because it is firmly established in people’s innate disposition that speech must subsist in a speaker, even if he also produces it, just as a person’s speech subsists in him while he acquires it. But to regard merely bringing speech into being in another as speech of one’s own is false.

The same applies to injustice. Grant that an unjust person is someone who commits injustice: this does not mean someone who brings it about in another while no act whatsoever subsists in him. Rather, an act must subsist in him, even if it affects someone else. This is one answer.

They may then be told:

Injustice involves a relation and an attribution. It is injustice on the part of the wrongdoer in the sense that it is aggression and oppression proceeding from him; and it is injustice against the wronged person in the sense that it is oppression and aggression directed against him. But someone against whom it constitutes no aggression, and from whom it constitutes no aggression against another, has no such relation to that injustice: it is neither injustice committed by him nor injustice done to him.

---

## Page 9291

When Allah, glorified be He, creates His servants’ acts, this is akin to His creating their attributes: it is they who possess those attributes. Thus, when He makes some things black and others white, tall or short, moving or still, knowledgeable or ignorant, capable or incapable, alive or dead, believing or disbelieving, blessed or wretched, wrongdoers or wronged, it is the created being that is described as white or black, tall or short, alive or dead, a wrongdoer or someone wronged, and so forth. Allah, glorified be He, does not thereby acquire any of these descriptions. His bringing into being an act that constitutes injustice committed by one person against another is comparable to His bringing eating and drinking into being: one individual does the eating and another is eaten, yet He is thereby neither the eater nor the eaten. There are many comparable examples. Although there are profound purposes of wisdom in His creating His servants’ acts, whether their effects remain with them or extend to others—just as there is profound wisdom in His creating their attributes and all other created things—this is not the place to explain that in detail. These two arguments have exposed the Qadariyyah’s misrepresentation.

As for the definitions advanced against them, these are mere assertions that also contradict what is known from revelation, language, and reason, or else contain some ambiguity. To say, “A wrongdoer is someone in whom injustice subsists,” entails that injustice must indeed subsist in him. But he may be asked: “Even if he does not perpetrate or command it—or must he be its perpetrator

---

## Page 9292

as well?” If he means the former, then his restricting the definition of a wrongdoer to someone in whom injustice subsists is like those others restricting it to the commission of injustice. What people, ordinary and learned alike, recognize is that a wrongdoer commits injustice and that his injustice is an act subsisting in him. Each group has denied part of the truth.

As for their saying that a wrongdoer is “someone who does something forbidden to him, or something he has been prohibited from doing,” and similar formulations, this description is valid.

However, it should be said: The Quran and Sunnah establish that Allah Most High has prescribed mercy for Himself, that helping the believers is a duty He has undertaken, that rewarding the obedient is a duty He has undertaken, and that He has forbidden injustice to Himself. It is He, glorified be He, who has Himself forbidden injustice to Himself, just as He has Himself prescribed mercy for Himself. No one else can forbid anything to Him or impose any obligation upon Him, much less can such a prohibition or obligation be known through reason or otherwise. Accordingly, the injustice He has forbidden to Himself is undoubtedly injustice. It is something possible and within His power, yet He, glorified be He, refrains from it by His will and choice, despite His ability to do it, because He is just and does no wrong—just as He refrains from punishing the prophets and believers, and from making the innocent bear the sins of transgressors.

Section

His words:

“I have made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another.”

It should be understood that this hadith is of lofty standing and immense importance. For this reason, Imam Ahmad used to

---

## Page 9293

say:
“It is the noblest hadith of the people of Syria.” Whenever Abū Idrīs al-Khawlānī narrated it, he would kneel. Its narrator is Abū Dharr, whom the sky has never shaded, nor the earth borne, anyone more truthful in speech. It is one of the divine hadiths that the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, narrated from his Lord, declaring them to be the words of Allah Most High, though they are not Quran. Zāhir al-Shahhāmī, ʿAbd al-Ghanī al-Maqdisī, Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Maqdisī and others have compiled collections on this subject. This hadith encompasses great foundational principles of religion concerning knowledge and action, fundamentals and subsidiary matters.

The first statement—His words:

“I have forbidden injustice to Myself”—

embraces most questions concerning the divine attributes and decree when given the explanation it deserves. We have mentioned only those initial, comprehensive points that must be brought to attention.

As for the second statement—His words:

“I have made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another”—

it encompasses the whole religion. Everything Allah has forbidden comes under injustice, and everything He has commanded comes under justice.

For this reason, He Most High says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs, and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance so that people may uphold justice. And We sent down iron, with its mighty force and benefits for people, so that Allah may know who supports Him and His messengers without having seen Him.”

He thus states that He sent the messengers and sent down the Scripture and the balance so that people would uphold justice. He also mentions that He sent down iron, through which He supports this truth.

---

## Page 9294

for the Scripture guides and the sword lends support, and sufficient is your Lord as a guide and helper.

For this reason, the welfare of the people depends on those entrusted with the Scripture and those entrusted with iron. As one of the early generations said: “There are two groups who, when upright, bring uprightness to the people: rulers and scholars.” In explaining His words, Most High:

“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you,”

they offered interpretations encompassing both scholars and rulers. Hence Imam Ahmad and others explicitly stated that both groups are included in this verse, since each must be obeyed in the duties he undertakes in obedience to Allah. During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, his deputies—such as ‘Alī, Mu‘ādh, Abū Mūsā, ‘Attāb ibn Asīd, ‘Uthmān ibn Abī al-‘Ās and others like them—combined both roles. The same was true of his successors, such as Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī, and their deputies.

Thus, the established practice was that the person leading the people in prayer was the one entrusted with the Scripture, and the person conducting jihad was the one entrusted with iron. This continued until these responsibilities were subsequently divided. Once they were divided, anyone responsible for military affairs—conducting jihad against the unbelievers and punishing evildoers—had to be obeyed in whatever he commanded in that capacity that constituted obedience to Allah. Likewise, anyone responsible for collecting and distributing funds had to be obeyed in whatever he commanded in that capacity that constituted obedience to Allah. Similarly, anyone entrusted with the Scripture, conveying and explaining its accounts and commands, must be believed in the truthful accounts he conveys in that capacity and obeyed in whatever he commands that constitutes obedience to Allah.

---

## Page 9295

The point here is that the purpose of all this is for people to uphold justice. Thus, when the polytheists prohibited things for which Allah had sent down no authority and commanded things for which Allah had sent down no authority, Allah revealed passages in Sūrat al-An‘ām, Sūrat al-A‘rāf and elsewhere condemning them for this and explaining what He Himself had commanded and prohibited. He said:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded justice. Turn your faces toward Him at every place of prayer, and call upon Him, devoting your religion sincerely to Him.’”

And He, Most High, said:

“Say, ‘My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether committed openly or in secret; sin; unjust aggression; associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority; and saying about Allah what you do not know.’”

This verse encompasses all categories of prohibited acts, as we have explained elsewhere, while the preceding verse encompasses all categories of obligations, as we have also explained. In His words:

“My Lord has commanded justice. Turn your faces toward Him at every place of prayer, and call upon Him, devoting your religion sincerely to Him,”

He commands, alongside justice, the affirmation of His oneness: worshipping Allah alone, without any partner. This is the foundation of the religion, and its opposite is the sin that is not forgiven. He, Most High, said:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives anything less than that for whomever He wills.”

This is the religion that Allah commanded all the messengers to follow and with which He sent them to all nations. He, Most High, said:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him: ‘There is no deity but Me, so worship Me.’”

And He, Most High, said: “And ask those whom

---

## Page 9296

We sent before you as Our messengers: did We appoint any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

And He, Most High, said:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’”

And He, Most High, said:

“He has ordained for you the same religion that He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed to you, and that which We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses and Jesus: uphold the religion and do not divide yourselves within it.”

And He, Most High, said:

“O messengers, eat of good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know all that you do.”

“And indeed, this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

For this reason, al-Bukhārī included in his Sahīh a chapter entitled “Reports Establishing That the Religion of the Prophets Is One,” and cited the authentic hadith concerning this. This is Islam in its universal sense, upon which all the prophets agreed. Noah, peace be upon him, said:

“And I have been commanded to be among the Muslims.”

And He, Most High, said concerning Abraham:

“When his Lord said to him, ‘Submit,’ he said, ‘I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds.’”

“And Abraham enjoined this upon his sons, as did Jacob: ‘My sons, Allah has chosen this religion for you, so do not die except as Muslims.’”

“And Moses said, ‘My people, if you have believed in Allah, then put your trust in Him, if you are Muslims.’”

And He, Most High, said:

“The disciples said, ‘We are Allah’s helpers. We believe in Allah, and bear witness that we are Muslims.’”

And He said in the account of Bilqīs:

“My Lord, I have wronged myself, and I submit with Solomon to Allah, the Lord of the worlds.”

And He said:

“Indeed, We sent down the Torah, containing guidance and light. By it, the prophets who submitted to Allah judged for the Jews.”

---

## Page 9297

And this monotheism, which is the foundation of religion, is the greatest justice. Its opposite, associating partners with Allah, is the greatest injustice. As al-Bukhārī and Muslim reported in their Sahīhs, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“When this verse was revealed, ‘Those who believe and do not mix their faith with wrongdoing,’ it weighed heavily upon the Prophet’s Companions, and they said, ‘Which of us has not wronged himself?’ He replied, ‘Have you not heard the words of the righteous servant: “Indeed, associating partners with Allah is a tremendous wrong”?’”

Also in the two Sahīhs, Ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, which sin is greatest?’ He replied, ‘To set up a rival to Allah when He created you.’ I asked, ‘Then which?’ He replied, ‘Then to kill your child for fear that he will eat with you.’ I asked, ‘Then which?’ He replied, ‘To commit adultery with your neighbour’s wife.’ Allah then revealed in confirmation of this: ‘And those who do not invoke another god besides Allah, nor kill a soul whose killing Allah has forbidden, except with just cause, nor engage in unlawful sexual intercourse’—to the end of the verse.”

The following has been reported from more than one of the early generations, and has also been narrated as a report traced back to the Prophet:

“There are three registers of wrongdoing: a register from which Allah forgives nothing, a register in which Allah leaves nothing unsettled, and a register to which Allah pays no heed at all. As for the register from which Allah forgives nothing, it is associating partners with Him, for Allah does not forgive the association of partners with Him. As for the register in which Allah leaves nothing unsettled, it is people’s wrongdoing against one another, for Allah will certainly secure justice for the wronged against the wrongdoer. As for the register to which Allah pays no heed at all, it is the servant’s wrongdoing against himself in matters between himself and

---

## Page 9298

his Lord.” That is, this kind of wrongdoing may be forgiven without the consent of other people. If He wills, He punishes the one who has wronged himself, and if He wills, He forgives him. We have discussed these noble subjects and comprehensive foundations at length in our writings on principles. We have explained the various kinds of wrongdoing, why associating partners with Allah is the greatest form of wrongdoing, and what constitutes such association, in its gross and subtle forms.

For it is stated in the hadith:

“Associating partners with Allah is more hidden within this community than the creeping of ants.”

It is also reported that the following verse was revealed concerning those who show off:

“So whoever hopes to meet his Lord, let him do righteous deeds and associate no one in worship with his Lord.”

Shaddād ibn Aws used to say: “O you who remain of the Arabs! O you who remain of the Arabs! What I fear for you is showing off and hidden desire.”

Abū Dāwūd al-Sijistānī, compiler of the well-known Sunan collection, said: “The hidden desire is the love of leadership.”

This is because the love of leadership is the root of aggression and wrongdoing, just as showing off is a form of associating partners with Allah, or its beginning. Associating partners with Allah is the greatest corruption, just as monotheism is the greatest righteousness. Thus Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, Pharaoh exalted himself in the land and divided its people into factions, oppressing one group among them, slaughtering their sons and sparing their women. Indeed, he was one of those who spread corruption.”

He went on to conclude the surah with His words:

“That home of the Hereafter We reserve for those who seek neither superiority on earth nor corruption.”

And He said:

“We declared to the Children of Israel in the Scripture: ‘You will surely cause corruption in the land twice, and you will surely become exceedingly arrogant.’”

And He said:

---

## Page 9299

“For that reason, We decreed for the Children of Israel that whoever kills a soul, unless in retribution for murder or for spreading corruption in the land, it is as though he has killed all mankind; and whoever saves a life, it is as though he has saved all mankind.”

And the angels said:

“Will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood?”

The foundation of righteousness is monotheism and faith, while the foundation of corruption is associating partners with Allah and disbelief. As He said of the hypocrites:

“When they are told, ‘Do not spread corruption in the land,’ they say, ‘We are only reformers.’ Indeed, it is they who spread corruption, but they do not realize it.”

This is because the soundness of anything consists in its being such that its intended purpose is realized in it and through it. Thus, the jurists say: a valid contract is one that produces its legal effects and achieves its intended purpose; an invalid contract is one that neither produces its legal effects nor achieves an intended purpose. What they call ‘valid,’ as opposed to ‘invalid,’ is what is sound.

The early generations frequently used the expressions ‘This is not sound’ or ‘This is sound,’ just as later scholars frequently used ‘This is valid’ and ‘This is not valid.’

Allah Most High created man solely to worship Him, and his body follows his heart. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the authentic hadith:

“Indeed, there is a piece of flesh in the body: when it is sound, the rest of the body is sound, and when it is corrupt, the rest of the body is corrupt. Indeed, it is the heart.”

The heart’s soundness consists in the realization, in it and through it, of the purpose for which it was created, namely:

---

## Page 9300

knowing Allah, loving Him, and venerating Him. Its corruption lies in the opposite of this. Hearts can never be sound without these things.

The heart has two faculties: knowledge and intention, just as the body has sensation and voluntary movement. Just as the faculties of sensation and movement become impaired when they depart from their innate, natural condition, so too does the heart become corrupt when it departs from the innate condition into which every child is born: acknowledging its Lord and desiring Him, so that He is the ultimate object of its purpose and will. This is worship, for worship is complete love combined with complete humility.

Whenever the heart’s movement, orientation, and will are not directed toward Allah Most High, it is corrupt. Either it turns away from Allah and His remembrance, remaining heedless, whether with or without denial; or it has remembrance and awareness, but its purpose and will are directed toward something other than Him, because its remembrance is too weak to draw the heart toward desiring Allah, loving Him, and worshipping Him. Conversely, whenever the heart’s knowledge and remembrance grow strong, they necessarily give rise to its intention and knowledge. Allah Most High said:

“So turn away from whoever turns away from remembering Us and desires nothing but the life of this world.”

“That is the extent of their knowledge.”

He thus commanded His Prophet to turn away from anyone who turned away from remembering Allah and desired nothing beyond what is in this world. This is the condition of someone whose heart has become corrupt, who does not remember his Lord or turn back to Him so as to seek His countenance and worship Him with sincere, exclusive devotion.

Then He said:

“That is the extent of their knowledge.”

Thus He stated that they

---

## Page 9301

have acquired no knowledge beyond the affairs of this world; it is their greatest concern and the limit of their knowledge. The believer, by contrast, has Allah as his greatest concern, and his knowledge and remembrance ultimately lead to Him. This, then, is a vast and important subject that we have discussed in its appropriate places.

Since affirming Allah’s oneness is the foundation of human righteousness, while associating partners with Him is the foundation of human corruption, equity is linked to affirming His oneness, which is the foundation of justice. Likewise, the desire for superiority is linked to corruption, since it is the foundation of injustice. The two in each pair are bound together, like two animals tied with a single tether. Affirming Allah’s oneness and the good deeds that follow from it constitute righteousness and justice. Thus, a righteous man is one who fulfills his obligations: he is virtuous and just.

Sins involving neglect of, or transgression against, the rights of Allah Most High and the rights of His servants constitute corruption and injustice. This is why highway robbers are called corrupters, and their punishment is a right belonging to Allah Most High, since both qualities are present in them. Whoever desires superiority over another human being wrongs him and transgresses against him. You have no greater claim to superiority over him than he has to superiority over you, since you both belong to the same human race. Equity and justice require that they be brothers, as Allah has described the believers.

Although affirming Allah’s oneness is the foundation of righteousness, it is also the greatest expression of justice. Thus Allah Most High said:

“Say, ‘O People of the Book, come to a common word between us and you: that we worship none but Allah, associate nothing with Him, and do not take one another as lords besides Allah.’ But if they turn away, then say, ‘Bear witness that we are Muslims.’”

This is why singling it out

---

## Page 9302

for mention in passages such as His words:

“Say, ‘My Lord has commanded equity. Direct your faces toward Him at every place of worship, and call upon Him, devoting your religion sincerely to Him,’”

does not preclude its being included in equity, just as mentioning righteous deeds after faith does not preclude their being included in faith. Similar examples occur in His words:

“His angels and His messengers, Gabriel and Michael,”

and:

“from the prophets their covenant, and from you.”

This is on the view that the term “faith” encompasses righteous deeds. This holds whether one maintains that, in such instances, the latter remains included under the first term and is therefore mentioned twice, or that joining it to the first term means that it is not included under it here, although it is included when that term occurs alone. The same has been said concerning the terms “the poor” and “the needy,” and similar expressions whose scope varies depending on whether they occur separately or together.

The point, however, is that every good falls within equity and justice, and every evil falls within injustice. Thus, justice is obligatory in every matter and upon everyone, while injustice is forbidden in every matter and to everyone. It is never permissible to wrong anyone, whether a Muslim, a disbeliever, or even a wrongdoer. Rather, even in responding to wrongdoing, what is permissible or obligatory is to treat the wrongdoer justly. Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, stand firm for Allah as witnesses in equity, and do not let hatred”

—that is, do not let hatred, meaning hatred of a people—namely the disbelievers—lead you to act unjustly:

“of a people lead you to be unjust. Be just; that is nearer to righteousness.”

Allah Most High also says:

“So whoever attacks you, respond against him in the same measure as his attack against you.”

And Allah Most High says: “And if you punish, then punish with the like of

---

## Page 9303

what you were made to suffer.” Allah Most High also says:

“The recompense of an evil deed is an evil deed like it.”

This is also indicated by His words in the hadith:

“My servants, I have forbidden injustice to Myself and have made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another.”

This addresses all servants: no one is to wrong anyone else. The ordering of human affairs under the Sharia rests upon this principle: justice concerning lives, property, sexual relations, lineage, and honour. Hence the Sunnah prescribes equivalent retaliation in these matters, responding to the aggressor with the like of his action. Yet determining or achieving exact equivalence may be impossible or difficult. What is then obligatory is the closest approximation possible. Thus one says, “This is a closer match,” or, “This is a closer likeness.” This is the best course because it most closely approximates what is truly just and right, when exact justice itself lies beyond one’s ability. Hence Allah Most High says:

“Give full measure and weight with justice. We do not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

When commanding that full measure and weight be given with justice, He states that He does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. This is because measuring inevitably leaves one of two measured quantities greater than the other, even if only by a grain or a few grains. Likewise, a slight, unavoidable discrepancy may occur in weighing. Thus Allah Most High says:

“We do not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

For this reason, equivalent retaliation is prescribed when it can be exacted without unjust excess, as with wounds that reach a bone and limbs that end at a joint. If exacting retaliation would involve unjust excess, recourse is made to its substitute, namely

---

## Page 9304

financial compensation; for this is closer to justice than inflicting additional injury on the person against whom retaliation is exacted. This is the argument of those jurists who hold that retaliatory execution may only be carried out by a sword-stroke to the neck. They argue: “When killing is carried out by something other than a sword, or at a place other than the neck, we cannot ascertain equivalence. Indeed, burning, drowning, cutting in two, and similar methods may cause greater pain.”

Yet the view of those who say, “He is to be subjected to the same treatment he inflicted,” is closer to justice. By striving to make the two acts equivalent, a person has achieved as much justice as he is capable of; any disparity in pain lies beyond his control. But if someone cuts off his victim’s hands and feet and then cuts him in two, and this is answered by beheading him with a sword; or if he crushes his victim’s head between two stones but is himself executed with a sword, then we know with certainty that there is neither parity nor equivalence. We would thus have done something that we know cannot achieve equivalence, and with which equivalence is impossible. This is unlike the first approach, in which equivalence may be achieved, since a disparity is not certain.

Likewise, some jurists have set aside equivalent retaliation for a blow, a slap, and similar acts in favour of discretionary punishment, on the grounds that equivalence cannot be achieved in such cases. The position of the Rightly Guided Caliphs and other Companions, however, which Ahmad also expressly affirmed, is what the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, establishes: that equivalent retaliation applies to such acts, because this comes closer to justice and equivalence. For if we endeavour to subject him to the same kind of act as he committed, and to approximate

---

## Page 9305

the severity of one to that of the other, this is preferable to inflicting a punishment that differs from what he inflicted in kind, severity, and manner. The same reasoning also applies to compensation for animals, real property, and the like: whether through an approximately equivalent replacement or through their monetary value, as Ahmad explicitly stated in various cases concerning compensation for animals and other property.

Al-Shāfiʿī likewise explicitly stated that whoever demolishes another person’s wall must rebuild it as it was. Solomon, peace be upon him, ruled on this basis in the case concerning the cultivated field in which both he and his father passed judgment, as has been explained elsewhere. In all these matters, the aim of the Sharīʿah is to pursue justice as far as possible. This is also the scholars’ aim, but those with the deepest understanding are those whose positions most closely accord with justice in reality, although each has been granted knowledge and sound judgment. For it is to establish justice that Allah revealed the Scriptures and sent the messengers. Its opposite is injustice, as He, glorified be He, said:

“O My servants, I have forbidden injustice for Myself and made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another.”

Justice must be preceded by knowledge—for someone without knowledge does not know what justice is. Human beings are unjust and ignorant, except those to whom Allah turns in mercy, who thereby become knowledgeable and just. Thus people, judges and others alike, fall into three categories: the knowledgeable but unjust, and the ignorant wrongdoer. These two belong to the people of the Fire, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

---

## Page 9306

“Judges are of three kinds: two are in the Fire and one is in Paradise. A man who knows the truth and judges accordingly is in Paradise; a man who judges people’s cases in ignorance is in the Fire; and a man who knows the truth but judges contrary to it is in the Fire.”

These latter two categories correspond to his saying:

“Whoever speaks about the Quran on the basis of his own opinion and is correct has nevertheless erred; and whoever speaks about the Quran on the basis of his own opinion and is mistaken, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

Everyone who judges between two people is a judge, whether a military commander, the head of an administrative bureau, or an official appointed to enjoin right and forbid wrong. This includes even someone who judges children’s handwriting, for the Companions counted such a person among judges. Since judges are commanded to act with justice and knowledge, while their obligation extends only as far as their utmost effort allows, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a judge exercises his judgment and is correct, he has two rewards; when he exercises his judgment and is mistaken, he has one reward.”

Section

Having mentioned at the beginning of the hadith the justice He has enjoined and the injustice He has forbidden, both for Himself and for His servants, He then mentioned His kindness to His servants, despite His having no need of them and their utter need of Him. They cannot secure any benefit for themselves or ward off any harm unless He makes it possible. He commanded His servants to ask Him for this and informed them

---

## Page 9307

that they can neither benefit nor harm Him, despite the immense blessings He bestows upon them and the afflictions He wards off from them. Securing benefit and warding off harm concern either religion or worldly life. Thus, there are four categories: guidance and forgiveness, which secure benefit and ward off harm in religion; and food and clothing, which secure benefit and ward off harm in worldly life.

Alternatively, one may say that guidance and forgiveness concern the heart, which is the sovereign of the body and the source of voluntary actions. Food and clothing concern the body: food secures its benefit, while clothing wards off harm to it.

He began with guidance, for although beneficial guidance is that which concerns religion, all human actions depend upon Allah’s guidance, as He, glorified be He, says:

“Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High, Who created and proportioned, and Who determined and guided.”

Moses said:

“Our Lord is He who gave each thing its form, then guided it.”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“And We showed him the two ways.”

And He says:

“Indeed, We guided him to the way, whether he be grateful or ungrateful.”

For this reason, it has been said that guidance is of four kinds. The first is guidance towards what serves worldly welfare. This is shared by human beings and other animals, and by believers and unbelievers alike.

---

## Page 9308

The second is guidance in the sense of calling people to what benefits them and commanding them to follow it. This consists of setting forth proofs, sending messengers, and revealing scriptures. This, too, extends to all those accountable under religious law, whether they believe or disbelieve. As Allah Most High says:

“As for Thamūd, We guided them, but they preferred blindness to guidance.”

And He, Most High, says:

“You are only a warner, and for every people there is a guide.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Indeed, you guide to a straight path.”

This, together with His words,

“You cannot guide whom you love,”

makes clear that the guidance He affirms consists of explaining and calling, commanding and forbidding, teaching, and everything that follows from these. It is not the guidance He denies, which is the third kind and which none but Allah has the power to grant.

The third kind is guidance that consists of placing guidance in hearts. Some call this inspiration and direction. Others describe it as the creation of the capacity to believe, just as they understand divine enabling and similar concepts. This is based on the view that capacity exists only concurrently with the act. Those among the proponents of affirmation who hold this view therefore define divine enabling, guidance, and similar concepts as the creation of the capacity to obey.

As for those who hold that there are two kinds of capacity: one precedes the act and is the capacity stipulated as a condition for religious obligation, as Allah Most High says:

“Pilgrimage to the House is a duty owed to Allah by people who are able to make the journey.”

---

## Page 9309

and the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said to ‘Imrān ibn Husayn:

“Pray standing; if you cannot, then sitting; and if you cannot, then lying on your side.”

This capacity may be accompanied either by performing the act or by refraining from it. It is the only capacity recognized by the Qadariyyah, just as their opponents among the proponents of affirmation recognize only the capacity concurrent with the act. The discerning scholars among the leading authorities in jurisprudence, hadith, theology, and other fields, however, affirm both kinds, as we have explained at length elsewhere. Both scriptural and rational evidence establish the two kinds.

The second is the capacity concurrent with the act, which necessitates it. This is the capacity denied to those who do not perform the act, as in His words:

“They were unable to hear, nor did they see,”

and His words:

“They are unable to hear.”

This is the guidance frequently mentioned in the Quran, as in His words:

“Guide us to the straight path,”

and His words:

“Whoever Allah wills to guide, He opens his heart to Islam; and whoever He wills to lead astray, He makes his heart tight and constricted,”

and His words:

“Whoever Allah guides is rightly guided, but whoever He leaves astray, you will never find for him a protecting guide,”

and similar passages. This is what the Qadariyyah deny that Allah brings about; they claim instead that the servant guides himself. This hadith and others like it constitute proof against them, for He says:

“O My servants, all of you are astray except those whom I have guided, so seek My guidance and I will guide you.”

Thus He commanded His servants to ask Him for guidance, just as He commanded them to do in the Mother of

---

## Page 9310

the Book, in His words:

“Guide us to the straight path.”

According to the Qadariyyah, Allah can provide no guidance beyond what He has already done: sending the messengers, establishing the proofs, and removing impediments. In their view, the believer enjoys no advantage over the disbeliever in Allah’s guidance, nor has Allah bestowed any greater favor upon the believer than upon the disbeliever in this regard.

He has made clear that He singles out some for this guidance, after mentioning the universality of His call, in His words:

“Allah invites to the Home of Peace and guides whom He wills to a straight path.”

Thus, the hadith combines declaring Him free of the injustice that some proponents of affirmation deem possible for Him with explaining that He is the One who guides His servants, thereby refuting the Qadariyyah. There He declares His justice, which some proponents of affirmation mention; here He declares His beneficence and power, which the Qadariyyah deny. Yet each group intends to glorify Him, without recognizing what its position entails.

The fourth category is guidance in the Hereafter, as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah will admit those who believe and do righteous deeds into gardens beneath which rivers flow. They will be adorned therein with bracelets of gold and pearls, and their clothing therein will be silk.”

“They will be guided to good speech and guided to the path of the Praiseworthy.”

He also says:

“Indeed, those who believe and do righteous deeds—their Lord will guide them because of their faith. Rivers will flow beneath them in Gardens of Bliss.”

His words, “Their Lord will guide them because of their faith,” are like His words:

---

## Page 9311

“And those who believed and whose descendants followed them in faith—We will join their descendants with them, and We will not deprive them of anything of their deeds,”

according to one of the two interpretations of this verse. This guidance is the reward for following guidance in this world, just as going astray in the Hereafter is the recompense for going astray in this world. Likewise, pursuing evil in this world is recompensed by being guided to the path of the Fire, as Allah Most High says:

“Gather those who committed wrongdoing, their counterparts, and whatever they used to worship
besides Allah, and guide them to the path of Hellfire.”

He also says:

“Whoever is blind in this world will be blind in the Hereafter and even further astray.”

And He says:

“When guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor suffer misery.
But whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.
He will say, ‘My Lord, why have You raised me blind when I used to see?’
He will say, ‘Thus did Our signs come to you, but you forgot them; and thus today you will be forgotten.’”

And He says:

“Whoever Allah guides is rightly guided, but those He sends astray—you will never find protectors for them besides Him. We will gather them on the Day of Resurrection on their faces, blind, mute, and deaf,”

to the end of the verse. He thus tells us that those who go astray in this world will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection blind, mute, and deaf. Recompense always corresponds to the deed, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The All-Merciful shows mercy to those who are merciful. Show mercy to those on earth, and the One in heaven will show mercy to you.”

He also said:

“Whoever follows a path seeking knowledge, Allah will thereby make a path to Paradise easy for him. Whoever makes things easier for someone in hardship, Allah will make things easier for him in this world and the Hereafter. Whoever conceals a Muslim’s faults, Allah will conceal his faults in this world

---

## Page 9312

and the Hereafter. And Allah helps His servant as long as His servant helps his brother.”

He also said:

“Whoever is asked about knowledge he possesses and conceals it, Allah will bridle him with a bridle of fire on the Day of Resurrection.”

Allah Most High says:

“Let them pardon and overlook. Would you not love for Allah to forgive you?”

And He says:

“Whether you disclose good or conceal it, or pardon an evil, Allah is ever Pardoning and All-Powerful.”

There are many such examples in the Quran and Sunnah. Likewise, Allah rewards a person in this world for the good he does in following guidance by opening the way to further guidance. Hence the saying: “Whoever acts upon what he knows, Allah grants him knowledge of what he did not know.”

Allah Most High says:

“If they had done what they were instructed to do, it would have been better for them and would have made them firmer,”

through to His word, “straight.”

And He says:

“There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book, by which Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace.”

And He says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah and believe in His Messenger; He will grant you a double share of His mercy, provide you with a light by which to walk, and forgive you.”

And He says:

“If you fear Allah, He will grant you a criterion.”

They interpreted this as victory and deliverance, as in His words, “the Day of the Criterion.” It has also been interpreted as a light by which one distinguishes truth from falsehood.

Similar to this are His words:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him and provide for him from where he does not expect.”

He promises those who fear Him ways out of hardship and beneficial provision.

Also belonging to this category are His words:

“As for those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and grants them

---

## Page 9313

their piety.” And His words:

“They were youths who believed in their Lord, and We increased them in guidance.”

Also among these are His words:

“Indeed, We have granted you a clear victory, so that Allah may forgive you your past and future sins, complete His favor upon you, guide you to a straight path, and grant you a mighty victory.”

Conversely, misguidance and acts of disobedience result from earlier sins, as Allah says:

“When they deviated, Allah caused their hearts to deviate.”

“And their saying, ‘Our hearts are veiled.’ Rather, Allah has sealed them because of their disbelief.”

And He says:

“For breaking their covenant, We cursed them and made their hearts hard.”

And He says:

“They swore their most solemn oaths by Allah,”

through to His words, “they will not believe,” and then through to His words, “wander blindly.” This is a vast subject.

Hence some of the early generations said: “Part of the reward for a good deed is another good deed after it, and part of the punishment for an evil deed is another evil deed after it.”

It has become common among ordinary people to place His words, “Fear Allah, and Allah teaches you,” in the first category, citing them as evidence that fearing Allah is a cause of His teaching a person. Most learned people challenge this inference, because He did not connect the second verb to the first as a consequence is connected to a condition. He did not say, “Fear Allah, and He will teach you,” or “so He will teach you.” Rather, He used the conjunction “and,” and conjunction does not in itself imply that the first is the cause of the second.

It may, however, be said that conjunction can convey an association and mutual dependence, as when one says: “Visit me, and I shall visit you; greet us, and we shall greet

---

## Page 9314

you,” and similar expressions that imply a connection between the two acts and a reciprocal exchange between the parties. It is as when a slave says to his master, “Free me, and I shall owe you a thousand,” or a woman says to her husband, “Divorce me, and you shall have a thousand,” or, “Release me from the marriage, and you shall have a thousand.” This is equivalent to her saying, “For a thousand,” or, “I shall owe you a thousand.”

Likewise, if he says, “You are free, and you owe a thousand,” or, “You are divorced, and you owe a thousand,” this is equivalent to saying, “I owe a thousand,” or, “For a thousand,” according to the majority of jurists. Distinguishing between these formulations is an anomalous opinion. One party to an exchange may say to the other, “I give you this and take this,” or use similar expressions, and the other replies, “Yes,” even though neither act is exclusively the cause of the other, to the exclusion of the reverse.

Thus, His words:

“Fear Allah, and Allah teaches you,”

may belong to this category. The Lord’s teaching and the servant’s fear of Him are closely connected: each accompanies and entails the other. Whenever Allah teaches a person beneficial knowledge, fear of Him accompanies it in proportion to that knowledge; and whenever a person fears Him, He increases that person in knowledge, and so it continues.

Section

As for His words:

“O My servants, all of you are hungry except those whom I feed, so ask Me for food and I shall feed you. All of you are naked except those whom I clothe, so ask Me for clothing and I shall clothe you,”

these entail two fundamental principles:

---

## Page 9315

The first is the obligation to rely upon Allah for provision, which includes securing benefits, such as food, and warding off harm, as clothing does. No one other than Allah has absolute power to provide food and clothing. Whatever power some servants possess extends only to some of the means of obtaining these things. Hence He says:

“The father is responsible for their provision and clothing according to what is reasonable.”

And He says:

“Do not entrust the foolish with your wealth, which Allah has made a means of support for you, but provide for them from it and clothe them.”

What people are commanded to do is thus within their power. Likewise, He says:

“Or feeding, on a day of severe hunger, an orphan who is a near relative, or a needy person in abject poverty.”

And He says:

“Feed the contented poor and those who approach you in need.”

And He says:

“Eat from them and feed the wretched and poor.”

And He says:

“When they are told, ‘Spend from what Allah has provided you,’ those who disbelieve say to those who believe, ‘Should we feed someone whom Allah could have fed, had He willed?’”

He thus condemns those who neglect what they have been commanded to do, contenting themselves with whatever comes to pass through divine decree. From this we understand that taking a means that is commanded or permissible does not conflict with the obligation to rely upon Allah when that means is present. Rather, our need for Allah and dependence upon Him remain even while we employ the means, for nothing in creation is, on its own, a sufficient cause for attaining what is sought. Consequently, events do not necessarily follow from what may serve as their causes; they do so only by the will of Allah Most High. Whatever Allah wills happens, and whatever He does not will does not happen.

Whoever imagines that employing the means dispenses with the need to rely upon Allah has abandoned the reliance that Allah has made obligatory upon him and has failed in his duty to uphold Allah’s oneness. This is why people such as these are forsaken

---

## Page 9316

when they rely upon the means. Whoever hopes for victory or provision from anyone other than Allah will be forsaken by Allah. As ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “Let no servant place his hope in anyone but his Lord, or fear anything but his sin.”

Allah Most High has said:

“Whatever mercy Allah grants to people, none can withhold; and whatever He withholds, none can release after Him. He is the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

And He has said:

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it except Him; and if He intends good for you, none can repel His bounty. He bestows it upon whomever He wills of His servants.”

And He has said:

“Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah intended harm for me, could they remove His harm? Or if He intended mercy for me, could they withhold His mercy?’ Say, ‘Allah is sufficient for me. In Him those who trust place their trust.’”

Likewise, anyone who sets about relying upon Allah while abandoning the means he has been commanded to employ is also ignorant and unjust, disobeying Allah by neglecting what He has commanded. For doing what is commanded is an act of worship of Allah.

Allah Most High has said:

“So worship Him and rely upon Him.”

And He has said:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

And He has said:

“Say, ‘He is my Lord; there is no god but Him. Upon Him I rely, and to Him I turn in repentance.’”

Shuʿayb, peace be upon him, said:

“Upon Him I rely, and to Him I turn.”

And He has said:

“Whatever you differ over, its judgment rests with Allah. That is Allah, my Lord; upon Him I rely, and to Him I turn.”

And He has said:

“You have an excellent example in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people, ‘We are free of you and whatever you worship besides Allah. We have rejected you, and between us and you there has arisen enmity

---

## Page 9317

and hatred forever, until you believe in Allah alone’—except for Abraham’s words to his father, ‘I will surely seek forgiveness for you, but I have no power to protect you from Allah at all.’ ‘Our Lord, in You we have placed our trust, to You we turn in repentance, and to You is the final return.’”

Someone who performs an action he has been commanded to perform but neglects the reliance upon Allah that he has been commanded to observe is not more sinful than someone who observes the commanded reliance upon Allah but neglects the means he has been commanded to employ. Both fall short of some of their obligations. Although their sins are of the same kind, one may be more blameworthy in some cases, and the other in others. Moreover, reliance upon Allah is itself one of the means.

Abū Dāwūd narrated in his Sunan:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, adjudicated between two men. The man against whom he ruled said, ‘Allah is sufficient for me, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Allah censures inaction, but you must be resourceful. If a matter overcomes you, then say: “Allah is sufficient for me, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.”’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The strong believer is better and more beloved to Allah than the weak believer, though there is good in both. Strive for what benefits you, seek Allah’s help, and do not give up. If something befalls you, do not say, ‘If only I had done this, such and such would have happened.’ Rather, say, ‘Allah has decreed, and He has done what He willed,’ for ‘if only’ opens the way for Satan’s work.”

His words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “Strive for what benefits you, seek Allah’s help, and do not give up,” command the use of the prescribed means, namely, striving for what is beneficial. He also commanded, along with

---

## Page 9318

that, reliance upon Allah—that is, seeking Allah’s help. Whoever confines himself to either one has disobeyed one of the two commands. He also forbade inaction, which is the opposite of resourcefulness, as he said in the other hadith:

“Allah censures inaction, but you must be resourceful.”

Likewise, the Syrian hadith states:

“The resourceful person is one who calls himself to account and works for what comes after death. The ineffectual person is one who follows his desires and entertains vain hopes concerning Allah.”

In this hadith, the ineffectual person is contrasted with the resourceful person. Whoever says that the ineffectual person is contrasted with the righteous person has distorted the hadith and failed to understand its meaning. Another example is the hadith:

“Everything is by divine decree, even inaction and resourcefulness.”

Another example is what al-Bukhārī narrated in his Sahīh from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said:

“The people of Yemen used to perform Hajj without taking provisions, saying, ‘We are those who rely upon Allah.’ Yet when they arrived, they would beg from people. So Allah Most High said:

‘And take provisions, but indeed, the best provision is fear of Allah.’”

Whoever obeys the command to take provisions, uses them to help him obey Allah, and gives some of them to those in need is obeying Allah in both these matters. This is unlike someone who neglects to take provisions, looking instead to the pilgrims’ supplies and becoming a burden on others. Even if his heart is not looking to any particular person, it is nevertheless looking to people collectively. However, if the person who takes provisions fails to fulfil his obligations of relying upon Allah and sharing with those in need, then his neglect of what he has been commanded to do may be of the same kind as that of the person who neglects the command to take provisions.

---

## Page 9319

These texts expose the errors of several groups:

One group downplays the prescribed means, regarding their use as a deficiency in, or a compromise of, affirming Allah’s oneness and relying upon Him. They consider abandoning these means a perfection of both. In this they are confused, and their error may be compounded by following their desires as they settle into idleness. Thus, you find that most people of this kind, who abandon the means they have been commanded to use, become attached to inferior means.

Either they attach their hearts to created beings in hope and fear, or their excessive devotion to reliance upon Allah leads them to abandon obligatory or recommended deeds that would benefit them more. An example is someone who concentrates all his resolve, in his reliance upon Allah, on recovering from illness without medicine or obtaining his provision without effort. This may indeed happen, but taking a simple medicine, making a modest effort, and directing that resolve and devotion towards righteous action would benefit him more. Indeed, that might be a greater duty upon him than devoting himself exclusively to this minor matter, worth a dirham or so.

Further still are those who regard even reliance upon Allah and supplication as a deficiency and a separation from the spiritual elite, imagining that contemplating what has already been settled by divine decree is the spiritual state of the elite.

Yet He says in this hadith:

“All of you are hungry except those whom I feed, so ask Me for food, and I will feed you.”

He also says:

“Ask Me for clothing, and I will clothe you.”

In al-Tabarānī or another source, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Let each of you ask his Lord for everything he needs, even the strap of his sandal when it breaks, for unless He makes it easy, it will not be easy.”

Such a person may consequently also have to place seeking Allah’s guidance and acting in obedience to Him in the same category.

---

## Page 9320

And their position entails rejecting everything commanded—indeed, rejecting both what is created and what is commanded. Their error arose from supposing that prior divine ordainment precludes what is decreed from coming about through the prescribed means. This resembles someone who falls into heresy and abandons obligatory deeds on the grounds that divine decree has already determined who will attain bliss and who will suffer misery. He fails to understand that divine decree encompasses things as they actually are: for anyone whom Allah has destined for bliss, His decree includes making the deeds of those destined for bliss easy for him; and for anyone whom He has destined for misery, His decree includes making the deeds of those destined for misery easy for him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, answered this very question in the hadiths narrated by ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, ʿImrān ibn Husayn, Surāqah ibn Jaʿsham, and others.

Among these is the hadith reported by al-Tirmidhī:

Ibn Abī ʿUmar narrated to us: Sufyān narrated to us, from al-Zuhrī, from Abū Khizāmah, from his father, who said:

“I asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saying, ‘O Messenger of Allah, tell me about medicines with which we treat ourselves, healing recitations which we ask others to recite over us, and precautions we take to protect ourselves: do they avert anything of Allah’s decree?’

“He replied, ‘They are themselves part of Allah’s decree.’”

Another group thinks that reliance upon Allah is merely one of the spiritual stations of the elect who draw near to Allah through voluntary acts of worship. They say the same about the deeds of the heart and what follows from them, such as love, hope, fear, gratitude, and the like. This is manifest error. Rather, all these matters are individual obligations, by agreement of the people of faith. Whoever abandons them entirely

---

## Page 9321

is either a disbeliever or a hypocrite. Yet people differ in these matters just as they differ in outward deeds: some wrong themselves, some follow a middle course, and some are foremost in good deeds. The texts of the Quran and Sunnah abound with evidence of this. Those who turn away from these matters in both knowledge and practice are no less blameworthy than those who neglect the outward deeds they have been commanded to perform, despite practising some of these inward deeds. Rather, whoever neglects what is commanded, whether inward or outward, deserves blame and punishment—even though inward deeds are the starting point and foundation of outward deeds, while outward deeds are their completion and branches, without which the inward deeds remain incomplete.

Section

As for His words:

“O My servants, you sin by night and by day, and I forgive all sins.”

And in another narration:

“I forgive sins and do not mind doing so. So seek My forgiveness, and I will forgive you.”

Forgiveness encompassing all sins is of two kinds.

The first is forgiveness for those who repent, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“Say, ‘O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of Allah’s mercy’”

through to His words:

“then you will not be helped.”

This context, together with the occasion of the verse’s revelation, makes clear that no sinner should despair of Allah’s forgiveness, however great his sins may be, for Allah

---

## Page 9322

—glory be to Him—considers no sin too great to forgive His repentant servant.

This general statement includes associating partners with Allah and all other sins, for Allah Most High forgives them for whoever repents of them. He says:

“When the sacred months have passed, kill the polytheists”
