through to His words:

“But if they repent, establish prayer, and give zakah, then let them go their way.”

And in the other verse, He says:

“But if they repent, establish prayer, and give zakah, then they are your brothers in religion.”

He also says:

“They have certainly disbelieved who say, ‘Allah is the third of three’”

through to His words:

“Will they not repent to Allah and seek His forgiveness? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

This comprehensive position—that every sin is forgiven for whoever repents of it, as indicated by the Quran and hadith—is the correct view according to the vast majority of scholars. Some people, however, make exceptions for certain sins. Some, for example, maintain that the repentance of someone who calls others to religious innovations is not accepted inwardly, citing the report from Israelite tradition which contains the words:

“But what of those whom you led astray?”

This is mistaken. Allah has made clear in His Book and in the Sunnah of His Messenger that He accepts the repentance of the leaders of disbelief, who are far worse than the leaders of religious innovation.

Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who persecuted believing men and women and then did not repent will suffer the punishment of Hell and the punishment of the blazing Fire.”

Al-Hasan al-Basrī said: “Look at this generosity! They tortured His allies and persecuted them, yet He calls them to repentance.”

---

## Page 9323

The same applies to the repentance of a murderer and others like him. The hadith of Abū Sa‘īd, recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, about the man who killed ninety-nine people demonstrates that his repentance is accepted. Nothing in the Quran or Sunnah contradicts this. Nor do the texts threatening punishment for murder and other major sins contradict the texts affirming the acceptance of repentance. Thus, the verse in Sūrat al-Furqān is not abrogated by the verse in Sūrat al-Nisā’, for there is no contradiction between them. It is known with certainty that, whenever a sin carries a threat of punishment, the fulfilment of that threat is conditional upon the absence of repentance. The texts concerning repentance clarify those texts, including the threats concerning associating partners with Allah, consuming usury, consuming an orphan’s wealth, sorcery, and other sins.

As for those scholars who say, “His repentance is not accepted,” the interpretation of their statement consistent with the foundations of Islamic law is that repentance alone removes Allah’s right to punish, whereas the wronged person’s right is not extinguished merely by repentance. This is true, and there is no difference in this respect between a murderer and other wrongdoers. Whoever repents of a wrong does not thereby extinguish the wronged person’s right. Rather, completing his repentance requires compensating that person in proportion to the wrong inflicted. If he does not compensate him in this world, compensation must be made in the Hereafter. The repentant wrongdoer should therefore accumulate good deeds so that, once those he wronged have received their due, he is not left bankrupt.

Nevertheless, if Allah wills to compensate the wronged person from His own bounty, none can withhold His grace, just as He may will to forgive anything short of associating partners with Him for whomever He wills. Hence, in the hadith concerning retribution, for which Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh travelled to ‘Abd Allāh ibn

---

## Page 9324

Unays, travelling for a month until he heard it directly from him—a hadith narrated by Imam Ahmad and others, cited as supporting evidence by al-Bukhārī in his Sahīh, and comparable to al-Tirmidhī’s authentic or sound hadiths—it states:

“On the Day of Resurrection, Allah will gather all creatures on a single plain, where the caller will be heard by them all and they will all be within sight. Then He will call to them in a voice heard by those far away just as it is heard by those nearby: ‘I am the King; I am the Judge. No one destined for Paradise may enter Paradise while anyone destined for Hell has an outstanding claim against him. Nor may anyone destined for Hell enter Hell while anyone destined for Paradise has a claim against him, until I have exacted retribution from him.’”

The hadith thus explains the justice and retribution between the people of Paradise and the people of Hell. In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Sa‘īd narrates:

“When the people of Paradise have crossed the Bridge, they will stop on a bridge between Paradise and Hell, where their claims against one another will be settled. Once they have been refined and purified, they will be permitted to enter Paradise.”

After saying, “And do not backbite one another”—backbiting being a wrong against people’s honour—Allah, Glorified is He, says:

“Would any of you like to eat the flesh of his dead brother? You would detest it. And fear Allah; indeed, Allah is ever Accepting of repentance, Most Merciful.”

He thus calls their attention to repentance from backbiting, which is a form of wrongdoing. An authentic hadith states:

“Whoever has wronged his brother in his blood, property, or honour should go to him and seek his pardon before a day comes when there will be no dirham

---

## Page 9325

or dinar, but only good and evil deeds. If he has good deeds, [recompense will be taken from them]; otherwise, some of his companion’s evil deeds will be taken and laid upon him, and then he will be cast into the Fire”—or words to that effect.

This concerns compensation of which the wronged person is aware. But if someone backbites or slanders another without that person knowing, it has been said that informing him is a condition of repentance. It has also been said that this is not required; this is the majority view. Both positions are reported from Ahmad. His view in such a case, however, is that one should do good for the wronged person, such as supplicating for him, seeking forgiveness for him, and performing a righteous deed whose reward one dedicates to him, to make amends for backbiting and slandering him.

Al-Hasan al-Basrī said:

“The expiation for backbiting is to seek forgiveness for the person you have backbitten.”

As for sins concerning which the jurists state without qualification that repentance is not accepted, examples include the majority’s statement, “The repentance of a zindīq—that is, a hypocrite—is not accepted”; their statement that if a muhārib (one who wages war) repents before he is apprehended, the prescribed punishments pertaining to Allah’s rights are waived; and the similar position held by many or most of them concerning other crimes. This is one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two positions and the more correct of the two reports from Ahmad. They also say concerning such offenders, “If they repent after their cases have been referred to the ruler, their repentance is not accepted.”

By this they mean only the lifting of the punishment prescribed for them. That is, their repentance is not accepted in the sense of allowing the offender to go free without punishment. Rather, he is punished, either because the sincerity of his repentance is unknown and he is suspected of lying about it, or because lifting the punishment on that basis would lead to the violation of sacred prohibitions and prevent crimes from being punished. They do not mean that if any of these people sincerely repents, Allah will not accept his repentance inwardly; for

---

## Page 9326

no leading jurist holds such a view. Rather, repentance is refused only once a person directly witnesses the realities of the Hereafter, as Allah Most High says:

“Allah only accepts the repentance of those who do evil in ignorance and then repent soon afterward. It is they whose repentance Allah accepts, and Allah is ever All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

“Repentance is not accepted from those who continue doing evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says, ‘Now I repent,’ nor from those who die as disbelievers”—to the end of the verse.

Abū al-ʿĀliyah said:

“I asked the Companions of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about this. They told me: ‘Everyone who disobeys Allah is ignorant, and everyone who repents before death has repented soon afterward.’”

As for someone who repents when death is before his very eyes, he is like Pharaoh, who said, “I am Allah”:

“Until, when drowning overtook him, he said, ‘I believe that there is no god except Him in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am one of the Muslims.’”

Allah said:

“Now, when you had previously disobeyed and were one of those who spread corruption?”

This is a question expressing disapproval, through which He made clear that such repentance is not the accepted repentance that He has commanded. A question expressing disapproval conveys either negation, when it concerns declarative speech, or censure and prohibition, when it concerns performative speech. This instance belongs to the latter category. Similar is His saying, Most High:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed, but they were engulfed by what they used to mock.”

“When they saw Our punishment, they said, ‘We believe in Allah alone, and we reject what we used to associate with Him.’”

“But their faith did not benefit them when

---

## Page 9327

they saw Our punishment”—to the end of the verse. He explained that repentance after witnessing the punishment is of no benefit, and that this is Allah’s established way with His servants, such as Pharaoh and others. The hadith states:

“Allah accepts His servant’s repentance so long as the death rattle has not begun.”

Another narration reads:

“So long as he has not directly witnessed the realities of the Hereafter.”

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called his uncle to affirm Allah’s oneness during the illness from which he died. He also visited a Jewish man who used to serve him when he was ill and invited him to embrace Islam. The man accepted Islam, whereupon he said, “Praise be to Allah, who saved him from the Fire through me.” He then said to his Companions, “Take your brother into your care.”

Further evidence that the comprehensive forgiveness mentioned in Sūrat al-Zumar is for those who repent is His statement in Sūrat al-Nisā’:

“Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating partners with Him, but He forgives anything less than that for whomever He wills.”

Here He restricted forgiveness to sins less than associating partners with Him and made it dependent upon His will, whereas there He left it unrestricted and all-encompassing. This restriction and qualification indicate that this passage concerns someone who has not repented. Accordingly, Ahl al-Sunnah cite this verse as evidence that those guilty of major sins may, in principle, be forgiven, contrary to the Khawārij and Mu‘tazilah, who maintain that the threatened punishment must be carried out against them.

Nevertheless, among those who oppose them, a faction of the Murji’ah went to excess, to the point of withholding judgment on whether the threatened punishment would befall anyone who faces the qiblah in prayer. Their extremists are even reported to have denied this altogether. Allah’s religion is a middle course between those who go to excess in it and those who fall short of it. The texts of the Quran and Sunnah, together with the consensus of the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars, all affirm that among those guilty of major sins

---

## Page 9328

are some who will be punished, and that no one with an atom’s weight of faith in his heart will remain in the Fire.

The Second Type

The second type of comprehensive forgiveness indicated by His words,

“O My servants, you sin by night and by day, and I forgive all sins,”

is forgiveness in the sense of lightening the punishment or postponing it until an appointed time. This applies universally, without restriction. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, interceded for Abū Tālib despite his having died while associating partners with Allah. He was moved from the depths of the Fire to a shallow part of it, with two sandals of fire on his feet that cause his brain to boil. He said:

“Were it not for me, he would have been in the lowest depth of the Fire.”

This meaning is also indicated by His words, glorified is He:

“If Allah were to take people to task for what they have earned, He would not leave a single living creature on its surface.”

“If Allah were to take people to task for their wrongdoing, He would not leave a single living creature upon it.”

“Whatever calamity befalls you is because of what your own hands have earned, and He pardons much.”

Section

As for His words, mighty and majestic is He:

“O My servants, you can never harm Me, nor can you ever benefit Me,”

He thereby makes clear that, in showing kindness to them by answering their supplications and forgiving their missteps, He does not seek recompense

---

## Page 9329

from them in the form of gaining some benefit or averting some harm, as is customary with a created being who benefits another so that the latter will repay him with some benefit, or wards off harm from another so as to protect himself against that person’s harm. Thus He said:

“You can never benefit Me, nor can you ever harm Me.”

Thus, when I favour you by guiding those who seek guidance and providing for those who seek provision, food, and clothing, I am not seeking any benefit from you. Nor, when I forgive your sins committed by night and by day, am I thereby protecting Myself against your harming Me. For you can never benefit Me, nor can you ever harm Me.

They are incapable of that. Indeed, even the actions within their power are possible only through His decree and governance—so what of those beyond their power? And how could they affect the Self-Sufficient One, upon whom all depend, who cannot possibly receive benefit or harm from anyone else?

Just as this statement makes clear that they can never do for Him what He does for them in bringing benefits and averting harms, it also implies that His commands to perform acts of obedience and His prohibitions against evil deeds do not entail seeking any benefit from them, as when a master commands his slave, a parent his child, or a ruler his subjects, and so forth. Nor do they entail warding off harm from them, as when these people or others forbid someone to harm them.

Created beings can benefit and harm one another, and their commands and prohibitions may serve such purposes. The Creator, glorified is He, is far exalted above that. He therefore makes clear that He is beyond receiving any benefit or harm from them in the kindness He shows them through

---

## Page 9330

His actions toward them and His commands to them. Qatādah said:

“Allah did not command His servants to do what He commanded because He needed them, nor did He forbid them what He forbade out of reluctance to share it with them. Rather, He commanded them to do what would set their affairs right and forbade them what would corrupt them.”

Section

For this reason, He went on to mention these two principles: their righteousness and wickedness—that is, their obedience and disobedience—neither increase nor diminish His dominion; and His granting them the utmost of what they ask bears the smallest proportion to what He possesses. This is unlike kings and others whose dominion increases through their subjects’ obedience and diminishes through their disobedience. If such a ruler gave people what they asked for, he would exhaust what he possessed without satisfying their needs. In all this, they can harm and benefit him, and whatever kindness he shows, pardon he grants, commands he issues, and prohibitions he imposes are motivated by hope of benefit and fear of harm.

Thus He said:

“O My servants, if the first of you and the last of you, the humans among you and the jinn among you, were all as pious in heart as the most pious man among you, that would not increase My dominion in the least. O My servants, if the first of you and the last of you, the humans among you and the jinn among you, were all as wicked in heart as the most wicked man among you, that would not diminish My dominion in the least.”

For His dominion is His power to dispose of affairs. It neither increases through their obedience nor diminishes through their disobedience, unlike the power of kings, which increases when those who obey them are numerous and diminishes when they are few; for His dominion depends

---

## Page 9331

on Himself. He is the Creator, Lord, and Sovereign of everything, and He grants dominion to whomever He wills and takes it away from whomever He wills. Dominion may mean the power to govern and dispose of affairs, the actual governing and disposing of affairs, the possessions themselves that are subject to governance, or all of these together. In any case, neither the righteousness of the righteous nor the wickedness of the wicked causes any of these to increase or diminish. Rather, by His will and power, He creates whatever He wills. Were He to wish to create whatever He willed despite the wickedness of the wicked, nothing could prevent Him, whereas the wickedness of subjects who oppose their kings’ commands prevents those kings from doing what they choose in this regard. Were He to wish not to create any of the things He has created despite the righteousness of the righteous, their righteousness would neither necessitate His creating them nor assist Him in doing so, unlike kings, who need and seek the assistance of large numbers of obedient subjects.

Section

He then described their condition in the two matters—asking for His goodness and obeying His commands—which He mentioned in the hadith when He spoke of seeking guidance, food, and clothing, and mentioned forgiveness, righteousness, and wickedness. He said:

“If the first of you and the last of you, the humans among you and the jinn among you, were all gathered in one place and asked Me, and I gave each person what he asked for,

---

## Page 9332

that would diminish what I have no more than a needle does when dipped into the sea.”

The words khayyāt and makhīt denote an implement used for sewing, since fa‘‘āl, muf‘il, and mif‘āl are patterns used for the names of instruments with which actions are performed, such as a fire poker, a mikhlāb, and a saw. He thus explained that if all creatures were to ask Him in one place and at one time, and each person were given what he asked for, that would diminish what He has no more than “a khayyāt”—that is, a needle—“does when dipped into the sea.”

His words, “would not diminish what I have,” admit two interpretations. The first is that they indicate that He has existing things from which He gives them what they ask Him for. On this interpretation, it may be said that the word “diminish” retains its ordinary meaning, since giving from an abundant supply, however little is given, must diminish it to some extent.

The version narrated as “would not diminish My dominion” is understood as referring to what He has, as expressed in the present wording. For “what I have” specifies something that “My dominion” does not.

It may, however, be said that what is given consists either of entities subsisting independently or of attributes subsisting in something else. Entities may be transferred from one place to another, so that a diminution becomes apparent in the first place. Attributes, however, are not transferred from the subject in which they subsist, even when their like comes to exist in another subject. Thus, knowledge like that of the teacher comes to exist in the learner’s heart without the teacher’s knowledge ceasing to exist. Likewise, a speaker utters the words of an earlier speaker without the first speaker’s words being transferred to

---

## Page 9333

the second speaker. On this basis, attributes do not diminish what He has at all, and they are among the things asked for, as in the case of guidance.

One possible response is that, for some attributes, their like may not come to exist in a second subject until they cease to exist in the first: a colour that fades, for example, or scents that cling to a place and then disappear. Similarly, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prayed that the fever of Medina be transferred to Mahyaʿah, which is al-Juhfah. Does such a transfer involve the original accident itself being transferred, or does its like come into existence without the original itself being transferred?

There are two views on this. Some allow that accidents can be transferred; indeed, some even allow that accidents can be made into independently subsisting entities. This is the position of Dirār, al-Najjār, and their followers, such as Burghūth and Hafs al-Fard. But if it is said that such a transfer occurs through the coming into existence of its like, without the original itself being transferred, then this occurs alongside the transformation and extinction of the original accident: it ceases to exist in that subject, and its like comes into existence in the second subject.

The second interpretation is that the word “diminish” here is used as it is in the hadith of Moses and al-Khidr, recorded in the two Sahīh collections, narrated by Ibn ʿAbbās from Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. It includes the following:

“When a sparrow alighted on the ship’s boat and dipped its beak into the sea, al-Khidr said to Moses: ‘O Moses, my knowledge and yours have diminished Allah’s knowledge no more than this sparrow has diminished this sea.’”

It is well known that Allah’s own knowledge, which subsists in Him, does not lose

---

## Page 9334

anything through His servants’ learning. Rather, what is meant is that my knowledge and yours stand in relation to Allah’s knowledge as the water clinging to the sparrow’s beak stands in relation to the sea.

The inheritance of knowledge belongs to this same category, as in the saying:

“Scholars are the heirs of the prophets.”

Likewise, His saying:

“Solomon inherited from David.”

The inheritance of the Book is another example, as in His saying:

“Then We gave the Book as an inheritance to those We chose from among Our servants.”

Expressions such as “diminishing” and the like are used in such cases, even though the original knowledge remains intact.

For example, Qatādah stayed with Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib for a week, asking him important questions, until Saʿīd marvelled at his memory and said to him: “You have drained me, blind man!” Draining a well or the like means drawing out its contents until nothing remains.

It is well known that even if Qatādah had learned everything Saʿīd knew, Saʿīd’s knowledge would not have disappeared from his heart as water disappears from a well. But it may be said that teaching takes place through speech, and speech requires movement and other things that arise in the subject and pass away from it. Hence speech is described as issuing from the speaker, as the Most High says:

“Grave is the word that comes out of their mouths; they speak nothing but a lie.”

It is also said: “The scholar has transmitted this hadith, but has not transmitted that one.” Thus, if teaching knowledge takes place through speech that necessarily entails the passing away of some of what subsists in the subject—and this constitutes a draining and an issuing forth—then Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib’s words would bear their literal meaning. Their import is that, during those seven nights, owing to his many answers and his speaking to Qatādah, certain things that had subsisted in him departed from him, namely movements and sounds;

---

## Page 9335

indeed, even qualities subsisting in the soul—so this would constitute a draining. What reinforces this interpretation is that, although a person’s knowledge resides within his soul, it does not adhere inseparably to the soul as colours adhere to coloured objects. Rather, a person may become distracted from it or heedless of it, and may forget it and then remember it. Thus, it is something that is present at one time and absent at another.

When a person speaks of it and knows it, his soul may grow tired and weary, so that he cannot bring it to mind again until some time has passed. In that state, the soul lacks the full realization and conscious presence of that knowledge by which one is a knower in actuality, even though what has departed is not itself the very thing subsisting in the soul of the questioner and listener. Whoever adopts this view says: the fact that teaching consolidates knowledge in one respect does not contradict what we have mentioned.

If such diminution and draining are conceivable in the knowledge of servants, then using the term “diminution” in relation to Allah’s knowledge follows customary linguistic usage in such cases. Nevertheless, He—glorified is He—is far exalted above being characterized by the opposite of knowledge in any respect, or above His knowledge departing from Him. Whether acts and movements subsist in Him, however, is a matter of dispute among people, Muslims and others.

The precise meaning is this: what my knowledge and yours have taken from Allah’s knowledge, what my knowledge and yours have attained of Allah’s knowledge, and what my knowledge and yours have encompassed of Allah’s knowledge—as He says:

“They encompass nothing of His knowledge except what He wills”—

is only comparable to what this sparrow has removed, taken, or obtained from this sea. That is, the proportion of the former to the latter is like the proportion of the one to the other, even though that with which the comparison is made is a body that moves from one place to another and departs

---

## Page 9336

from its original location, whereas the thing being compared is not like that. This distinction is obvious to the listener and admits of no confusion, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“You will indeed see your Lord just as you see the sun and the moon.”

He compared one act of seeing to another. Although seeing concerns an object seen in both the case being compared and the case used to illustrate it, the listeners knew that the two objects seen were not alike. Likewise, here he compared one diminution to another, even though the respective agents of diminution, the amounts removed, and the sources from which they are removed are not alike in the cases compared.

Thus, everyone knows that a teacher does not lose his knowledge through teaching. Rather, they liken it to the light a lamp produces: anyone may kindle a flame from it, and people may take as many flames as they wish, while it remains unchanged. This is an apt analogy. When someone lights something from a lamp, Allah brings into being in his wick or fuel a fire of the same kind as the original fire—although it may be said that it arises through the transformation of that air—while the original fire remains. Likewise, the learner acquires in his heart knowledge like the teacher’s knowledge, while the teacher’s knowledge remains. Hence, ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “Knowledge grows through practice—or he said, through teaching—whereas spending diminishes wealth.”

On this basis, it may be said that, in the hadith of Abū Dharr, His words “of what I possess” and “of My dominion” belong to this same category. There are then two possible interpretations:

The first is that what He has given them falls outside what is designated by His dominion and what is designated by what

---

## Page 9337

He possesses, just as the knowledge of Moses and al-Khidr is not itself included within Allah’s knowledge.

The second interpretation is that the expressions “dominion” and “what He possesses” encompass everything. What He has given them is therefore part of His dominion and of what He possesses, but its proportion to the whole is this insignificant proportion.

Supporting this second interpretation is the fact that al-Tirmidhī narrated this hadith through ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Ghunm, from Abū Dharr, tracing it back to the Prophet. It includes the following:

“If the first and last of you, the humans and jinn among you, the moist and the dry among you, were to ask Me until each had exhausted all his requests, and I granted them what they asked, that would diminish what I possess no more than a needle dipped into the sea by one of you. That is because I am Generous, Glorious, and Rich. My giving is speech, and My punishment is speech. Whenever I will something, My command to it is only to say, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

His statement, glorified be He, that His giving is speech and His punishment is speech indicates that by “of My dominion” and “of what I possess,” He meant “of what lies within My power.” Thus, this concerns power just as the hadith of al-Khidr concerns knowledge. And Allah knows best.

This is further supported by the other wording found in Abū Mushir’s copy:

“That would diminish My dominion no more than the sea is diminished.”

This may be understood as a disconnected exception, meaning: “It would not diminish My dominion at all; rather, its relation to it would be like this proportion.” Alternatively, it may be understood as a complete exception, with the meaning explained above.

---

## Page 9338

Section

He then concluded the hadith by affirming the justice and kindness He had explained within it, saying:

“My servants, it is only your deeds that I record for you, then recompense you for in full. Whoever finds good, let him praise Allah; whoever finds otherwise, let him blame no one but himself.”

He thus made clear that, in rewarding His servants for their righteous deeds, He shows them kindness for which He deserves praise. For He is the One who favors them by commanding these deeds, guiding them to them, and helping them perform them, then recording them and granting their reward in full. All of this is bounty and kindness from Him, for every blessing from Him is bounty, and every punishment from Him is justice.

Although He has prescribed mercy for Himself, and helping the believers is incumbent upon Him—as explained earlier—this obligation is not like the obligations people owe one another, whose fulfillment constitutes justice rather than bounty. Such obligations arise because one person has shown kindness to another and thereby become entitled to recompense; that kindness was accomplished through the benefactor’s power, not the recipient’s. Thus, when two parties exchange equivalent benefits, neither can be singled out as conferring favor upon the other, since they are on an equal footing.

He has explained in the hadith that His servants will never be able to harm Him, nor will they ever be able to benefit Him. It is therefore impossible for anyone to have a right over Him in his own right. Rather, He Himself has made that right binding upon Himself through His words. He is thus beneficent both in bestowing His bounty and in making it binding

---

## Page 9339

and in prescribing it for Himself. Thus, in prescribing mercy for Himself, making the support of His believing servants incumbent upon Himself, and other such acts, He bestows kindness upon kindness.

Let the discerning reader reflect on these distinctions, which make clear the decisive answer in matters that have occasioned such great confusion. Some impose obligations upon their Lord in a way that denies His kindness and bounty; others equate His justice and kindness with the injustice and aggression from which He is utterly free, treating them all as one category. All of this is a departure from the straight path. Allah speaks the truth and guides to the way.

Just as He has explained that He shows kindness in granting good deeds, completing His kindness by recording them and rewarding them, He has also explained that He is just in recompensing evil deeds. He said:

“Whoever finds otherwise, let him blame no one but himself.”

This has already been explained in connection with such statements of His as:

“We did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves.”

Upon this principle rests the revealed Law, which accords with the natural disposition upon which Allah created mankind. Thus, in the authentic hadith narrated by al-Bukhārī from Shaddād ibn Aws, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The foremost supplication for forgiveness is for a servant to say: ‘O Allah, You are my Lord; none has the right to be worshipped but You. You created me, and I am Your servant. I abide by Your covenant and promise to the best of my ability. I seek refuge in You from the evil of what I have done. I acknowledge before You Your blessings upon me, and I acknowledge my sin. So forgive me, for none forgives sins but You.’”

His words, “I acknowledge before You Your blessings upon me,” acknowledge Allah’s favor upon him in his good deeds and in other matters. And his words, “I acknowledge my sin,”

---

## Page 9340

are an acknowledgment that he is a sinner who has wronged himself. In this way, the servant becomes grateful to his Lord and seeks forgiveness for his sin, thereby deserving further goodness and forgiveness of evil from the Ever-Appreciative, the All-Forgiving, who rewards even a little good work and forgives many lapses.

Here people fall into three categories regarding how they attribute good and evil deeds—acts of obedience and disobedience—to their Lord and to themselves. The worst are those who, when they do wrong, attribute it to divine decree, excusing themselves on the grounds that it had already been decreed and that they cannot escape the decree. Thus they invoke an argument against their Lord to justify wronging themselves. Yet when they do good, they attribute it to themselves, forgetting Allah’s favor in easing their way to goodness.

This is not the doctrine of any particular group among mankind. Rather, it is the condition of the worst of the ignorant wrongdoers, who neither observe the bounds of command and prohibition nor recognize the reality of divine determination and decree. As Shaykh Abū al-Faraj Ibn al-Jawzī said of them:

“When you obey, you are a Qadarī; when you sin, you are a Jabrī. Whichever doctrine suits your desires, you adopt it.”

The best category—the one prescribed by the revealed Law, embodying the truth it has brought—is that of a person who, when he does good, thanks Allah for His favor and praises Him for having blessed him by making him one who does good rather than one who does evil.

For he is inherently poor and dependent upon his Lord in his very being, his attributes, and every movement and moment of stillness. He has neither power nor strength except through Him. Had Allah not guided him, he would not have been guided, as the people of Paradise say:

“All praise is for Allah, who has guided us to this. We would never have been guided had Allah not guided us. The messengers of our Lord certainly came with the truth.”

And when

---

## Page 9341

he does wrong, he acknowledges his sin, seeks his Lord’s forgiveness, and repents of it. He follows the example of his forefather Adam, who said:

“Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy on us, we will surely be among the losers.”

He does not follow the example of Iblīs, who said:

“My Lord, because You have led me astray, I will surely make evil alluring to them on earth and mislead them all, except Your chosen servants among them.”

He does not invoke divine decree to justify neglecting a command or committing a prohibited act, despite believing in divine decree, both its good and its evil; that Allah is the Creator, Lord, and Sovereign of everything; that whatever Allah wills happens, and whatever He does not will does not happen; that He guides whom He wills and leads astray whom He wills; and other such truths.

These are the people who obey Allah’s words in this authentic hadith:

“Whoever finds good, let him praise Allah; and whoever finds otherwise, let him blame no one but himself.”

However, a full exposition of this, and a precise explanation of how sin is attributed to oneself while knowing that Allah creates His servants’ actions, involve subtleties that this is not the place to discuss.

Nevertheless, in the words of the Most High:

“If something good befalls them, they say, ‘This is from Allah.’ But if something bad befalls them, they say, ‘This is from you.’ Say, ‘All is from Allah.’ What is the matter with these people that they can hardly understand any statement?”

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

The good and evil mentioned in this verse do not mean acts of obedience and disobedience, as many people suppose. Some go so far as to distort the Quran and read, “Then who are you?” Clearly, the meaning of this reading contradicts the mass-transmitted reading. Others even posit an implicit “they say,” treating the statement as something to be rejected, although it is the statement of

---

## Page 9342

Allah, the Truth. Thus, through the baseless ellipsis he posits, despite nothing in the context indicating it, he turns Allah’s truthful words, which He commends and approves, into a statement by disbelievers which He declares false, condemns, and abhors.

These people’s ignorance is also evident in their supposition that this verse supports the Qadariyyah, and in some Qadariyyah’s use of it as evidence. For there is no disagreement that acts of obedience and disobedience stand on the same footing with respect to divine decree.

Whoever says that the servant, rather than Allah, brings his own actions into existence, or that he creates his own actions and Allah has not created His servants’ actions, makes no distinction in this respect between obedience and disobedience. Likewise, whoever affirms that actions are created—whether he affirms compulsion, denies it, or refrains from either affirming or denying it categorically, and whether he clarifies its meaning or not—makes no distinction in this respect between obedience and disobedience.

It is therefore clear that bringing this verse into the debate over divine decree displays the utmost ignorance. The good and evil mentioned in the verse mean favourable and adverse circumstances, not acts of obedience and disobedience, as in the words of the Most High:

“We tested them with good times and bad, so that they might return.”

These are the evil and good mentioned in His words:

“We test you with evil and good as a trial.”

Likewise, He says:

“If something good befalls you, it grieves them; but if misfortune befalls you, they rejoice in it.”

And the Most High says:

“And if We let him taste prosperity after adversity has touched him, he will surely say, ‘My troubles have gone away.’”

And the Most High says:

“And We never sent to

---

## Page 9343

any town a prophet without afflicting its people with hardship and suffering, so that they might humble themselves.”

“Then We replaced adversity with prosperity until they flourished and said, ‘Our forefathers too experienced adversity and ease.’ So We seized them suddenly, while they were unaware.”

And the Most High says:

“When good came their way, they said, ‘This is ours.’ But when misfortune befell them, they regarded Moses and those with him as ill omens.”

This was the attitude of Pharaoh and his chiefs towards Moses and those with him, just like the attitude of the disbelievers, hypocrites, and wrongdoers towards Muhammad and his Companions. When blessings and good fortune came their way, they said, “This is ours,” or, “This is from Allah.” But when punishment and misfortune befell them, they regarded the Prophet and the believers as ill omens and said, “This is because of their sins.” Yet these things befell them only because of their own sins, not the sins of the believers.

He, glorified is He, mentions this when describing those who shrink from jihad and blame the believers for engaging in it. When victory or something similar comes their way, they say, “This is from Allah.” But when an ordeal befalls them, they say, “This is from the one who brought us commands, prohibitions, and jihad.” Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, take your precautions,”

up to His words:

“And among you are certainly those who hang back,”

and then:

“Have you not seen those who were told, ‘Restrain your hands, establish prayer, and give zakah’? Then, when fighting was prescribed for them, a group of them feared people as they feared Allah, or even more, and said, ‘Our Lord, why have You prescribed fighting for us?’”

up to His words:

“Wherever you may be, death will overtake you, even if you are in lofty fortified towers. If something good befalls them”—

meaning these censured people—

“they say, ‘This is from Allah’; but if misfortune befalls them, they say, ‘This is from you.’”

That is, because of what you command and forbid.

---

## Page 9344

Allah Most High says:

“So what is the matter with these people that they can hardly understand any statement?”

“Whatever good befalls you”—that is, any blessing—“is from Allah, and whatever misfortune befalls you is from yourself”—that is, because of your sin.

As He says:

“Whatever misfortune befalls you is because of what your own hands have earned.”

And He says:

“And if misfortune befalls them because of what their hands have sent forth.”

As for the third category in this matter, they are people who mix truth with falsehood, standing between the people of faith and goodness and the worst of people. They engage in false speculation about divine decree. Some hold that they themselves guide themselves and lead themselves astray, and bring about their own acts of obedience and disobedience, without any assistance from Him or His granting them success in obedience, and without His withholding His help from them in disobedience. Others deny that they have any action, ability, or say in the matter.

Some of the latter cast off the commands and prohibitions altogether, thus becoming the most disbelieving of all creatures. Yet they contradict themselves in invoking divine decree as an excuse, for there must be actions they love and actions they hate. They, like everyone else, must ward off the harm caused by the actions of aggressors. If they treat good and evil deeds as equal, they cannot censure anyone, repel an oppressor, or respond to a wrongdoer—even if they allow others to do to them whatever anyone desires, and other such things under which life cannot be sustained

---

## Page 9345

by the children of Adam; for their need for a law containing commands and prohibitions is greater than their need for food and clothing. This is a broad subject whose detailed explanation belongs elsewhere.

We have merely drawn attention to the comprehensive statements and beneficial principles contained in the hadith through brief observations that alert the discerning reader to what these realities share and what distinguishes them—distinctions that separate truth from falsehood in these difficult matters—within the space afforded by the questioner’s sheets.

May Allah benefit us and all our believing brethren through what we have learned, teach us what will benefit us, and increase us in knowledge. There is no power or strength except through Allah, and no refuge from Him except with Him. To Him belong all blessings and bounty, and to Him belongs all worthy praise. I ask Allah the Most Great to forgive me and all our believing brethren.

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless Muhammad and his family and grant them abundant peace.

---

## Page 9346

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none is worthy of worship except Allah alone, without partner. And we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and grant him abundant peace.

Section

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī and elsewhere, it is narrated from ʿImrān ibn Husayn—may Allah be pleased with him—that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“‘O Banū Tamīm, accept the glad tidings.’ They replied, ‘You have given us glad tidings; now give us something.’ He then turned to the people of Yemen and said, ‘O people of

---

## Page 9347

Yemen, accept the glad tidings, since Banū Tamīm have not accepted them.’ They replied, ‘We have accepted them, O Messenger of Allah.’ They said, ‘We have come to you to gain understanding of the religion and to ask you about the beginning of this affair.’ He replied, ‘Allah existed, and there was nothing before Him’—in another wording, ‘with Him,’ and in another, ‘other than Him’—‘and His Throne was upon the water. He wrote everything in the Record and created the heavens and the earth.’” In another wording: “Then He created the heavens and the earth.”

“Then a man came to me and said, ‘Go after your she-camel!’ I went, and there she was, beyond where the mirage ended. By Allah, I wished I had left her and not got up.”

His words, “He wrote in the Record,” refer to the Preserved Tablet, as Allah says:

“And We have already written in the Psalms, after the Record.”

That is, after the Preserved Tablet. What is written in the Record is called a record, just as that in which something is written is called a book, as in His words, Almighty and Majestic:

“Indeed, it is a noble Quran, in a well-guarded Book.”

There are two views concerning this hadith. Some say that its purpose is to explain that Allah existed alone and then began bringing all originated things into existence; that originated things, both as a whole and in their individual instances, have a beginning and are preceded by nonexistence; that time as a whole originated, though not within time; that motion and moving things, as entire categories, originated; and that Allah became an agent after having done nothing from eternity until the moment He began to act, nor had action even been possible.

Those who hold this view are themselves divided into two groups. Some say that He likewise began to speak after

---

## Page 9348

having never spoken at all; indeed, speech had not even been possible for Him.

Others say that speech is ascribed to Him in the sense that He has power over it, not that He speaks by His will and power; rather, it is inseparable from His essence, independently of His power and will. Among these, some say that it is the meaning, not the recited wording, expressed in each of the Torah, the Gospel, the Psalms, and the Criterion. Others say that it consists instead of letters and sounds inseparable from His essence, which have always existed and will always exist, and of all the words of the Books He revealed, and other things besides.

The second view concerning the meaning of the hadith is that this was not what the Messenger intended; indeed, the hadith contradicts it. Rather, his purpose was to tell of the creation of this observable world, which Allah created in six days, then rose over the Throne, as the glorious Quran relates in several places. Allah Most High says:

“It is He who created the heavens and the earth in six days, and His Throne was upon the water.”

It is also authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah decreed the destinies of all creatures fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water.”

Thus he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told of the decree concerning the creation of this world, which was created in six days, and that His Throne was then upon the water. This is likewise related in the Quran and in the preceding hadith, which al-Bukhārī narrated in his Sahīh from ʿImrān, may Allah be pleased with him.

---

## Page 9349

Another instance of this is the hadith narrated by Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī and others from ʿUbādah ibn al-Sāmit, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“The first thing Allah created was the Pen. He said to it, ‘Write.’ It asked, ‘What shall I write?’ He said, ‘Whatever will happen until the Day of Resurrection.’”

He created this Pen when He commanded it to record the decree in writing, fifty thousand years before the creation of the heavens and the earth. It was created before the heavens and the earth and was the first thing created belonging to this world. He created it after the Throne, as the scriptural texts indicate. This is the view of the majority of the early generations, whose statements I have cited elsewhere.

The purpose here is to explain what the texts of the Quran and Sunnah indicate. There are several grounds supporting this second view.

The first is that, in the words of the people of Yemen, “We have come to ask you about the beginning of this matter,” the matter referred to must mean either this world or created things in general. If the former was intended, then the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, answered them, because he told them about the beginning of Allah’s creation of this world. If the latter was intended, then he did not answer them, because he did not mention the beginning of creation in an absolute sense. Rather, he said:

“Allah existed, and nothing preceded Him. His Throne was upon the water. He wrote everything in the Record, then created the heavens and the earth.”

Thus, he mentioned only the creation of the heavens and the earth

---

## Page 9350

and did not mention the creation of the Throne, even though the Throne too is created. For Allah says:

“He is the Lord of the Mighty Throne.”

He is the Creator of everything—the Throne and all else—and the Lord of everything—the Throne and all else. In the hadith of Abū Razīn, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did speak of the creation of the Throne. In the hadith of ʿImrān, however, he did not speak of its creation; rather, he spoke of the creation of the heavens and the earth. It is therefore clear that he described the beginning of the creation of this world, not the beginning of creation in an absolute sense.

Since this alone was his answer, it is clear that this alone was what they asked him about; they did not ask him about the beginning of creation in an absolute sense. It cannot be that he answered a question they had not asked while leaving their actual question unanswered. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is far above that. Moreover, his wording indicates only this. It does not indicate that he mentioned the very beginning of creation and intended, by relating the creation of the heavens and the earth after His Throne had been upon the water, merely to explain the order of some created things in relation to others. They did not ask him merely about their order; rather, they asked him about the beginning of this matter. It is therefore clear that they asked him about the beginning of the creation of this world, and he told them about it, as stated at its beginning: in the beginning, “Allah created the heavens and the earth.”

Some explain this as: In the beginning—or at the outset—Allah created the heavens and the earth. The point is that it describes the beginning of the creation of the heavens and the earth, that water covered the earth, and that the wind was blowing over the water. It thus states that

---

## Page 9351

at that time this world consisted of water, air, and earth. He tells us in the Glorious Quran that He created the heavens and the earth in six days, and that His Throne was upon the water. In another verse, He says:

“Then He turned to the heaven while it was smoke and said to it and to the earth, ‘Come willingly or unwillingly.’ They said, ‘We come willingly.’”

Reports from the early generations state that the heaven was created from water vapour, which is the smoke mentioned here.

The point here is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, answered what they had asked him about, mentioning only the beginning of the creation of the heavens and the earth. This indicates that when they said, “We have come to ask you about the beginning of this matter,” they meant the creation of this world. And Allah knows best.

The second point: Their words “this matter” refer to something present and existing. The word amr (“command” or “matter”) may denote the act of commanding, or its object—that which Allah brought into being by His command. The latter is what they meant. For His saying “Be” is not something they could observe and point to; rather, what they could observe and point to was that which had been commanded into being. Allah Most High says:

“Allah’s command is a decree firmly determined.”

And He Most High says:

“Allah’s command has come.”

There are numerous similar examples. Had they asked him about the very beginning of creation, they would not have referred to it as “this,” for they had not witnessed it and so could not point to it as “this.” Indeed, they did not even know of it, since it can be known only through the reports of the prophets. The Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had not told them about it; had he already told them, they would not have asked him about it. Thus it is clear that they were asking

---

## Page 9352

about the beginning of this observable world.

The third point: He said:

“Allah existed, and nothing existed before Him.”

The wording “with Him” has also been transmitted, as has “other than Him.” All three wordings occur in al-Bukhārī. Yet there was only one gathering, and their question and his answer took place at that gathering. Moreover, ʿImrān, who narrated the hadith, did not leave when the gathering ended; rather, he left before it ended, upon being told that his riding animal had wandered off. He is the one who reported the Messenger’s words. This indicates that the Messenger uttered only one of these wordings, while the other two were transmitted in paraphrase.

Accordingly, the wording established from him is “before.” For it is established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say in his supplication:

“You are the First, and nothing is before You; You are the Last, and nothing is after You; You are the Manifest, and nothing is above You; You are the Hidden, and nothing is nearer than You.”

This accords with, and explains, His saying, Most High:

“He is the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden.”

Since the wording “before” is established in this hadith, it is established that the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said it. Neither of the other two wordings, however, has ever been established. Most hadith scholars—including al-Humaydī, al-Baghawī, Ibn al-Athīr, and others—transmitted it only with the wording “before”:

“Allah existed, and nothing was before Him.”

If, then, he said only, “Allah existed, and nothing existed before Him,” this wording addresses neither the beginning of things that come into existence nor the first thing created.

---

## Page 9353

The fourth point: He said in it:

“Allah existed, and nothing existed before Him—or with Him, or other than Him—and His Throne was upon the water, and He wrote everything in the Record.”

He thus related these three matters using the conjunction “and,” without using “then” in any of them. “Then” occurs only in his statement, “He created the heavens and the earth.” Some narrators related the creation of the heavens and the earth using “then,” while others used “and.” As for the three preceding clauses, the narrators agree that he connected them with “and.”

It is well known that “and” does not imply chronological sequence, according to the correct view held by the majority. It therefore does not indicate that some of these matters preceded others. Even if we assume that chronological sequence was intended—either because of the order in which he mentioned them, placing some before others, or because of the conjunction “and,” according to those who hold that it implies sequence—all this would indicate is that His existence preceded His Throne’s being upon the water; that His Throne’s being upon the water preceded His writing everything in the Record; and that His writing everything in the Record preceded the creation of the heavens and the earth.

There is no mention here of the very first created thing. Indeed, it does not even state that the Throne and the water were created, although all of that is created, as he stated elsewhere. Rather, in answering the people of Yemen, his purpose was only to inform them about the beginning of the creation of the heavens, the earth, and everything between them—the things created in six days—not about the beginning of what Allah created before that.

The fifth point: He mentioned those things in terms indicating their being and existence

---

## Page 9354

and did not address the beginning of their creation, whereas he spoke of the heavens and the earth in terms indicating their creation. Whether his words were “And He created the heavens and the earth” or “Then He created the heavens and the earth,” in either case he stated that they were created. Every created thing comes into being after not having existed, even if it was created from matter. Thus, in Sahīh Muslim, ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, narrated that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Allah created the angels from light, created the jinn from a flame of fire, and created Adam from what has been described to you.”

If the Messenger’s wording, peace and blessings be upon him, was “Then He created,” this indicates that the creation of the heavens and the earth followed what he had previously mentioned: His Throne’s being upon the water and His writing in the Record. This wording is more fitting for the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, because it provides a complete explanation and conveys the intended meaning through a word denoting sequence. If his wording used “and,” the context nevertheless indicates that he meant that He created the heavens and the earth after that, as the other texts also indicate.

It is known that his purpose was not to speak of the creation of the Throne or the water, much less to suggest that their creation coincided with the creation of the heavens and the earth. If the wording contains nothing indicating the creation of those things except their being created concurrently with the heavens and the earth—yet he spoke of the creation of the heavens while those things existed—then it is clear that he meant that He created the heavens and the earth when

---

## Page 9355

the Throne was upon the water, as He stated in the Quran. This necessarily means that the Throne was upon the water before the creation of the heavens and the earth, as he stated in the authentic hadith:

“Allah decreed the destinies of created beings fifty thousand years before creating the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water.”

He thus stated that this decree, which preceded the creation of the heavens and the earth by fifty thousand years, took place when His Throne was upon the water.

(Sixth point: The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, either said, “He existed, and there was nothing before Him,” or said, “and nothing was with Him,” or “other than Him.” If he used only the first wording, it did not address His existence—exalted is He—before all things that come into being.

If he used the second or third wording, then his statement, “There was nothing with Him, and His Throne was upon the water, and He wrote in the Record,” must mean either that His Throne was upon the water when there was nothing with Him, or that His Throne was upon the water afterwards. If he intended the first, the meaning would be that nothing belonging to the subject of the question—namely, this world—was with Him. The intended meaning would thus be that Allah existed before this observable world, and His Throne was upon the water.

As for the third possibility—namely, that the intended meaning is that He existed with nothing alongside Him, and afterwards His Throne was upon the water and He wrote in the Record, then created the heavens

---

## Page 9356

and the earth—this says nothing about the very first thing Allah created. Indeed, it does not even mention His creation of the Throne and the water; it speaks only of His creation of the heavens and the earth. Nor does it explicitly state that His Throne was upon the water only afterwards. Rather, this is mentioned using the conjunction wāw (“and”), which simply joins the coordinated clauses without specifying their sequence.

Since the hadith neither identifies the first created thing nor mentions when the Throne was created—which it describes as being upon the water alongside the statement, “Allah existed, and there was nothing with Him”—this indicates that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not intend to speak of Allah’s existence alone before everything else and the beginning of created things thereafter, if his wording does not indicate that. Rather, he intended to describe the beginning of the creation of the heavens and the earth.

(Seventh point: It may be said that one cannot assert with certainty what the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, intended without evidence indicating his intent. Even supposing that his wording admits either of two meanings, neither may be asserted definitively without evidence. Thus, if one of them is the more likely meaning, anyone who asserts that the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, intended the other meaning is mistaken.

(Eighth point: It may be said that, if this proposition were true, it would be too momentous to establish through ambiguous wording in a report narrated by only one person. Mentioning it in the Quran and Sunnah would have been among the most important matters, because of people’s need to know

---

## Page 9357

this, given the confusion, dispute, and disagreement it has occasioned. Since nothing in the Sunnah establishes this claim, it cannot be established through what is assumed to be the meaning of the hadith in its context. They merely heard that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Allah existed, and there was nothing with Him.”

They assumed this wording was established, taking it in isolation from the rest of what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said. They also assumed that it meant to state that He, exalted is He, preceded everything. On the strength of these two assumptions, they attributed that meaning to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. Yet they have no knowledge of either premise, nor even a supposition grounded in any indication.

Even supposing that they did not assert with certainty that he intended the other meaning, they have nothing that warrants certainty about this meaning. Doubt has arisen among them, yet they attribute to the Messenger something they do not know that he said. Allah, exalted is He, has said:

“Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge.”

And He, exalted is He, has said:

“Say, ‘My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether open or concealed, sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.’”

All of this is impermissible.

(Tenth point: Some people have added to it:

“And He is now as He has always been.”

This addition was merely introduced by someone on his own authority; it does not appear in any of the narrations. Some of them then interpret it to mean that nothing exists with Him even now; rather, His existence is the very existence of created things, as maintained by the proponents of

---

## Page 9358

the unity of existence, who say: “The Creator’s very existence is identical to that of the created being.” This is the view expressed by Ibn ‘Arabī, Ibn Sab‘īn, al-Qūnawī, al-Tilimsānī, Ibn al-Fārid, and others like them. This view is necessarily known to be false on both scriptural and rational grounds.

(Eleventh point: Many people make this their principal proof from revelation that temporally originated events have a beginning, and that such events, as a class, were preceded by nonexistence, since they have found nothing explicitly stating this in the Quran and Sunnah. Yet they attribute this view to Muslims, Jews, and Christians. Such claims appear in the books of most adherents of the speculative theology introduced into Islam—a theology condemned by the early generations, through which its adherents contradicted revelation and reason.

Some report this as a consensus of the Muslims, although they have no transmitted evidence for it from any of the Companions or those who followed them in righteousness, nor from the Quran and Sunnah, let alone evidence that it is the position of all Muslims. Some suppose that anyone who disagrees with this has affirmed the eternity of the world and sided with the eternalist philosophers. They suppose this because they have examined many works of speculative theology and found only two positions in them:

The position of the philosophers who affirm the eternity of the world, whether its form or its matter, regardless of whether it is said to exist independently or to be an effect of something else;

And the position of those speculative theologians who refuted them—the Jahmiyyah, the Mu‘tazilah, and the Karrāmiyyah—who say that

---

## Page 9359

the Lord had never done anything or spoken anything, then brought speech and action into being without any cause whatsoever.

Another group, such as the Kullābiyyah and those who agree with them, say instead that speech is eternal in its individual reality: either a single meaning or eternal, beginningless letters and sounds, each eternally existent. They say that the Lord had never done anything or spoken by His will and power; then events began to occur through His power and will—either subsisting in His essence or separate from Him, according to those who consider this possible, or only separate from Him, according to those who do not consider it possible for such events to subsist in His essence.

Clearly, this view more closely resembles what the messengers conveyed: that Allah is the Creator of all things and that Allah created the heavens and the earth in six days. Thus, anyone who supposes that these are the only two positions people hold, and believes that the messengers speak only the truth, will suppose that this is the position of the messengers and their followers.

Yet when asked to produce transmitted evidence for this position from the messengers, he cannot do so. No one can produce a verse or hadith indicating it, either explicitly or by its apparent meaning. Indeed, he cannot even transmit it from any of the Prophet’s Companions—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or those who followed them in righteousness. Yet they have made this the meaning of the temporal origination of the world, which is the first issue in the fundamentals of

---

## Page 9360

religion as they understand it. Thus, they have no means of knowing that the Messenger taught what they regard as the foundation of religion—the religion of the messengers—nor is there any rational evidence for it. Rather, both reason and revelation indicate the contrary. Anyone who does not know whether the Messenger brought the very foundation of his religion, which he regards as the religion of Allah and His Messenger, is among the most misguided people in religious matters.

The twelfth point is that, once they believed this to be the religion of Islam, they began defending it with the rational arguments familiar to them. Their principal and strongest argument rests on the impossibility of a beginningless succession of events. Through it, they sought to establish that everything possessing an attribute originated in time, calling this a proof of the temporal origination of bodies. This committed them to denying the attributes of the Lord, Mighty and Majestic: that He has no knowledge, power, or speech subsisting in Him; rather, His speech is created and separate from Him, as are His pleasure and anger. They also accepted the consequences that Allah will not be seen in the Hereafter and that He is not above the Throne, along with other implications through which they denied what Allah and His Messenger affirmed. In reality, their position amounted to denying the truth of what the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—brought. People of reason then attacked their arguments and exposed their invalidity. This was among the things that empowered the eternalists who maintained that the world is eternal, once they understood the true nature of these people’s position and arguments and forgot its invalidity.

Then, supposing that this was the position of the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and believing it to be false, they said:

“The Messenger did not explain

---

## Page 9361

the realities, whether he knew them or not; rather, he addressed the general public only with what they could imagine and what would benefit them.”

Thus, those speculative theologians who denied the divine attributes erred in matters of both revelation and reason. Their error became one of the chief reasons the philosophers gained the upper hand, for those eternalist philosophers assumed that there were only two positions on this question: the position of those theologians and their own. Having seen that the theologians’ position was false, they treated this as proof that their own position was correct. Yet the eternalist philosophers have no rational proof whatsoever for their claim that the celestial spheres are eternal. One of the principal causes of this was their failure to ascertain what Allah had sent His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—to convey.

The thirteenth point is that misunderstanding this hadith stems from unfamiliarity with the texts of the Quran and Sunnah, and indeed with sound reason itself. This misunderstanding has plunged many speculative thinkers and their followers into bewilderment and misguidance, for they knew only two positions: that of the eternalists, who affirmed the eternity of the world, and that of the Jahmiyyah, who held that He had always been inactive, neither acting nor speaking by His power and will. They saw that the implications of each position entailed its falsity and inconsistency, and so remained bewildered, doubtful, and ignorant. This is the condition of countless people among them. Some expressly acknowledged this about themselves, as al-Rāzī and others did.

One of the principal causes of this was that they examined the true nature of the philosophers’ position and found that, according to it, the particular effect had always coexisted with its agent, from eternity and forever, and sound

---

## Page 9362

reason requires that the agent must precede its act, and that positing an effect while supposing that it has always coexisted with its agent—the agent never preceding it, but accompanying it from eternity and forever—contradicts sound reason. It is firmly established in people’s innate dispositions that for an effect to be created means that it came into existence after not existing. Thus, Allah’s statement in His Book that He created the heavens and the earth conveys to all people that they came into existence after not existing. As for supposing that they have always existed alongside Him while also being His creations, this is rejected by people’s innate dispositions. Only a small faction of eternalists, such as Ibn Sīnā and others like him, have maintained this.

The majority of eternalist philosophers, such as Aristotle and his followers, do not maintain, as these others do, that the celestial spheres are effects of an efficient cause. Although their position is even more unsound than that of their later counterparts, they did not contradict sound reason on this particular point as the latter did, even though they contradicted it in other respects.

They also examined the true nature of the position held by the speculative theologians of the Jahmiyyah and Qadariyyah and their followers. They found that, according to this position, the agent began to act after not acting, without anything occurring that necessitated its acting. They saw that sound reason requires that, if an agent begins to act after not acting, something new must have occurred; that it is rationally impossible for something to become possible after having been impossible without some new occurrence; that there is no cause necessitating the occurrence of a time that began at the time of origination; and that time as such cannot have a beginning. Thus they came to

---

## Page 9363

think that reconciling these views would entail combining two contradictories: the agent must precede its act, yet it is impossible for it to become an agent after not having been one, so its act must coexist with it. The act would thus both coexist with the agent and not coexist with it, in that it came into existence after not existing, having originated and been preceded by nonexistence. On the latter assumption, it would be impossible for the agent’s act to have been preceded by nonexistence, whereas on the former assumption, its act must have been preceded by nonexistence. They found their minds unable to comprehend what necessitated this affirmation and what necessitated this negation. Since combining two contradictories is impossible, this plunged them into bewilderment and doubt.

One reason for this is that they did not understand the true nature of revelation and reason. They neither understood what the Quran and Sunnah indicated nor distinguished between seemingly similar matters in rational inquiry. Reason distinguishes between a speaker’s continually saying one thing after another, or an agent’s continually doing one thing after another, and the individual acts and utterances themselves. It therefore holds:

Each of the agent’s acts must be preceded by the agent and must be preceded by nonexistence. It is impossible for a particular act to coexist with its agent from eternity and forever. But the agent’s having always performed one act after another is an aspect of its perfection. Thus, if the agent is living, and it is said that life necessarily entails action and movement—as stated by leading scholars of hadith such as al-Bukhārī, al-Dārimī, and others—and that He has always spoken whenever He wills and whatever He wills, and the like, as stated by Ibn al-Mubārak, Ahmad, and others

---

## Page 9364

among the leading scholars of hadith and the Sunnah—then His speaking or acting would be inseparable from His life, and His life is inseparable from Him. Thus He has always spoken and acted.

This is understood alongside the recognition that a living being speaks and acts by its will and power, and that this entails one utterance after another and one act after another. The agent precedes each of its acts, which entails that everything other than Him is originated and created. We do not say that there was a time when He had no power until power was created for Him—for one who has no power is powerless. Rather, we say: Allah has always been All-Knowing, All-Powerful, and Sovereign, without likeness or specification of how. None of the things He has made is eternal alongside Him. No indeed: He is the Creator of everything, and everything other than Him is His creation. Every created thing originated, coming into existence after not existing, even if it is supposed that He has always been creating and acting.

If it is said that creating is an attribute of perfection, because of His words, Most High:

“Is He who creates like one who does not create?”

is it possible for His being the Creator to be everlasting, while everything He creates is originated and preceded by nonexistence, with nothing eternal alongside Allah? This expresses perfection more fully than His being inactive and unable to act, then becoming able to act, with action becoming possible for Him without any cause.

As for holding that a particular thing He has made coexists with Him from eternity and forever, this in reality negates His creating and acting. For an agent’s coexisting with its effect from eternity and forever contradicts clear reason.

---

## Page 9365

Thus, although these eternalist philosophers claim to affirm His perpetual agency, they actually deny His agency—the most manifest of the attributes of the Lord, Most High. This is why it was proclaimed in the very first revelation sent down to the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, which begins:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created.”
“Created man from a clinging substance.”
“Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous.”
“Who taught by the pen.”
“Taught man what he did not know.”

He spoke of creation in general terms, then singled out man; He spoke of teaching in general terms, then specified teaching by the pen. Creation entails His action, while teaching entails His speech, for He teaches through His speaking. He speaks through revelation, from behind a veil, or by sending a messenger who reveals, by His permission, whatever He wills. He, Most High, said:

“And taught you what you did not know.”

And He, Most High, said:

“So whoever disputes with you about it after knowledge has come to you”

And He, Most High, said:

“Do not hasten with the Quran before its revelation to you is completed, and say, ‘My Lord, increase me in knowledge.’”

And He, Most High, said:

“The Most Merciful.”
“Taught the Quran.”
“Created man.”
“Taught him eloquent expression.”
“The sun and the moon follow precise reckoning.”

In reality, the position of these philosophers entails that He neither created nor taught, for what they affirm of creation and teaching amounts only to negating them. According to them, the celestial sphere coexists with Him from eternity and forever. It therefore cannot be something He has made, since an agent must precede its act. They also hold that He does not teach any particular item of knowledge. Yet teaching presupposes knowledge, so anyone who does not know particulars cannot

---

## Page 9366

teach them to anyone else. Everything that exists is a particular, not a universal. Thus, universals exist only in minds, not in external reality. If He knows none of the particulars, He knows nothing of existing things; it is therefore impossible for Him to impart to anyone else any knowledge of particular existing things.

Those among them who say, “He knows neither universals nor particulars,” hold an even more repugnant position.

Those who say, “He knows unchanging universals but not changing ones,” maintain that He knows nothing of events that come into being and imparts no knowledge of them to any of His creatures, just as their position entails that He did not create them. Thus, according to them, there is neither creation nor knowledge. This is the true substance of the position of their leading authority, Aristotle. He did not affirm that the Lord brought the world into being, nor did he regard Him as an efficient cause. Rather, he affirmed only that He is a final cause: the celestial sphere moves in imitation of Him, just as the beloved moves the lover. He explicitly stated that He does not know things.
