Thus, in his view, there is neither creation nor knowledge. Yet the first revelation Allah sent down to His Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was:

“Read in the name of your Lord who created.”
“Created man from a clinging substance.”
“Read, and your Lord is the Most Generous.”
“Who taught by the pen.”
“Taught man what he did not know.”

The fourteenth argument:

Allah, Most High, sent the messengers and revealed the Books to call creation to worship Him alone, without partner. This entails His knowledge of the creatures He has brought into being—the observable, existing creatures: the heavens, the earth, and everything between them. He therefore declared in the Book—no Book has come from Him

---

## Page 9367

that offers better guidance than it—that He created the fundamental components of these existing, observable created things in six days, then rose over the Throne. He prescribed for the believers to assemble one day each week to worship Allah and solemnly observe that occasion, making it a reminder of the first week, in which Allah created the heavens and the earth.

Since the week became known only through the reports of the prophets, the names of its days appeared in their languages, peace be upon them. For naming follows the texts, and a name expresses what has been conceived. Since the day, the month, and the year could be conceived through reason, these notions were given names and expressed in words. As for the week, reason alone provides no means of knowing it; it became known only through revelation. Knowledge of it therefore belonged to those who received revelation transmitted from the prophets, rather than to others.

The prophets thus informed people about the creation of this existing, observable world, how its creation began, and that Allah created it in six days. As for what He created before that, one thing after another, this is comparable to what He will create after the Resurrection has taken place and the people of Paradise and the people of Hell have entered their respective dwellings. Such matters cannot be known in detail by His servants.

For this reason, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, once stood among us and told us about the beginning of creation, all the way to the people of Paradise entering their dwellings and the people of Hell entering theirs.”

Narrated by al-Bukhārī. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, informed them about the beginning of creation through to the people of Paradise and Hell entering their respective dwellings.

---

## Page 9368

The wording “He began creation” is comparable to that in the other hadith:

“Allah determined the destinies of created beings fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth.”

Here, “created beings” means the familiar created beings brought into existence after the Throne had been created and was upon the water. Thus, the decree concerning created things refers to the decree concerning the creation of this world, as in the hadith about the Pen:

“When Allah created it, He said, ‘Write.’ It asked, ‘What shall I write?’ He said, ‘Write what will happen until the Day of Resurrection.’”

Likewise, the authentic hadith states:

“Allah determined the destinies of created beings fifty thousand years before He created the heavens and the earth, and His Throne was upon the water.”

And the wording in the other authentic hadith:

“Allah was, and nothing was before Him. His Throne was upon the water. He wrote everything in the Record, then created the heavens and the earth.”

This means that He wrote everything He intended to create of that creation. For the expression “everything” has, in each context, a scope determined by the purpose for which it is used, as in His words:

“He has knowledge of everything”; “He has power over everything”; “Allah is the Creator of everything”; “destroying everything”; “She has been given of everything”; “We opened for them the gates of everything”; and “Of everything We created pairs.”

The messengers also told of the prior existence of His names and attributes, as in His words: “And Allah has always been Almighty, All-Wise”; “All-Hearing, All-Seeing”; “All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”; and similar expressions.

Ibn ‘Abbās said, “He was, and He ever remains so.” He did not restrict His being so to one time rather than another.

---

## Page 9369

and it is impossible for anyone other than Him to bring an attribute into being in Him. Indeed, it is impossible for anything inseparable from Him to depend on anyone other than Him, glory be to Him. He deserves the utmost perfection, and His essence itself necessarily entails this. Nothing of His perfection, or of what His perfection necessarily entails, depends on anyone other than Him, but rather on His own holy Self. He is praised for this from eternity and forever, and He praises Himself and extols Himself as He deserves.

No one other than Him can fully encompass His praise. Rather, He is as He has praised Himself, as the foremost of Adam’s descendants said in the authentic hadith:

“O Allah, I seek refuge in Your pleasure from Your wrath, and in Your pardon from Your punishment. I seek refuge in You from You. I cannot encompass Your praise; You are as You have praised Yourself.”

If it is said, “He did not speak, then He spoke,” or, “Speech was impossible for Him, then became possible,” such a claim—besides attributing deficiency to Him in eternity and asserting that He subsequently acquired perfection, and likening Him to a created being that passes from deficiency to perfection—is impossible. This is because what is impossible cannot become possible without a cause. There is nothing in sheer nonexistence, so what is impossible in that state cannot become possible without a newly arising cause.

Likewise, if it is said, “His speech in its entirety is a single meaning inseparable from His essence, with regard to which He has neither power nor will,” this in reality amounts to denying speech and combining contradictions. For it affirms the existence of something that has no reality—indeed, something that cannot possibly exist—while entailing nothing praiseworthy and no perfection.

---

## Page 9370

Likewise, if it is said, “His speech is eternal in every particular, consisting of eternal letters and sounds inseparable from His essence, with regard to which He has neither power nor will,” this—besides its evident contradiction and rational incoherence—entails no perfection, since He does not speak by His will or power, nor whenever He wills.

As for those who say, “His speech is nothing but what He creates in something other than Himself,” this amounts to a complete denial of His speech. Its true implication is that He does not speak, as the early Jahmiyyah said, and it constitutes a negation of His attributes. By affirming speech in its recognized sense while denying its necessary implications, they fall into such contradiction and incoherence that their position is manifestly among the most unsound views anyone has held. They affirm that He commands and forbids, informs and gives glad tidings, warns and calls, without any of these subsisting in Him. Likewise, they say that He wills, loves, hates, and becomes angry, without any of these subsisting in Him. The extent to which this contradicts clear reason and authentic revelation has been discussed elsewhere.

As for those who maintain that this world is eternal, they are further removed from reason and revelation than all the other groups. Thus, they deny both speech subsisting in His essence and speech that He creates in something other than Himself. According to them, His speech is nothing but the intellectual concepts and imagined representations that arise in souls. This is what His speaking to Moses, peace be upon him, means according to them. Speaking is thus reduced to nothing more than the speaker’s knowledge.

Then, if they also say, “He does not know particulars,” there is neither knowledge nor imparting of knowledge. This is the utmost denial of His attributes and attribution of deficiency to Him. They have no proof whatsoever

---

## Page 9371

for the eternity of anything in the world. Rather, their arguments indicate only the eternity of action as a kind, and that the agent has always been acting, or that his action has always had a duration, or that matter has always had underlying matter. None of their proofs establishes the eternity of the celestial sphere, of any of its motions, or of time as the measure of the celestial sphere’s motion. The messengers declared that the celestial spheres were created, as was the time that measures their motion, while also declaring that they were created from previously existing matter and in a time preceding this time.

For He, glorified be He, has stated that He created the heavens and the earth in six days. Whether those days are said to have been equal in length to these days measured by sunrise and sunset, or longer—as some have said, each day lasting a thousand years—there is no doubt that the days in which the heavens and the earth were created were different from these days, and from the time that measures the motion of these celestial spheres. Those days were measured by the motion of bodies that existed before the creation of the heavens and the earth. He, glorified be He, has also stated:

“He turned to the heaven while it was smoke and said to it and to the earth, ‘Come, willingly or unwillingly.’ They said, ‘We come willingly.’”

It was thus created from smoke. Reports from the early generations state that it was created from water vapour—the water upon which the Throne rested, mentioned in His words:

“It is He who created the heavens and the earth in six days, and His Throne was upon the water.”

He has thus stated that He created the heavens and the earth over a period of time and from matter. The Quran does not mention the creation of anything

---

## Page 9372

from nothing. Rather, it states that He created a being after it had been nothing, as He says:

“I created you before, when you were nothing.”

Yet He also states that He created him from a drop of sperm.

There are two interpretations of His words:

“Or were they created by nothing, or are they themselves the creators?”

The majority understand this to mean: Were they created without a creator, but rather from sheer nonexistence? Similarly, the Most High says:

“He has subjected to you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth—all from Him.”

And the Most High says:

“And His word which He conveyed to Mary, and a spirit from Him.”

And the Most High says:

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah.”

Another interpretation is: Were they created without matter? This, however, is weak, because He says immediately afterwards:

“Or are they themselves the creators?”

This shows that the alternatives are: Were they created without a creator, or are they themselves the creators? Had the intended meaning been “without matter,” He would have said: Were they created from nothing, or from a lowly fluid? This indicates that the intended meaning is: I am their Creator, not the matter from which they were made.

Moreover, the notion that they were created without matter does not entail denying the Creator’s existence. Had they believed this, it would not have undermined their belief in the Creator, but would merely have shown their ignorance. Furthermore, they never thought this, nor does Satan whisper such a notion to human beings. Rather, they all know

---

## Page 9373

that they were created from their fathers and mothers. Furthermore, their acknowledgment of this neither entails faith nor precludes disbelief. The question is rhetorical, expressing denial and intended to elicit their acknowledgment that they were not created by nothing. If they acknowledge that a Creator created them, this benefits them. But if they merely acknowledge that they were created from matter, this avails them nothing before Allah.

The fifteenth argument:

To acknowledge that Allah has always done whatever He wills and spoken whatever He wills is to ascribe to Him the perfection befitting Him. Anything contrary to this is a deficiency that must be denied of Him. For the claim that He was once unable to speak or act and then became able—even though ability is an attribute of His—implies that He previously lacked the attribute of power, which is inseparable from His essence and is among the clearest attributes of perfection. This is rationally impossible, as established by conclusive proof.

If He was not capable and then became capable, there must have been something that made Him capable after He had not been so. If there was nothing but sheer nonexistence, it would be impossible for Him to become capable after not having been so. Likewise, it is impossible for Him to become knowing after previously not having been so. This is unlike the human being, who was neither knowing nor capable, and whom another then made knowing and capable.

The same applies when they say, “He did not speak, then became one who speaks.” This is among the objections Imam Ahmad raised against the Jahmiyyah, since they held that He did not speak and then became one who speaks. They said, “Like a human being.” He replied, “You have thus combined likening Allah to His creation with disbelief.” I have quoted his words elsewhere.

---

## Page 9374

If someone says, “He was able from eternity to create in the unending future,” this is a contradictory statement, because, according to them, it was not possible for Him to act from eternity. If acting was not possible for Him from eternity, He could not have possessed power from eternity. To affirm both His power and the impossibility of what that power encompasses is to combine two opposites: when the act was impossible, He did not possess the power to perform it.

Moreover, the act would pass from being impossible to being possible without a necessitating cause determining this, and without an impossible nonexistence.

Furthermore, in every state the mind can conceive, the act is possible and He possesses the power to perform it. If something Allah willed is supposed to have preceded that, the same applies. Thus, He has always possessed power, and acting has always been possible. His power and His ability to act have no beginning. He has always possessed power and been able to act; acting has never been impossible for Him.

They also claim that acting is impossible in beginningless eternity. Yet beginningless eternity is not something bounded at which the mind can stop. Whatever limit one reaches in conceiving how far back an act might occur, beginningless eternity extends before it without any fixed limit. Even if one were to suppose that there were cities many times more numerous than all the cities of the earth, each filled with mustard seeds, and that one mustard seed perished every million years, all the mustard seeds would perish without beginningless eternity having been exhausted. Even if this were multiplied many times over, it would never be exhausted. Thus, whatever time one supposes, beginningless eternity extends before it.

---

## Page 9375

And whenever an act occurred, it had already been possible before then. If it was possible, what necessitated singling out that moment for creation rather than any moment preceding it in the infinite past?

Moreover, beginningless eternity means the absence of a beginning; it is not something bounded. Saying, “He has always possessed power,” is equivalent to saying, “He possesses power perpetually.” His possession of power is a perpetual attribute without a beginning. Likewise, saying, “He has always spoken whenever He willed, and has always done whatever He willed,” entails that He perpetually speaks and acts by His will and power. If someone imagines that this entails something else being eternal alongside Him, that stems from his faulty conception. Since He is the Creator of everything, everything other than Him is created and preceded by nonexistence. Nothing alongside Him is coeternal with Him.

When it is said, “He has always created,” this means that He has always created one creature after another, just as He will continue throughout the endless future to create one creature after another. We negate whatever we negate of events and motions, one after another. This merely describes Him as perpetually acting, not as having any particular product of His action eternally alongside Him.

If it is supposed that their kind has always existed with Him, neither revelation nor reason negates this coexistence. Rather, it belongs to His perfection. The Most High says:

“Is He who creates like one who does not create? Will you not then take heed?”

Created beings will continue to exist with Him, and their continuing to exist with Him in the future does not conflict with His perfection. And between beginningless eternity, in the future, although in the past it came into being after not existing—for every created thing has a beginning, while we do not assert definitively that it has an end.

---

## Page 9376

And this is a distinction concerning individual created beings, and it is a valid one. Yet many people confuse the kind with the individual instance. Many have likewise been confused about speech, failing to distinguish between His speech being beginningless—in the sense that He has always spoken whenever He willed—and a particular utterance being beginningless. Similarly, they have failed to distinguish between a particular act being beginningless and the kind of action to which it belongs being beginningless. The celestial sphere, for example, originated: it is created and preceded by nonexistence, as is everything other than Him.

This is what the Quran, the Sunnah, and the transmitted reports indicate. It is also what sound rational arguments, free of specious doubts, demonstrate, as we have explained at length elsewhere, showing the agreement between sound reason and authentic revelation. Even if philosophers, speculative theologians, or others err concerning both or either of them, true statements known through reason or revelation corroborate rather than contradict one another. The Most High says:

“Those who bring the truth and affirm it—it is they who are the God-fearing.”

This follows His words:

“Who is more unjust than one who fabricates a lie against Allah or denies the truth when it comes to him?”

He praises only those who bring the truth and affirm the truth that comes to them. Such is the condition of someone who accepts nothing but the truth and does not reject the truth brought to him by others. Rather, he accepts it, neither setting the two against one another nor using either to reject the other,

---

## Page 9377

[in contrast to] (1) the condition of someone who lies about Allah, attributing to Him, on the basis of revelation or reason, what cannot rightly be attributed to Him, or denies the truth when it comes to him, branding as a liar anyone who brings truth established through revelation or reason. The Most High says concerning the people of the Fire:

“Had we listened or used our reason, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze.”

He thus states that, had they listened or reasoned, they would not have entered the Fire. The Most High also says:

“Have they not traveled through the land, so that they may have hearts with which to reason or ears with which to hear? For it is not the eyes that are blind, but the hearts within the breasts.”

And the Most High says:

“We will show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth.”

That is, that the Quran is true. He thus states that He will show His servants the visible, created signs until it becomes clear that the verses recited and heard are true.

One way to recognize the source of these two groups’ error is to examine their misunderstanding of motion, coming into existence, and what these terms designate. One group—such as Aristotle and his followers—said that it is inconceivable that motion, time, and occurrences, considered as kinds, should have come into existence; that the source of every motion and occurrence should have begun to bring them about after not doing so; or that time should have come into existence after not having existed, when “before” and “after” can only be within time. All these propositions hold true only of the kind as a whole, not of particular instances. Then they supposed that the particular motion—namely, the motion of the celestial sphere—is

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 9378

eternal and beginningless, and that its time is beginningless. They thus went plainly astray, contradicting the authentic, mass-transmitted reports from the prophets—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them—as well as the clear conclusions of reason accepted by most rational people, past and present.

Another group supposed that motion, occurrences, and action, considered as kinds, could exist only after none of them had existed; or that the agent of all things must have been eternally inactive, and that occurrences then came about without any cause whatsoever, action passed from impossibility to possibility without any cause, and the agent became capable after not having been so, without any cause. They supposed that something came into existence after not existing, outside time, and held similar notions that contradict clear reason.

Yet they imagine that this is the position of the adherents of the revealed religions—Muslims, Jews, and Christians. But this view has not been transmitted from Moses, Jesus, or Muhammad—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them—or from any of their companions. Rather, it is something introduced by certain adherents of religious innovation and spread among those ignorant of what the messengers and their companions actually taught. They therefore supposed that this was the teaching of the messengers—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them.

Attributing this view to the messengers and their followers thus leads to impugning them: either for not knowing the truth concerning these lofty questions, or for failing to explain it. In the eyes of these people, either charge warrants setting aside the Book, the Sunnah, and the reports from the early generations as sources of guidance.

---

## Page 9379

They went astray only because they did not know the way of the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—his Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—and those who followed them in righteousness. For Allah Most High sent His Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with guidance and the religion of truth, to make it prevail over all religion. And sufficient is Allah as a witness.

---

## Page 9380

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah, who is worthy of every attribute of praise and perfection and deserves praise in every circumstance. No one can praise Him fully; rather, He is as He has praised Himself, with the most perfect praise and the finest words. He bestows His blessings upon His servants by creating them, sending messengers to them, and guiding the believers among them to righteous deeds. He graciously pardons them and grants them an everlasting reward that neither ceases nor passes away. To Him belongs praise in this world and the Hereafter—abundant, pure, blessed praise, continuing without interruption.

I bear witness that there is no god worthy of worship except Allah alone, without partner—the Knower of the unseen and the seen, the Most Great, the Most High. And I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger, through whom He guided people out of error, and who enjoined upon the believers what is right and forbade them what is wrong, made lawful for them good things and prohibited foul things, and relieved them of their burdens and shackles. May Allah bless him and his family, the best of

---

## Page 9381

families—and his Companions, who championed the religion until the truth prevailed and the landmarks of misguidance were effaced.

To proceed:

Allah Most High created all things for such wise purposes as He willed, lavished upon them blessings beyond their reckoning, and honoured the children of Adam in manifold ways. He singled out His believing servants by choosing and guiding them, and made the nation of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—the best community among His creation ever raised up for mankind.

He sent among them a Messenger from their own number, whose truthfulness, trustworthiness and noble conduct they knew, reciting His verses to them to bring them out of the darkness and bewilderment of disbelief, guide them to a straight path, and call them to worship Him. He sent down to them the finest Book He has ever revealed to His creation and made it an enduring sign until the coming of His Hour: a dazzling miracle setting forth His proof, and clear evidence elucidating His call. Through it Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace, brings them out of darkness into light by His permission, and shows them the path to His Paradise.

Blessed, then, is whoever holds fast to the Book of Allah and follows the Messenger in his sunnah and revealed law. Whoever takes his beacon as a guide and follows in his footsteps is the best of people in this world and the Hereafter. Whoever revives any part of his sunnah will receive its reward and the reward of those who act upon it, without any reduction in the reward for their obedience. Allah does not wrong anyone even by an atom’s weight; rather, He multiplies good deeds by His grace and mercy.

---

## Page 9382

Reviving his sunnah encompasses many forms of righteousness, such is the vastness of Allah’s bounty and generosity. It may be done by conveying and explaining it so that the truth prevails and is upheld, or by supporting it through spending wealth and engaging in jihad, thereby aiding Allah’s religion and making His word supreme. Jihad with one’s wealth is coupled with jihad in person; indeed, Allah Most High has mentioned the former before the latter in several passages because of its great standing and benefits. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever equips a warrior has himself taken part in the campaign, and whoever takes good care of that warrior’s family in his absence has himself taken part in the campaign.”

He also said:

“Whoever provides a fasting person with food to break his fast will have a reward equal to his,” and an equal recompense.

This is especially true of deeds whose benefit endures after a person dies and goes to his grave, as he said in the hadith:

“When a son of Adam dies, his deeds come to an end except for three.”

These three are among his own deeds that endure after his death. They differ from the deeds of others that benefit him after his death, such as supplication, charity and freeing slaves. These are not his own endeavours, but the endeavours and intercession of others. Likewise, a believer is joined by those of his descendants whom Allah admits to Paradise.

The foundation of righteous deeds is the servant’s sincerity to Allah in his intention. For He, glorified is He, sent down the Scriptures, sent the messengers and created all creation only that He might be worshipped. This is what the messengers called all His creatures to, as He stated in His Book through the words of His messengers with the utmost clarity.

For this reason, the early generations favoured opening their gatherings, books and other undertakings with the hadith:

“Actions are judged only by intentions,”

placing it at the very outset. We therefore follow their path in this, for they were Islam’s finest army and its vanguard, and so we say

---

## Page 9383

seeking Allah’s help in following the path of His allies and those He loves:

Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Ansārī narrated from Muhammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Taymī, from ‘Alqamah ibn Waqqās al-Laythī, from ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—who said:

“I heard the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—say:

‘Actions are judged only by intentions, and each person will have only what he intended. Whoever migrates to Allah and His Messenger, his migration is to Allah and His Messenger. Whoever migrates for some worldly gain or to marry a woman, his migration is for that which he migrated.’”

This is an authentic hadith whose authenticity is unanimously accepted. The Muslim community has received it with acceptance and belief in its truth, although it is among the singular authentic hadiths. For although it has been narrated from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—through multiple chains, as collected by Ibn Mandah and other leading hadith scholars, hadith scholars agree that none of these chains is authentic except the aforementioned chain through ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him. No one narrated it from him except ‘Alqamah ibn Waqqās al-Laythī; no one narrated it from ‘Alqamah except Muhammad ibn Ibrāhīm; and no one narrated it from Muhammad except Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Ansārī, the judge of Medina.

The leading scholars of Islam narrated it from Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd. It is said that some two hundred scholars narrated it from him, including Mālik, al-Thawrī, Ibn ‘Uyaynah, Hammād and Hammād, ‘Abd al-Wahhāb al-Thaqafī, Abū Khālid al-Ahmar, Zā’idah, and Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd

---

## Page 9384

al-Qattān, Yazīd ibn Hārūn, and many others from Mecca, Medina, Kufa, Basra, Syria, and elsewhere, who taught al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, Ishāq and others of their generation, as well as Yahyā ibn Maʿīn, ʿAlī ibn al-Madīnī, and Abū ʿUbayd.

This hadith has parallels among authentic hadiths with singular chains, such as the hadith of Ibn ʿUmar reporting that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—“forbade selling or giving away rights of patronage.” Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded it. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dīnār alone narrated it from Ibn ʿUmar.

Another example is the hadith of Anas:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—entered Mecca wearing a mail helmet. He was told, ‘Ibn Khatal is clinging to the coverings of the Kaaba.’ He said, ‘Kill him.’”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded it. Al-Zuhrī alone narrated it from Anas, and it is said that Mālik alone narrated it from al-Zuhrī.

A singular hadith is one transmitted by a single narrator. Its singularity may concern its text or its chain of transmission. For example, its text may be authentically transmitted through a known chain, yet also be narrated through another, singular chain.

Some singular hadiths are authentic, but most are not, as Ahmad said:

“Beware of these singular reports, for most of them come from liars.”

This is why al-Tirmidhī says of certain hadiths, “It is singular through this route.” Al-Tirmidhī was the first to classify hadiths as authentic, good, singular

---

## Page 9385

and weak. No one before him is known to have used this classification. Rather, they used to divide hadiths into authentic and weak, just as they divided narrators into weak and not weak. In their terminology, weak hadiths were of two kinds: those that could not be used as evidence, corresponding to “weak” in al-Tirmidhī’s terminology; and those that could be used as evidence, corresponding to “good” in his terminology.

Similarly, in the terminology of jurists, weakness caused by illness is of two kinds. One restricts a person’s gratuitous dispositions to one-third of his estate, as when he becomes bedridden. The other allows his gratuitous dispositions to be charged against the estate as a whole, as in a minor illness that does not incapacitate him. Hence, statements by Ahmad and other jurists indicate that they used weak hadiths as evidence, such as the reports of ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, Ibrāhīm al-Hajarī, and others. What those scholars called weak is superior to many reports classified as good; indeed, many scholars classify it as authentic. Al-Tirmidhī explained that by “good” he meant a hadith transmitted through multiple routes, with no narrator accused of lying, and which is not anomalous.

Section

The meaning conveyed by this hadith is a great foundation of the religion—indeed, it is the foundation of every deed. Hence, scholars said, “Islam revolves around three hadiths,” and included this hadith among them. Ahmad, for example, cited the hadiths:

“Actions are judged only by intentions,”

and:

“Whoever performs an act that is not in accordance with our teaching will have it rejected,”

and:

“The lawful is clear, and the unlawful

---

## Page 9386

is clear.”

The significance of this hadith is that religion consists of doing what Allah has commanded and refraining from what He has forbidden. The hadith “The lawful is clear” explains what He has forbidden.

What Allah has commanded is of two kinds: outward deeds, comprising obligatory and recommended acts; and inward deeds, consisting of sincere devotion to Allah alone.

Thus, his statement, “Whoever performs an act…” rules out drawing near to Allah through anything other than what Allah has commanded, whether as an obligation or a recommendation. His statement, “Actions are judged only by intentions…” explains inward deeds and makes clear that drawing near to Allah requires sincere devotion to Him alone.

As al-Fudayl said concerning Allah’s words,

“that He may test you as to which of you is best in deeds,”

“This means the most sincere and the most correct.” He explained: “If a deed is sincere but not correct, it will not be accepted; and if it is correct but not sincere, it will not be accepted. It must be both sincere and correct. To be sincere is to be done for Allah, and to be correct is to conform to the Sunnah.”

This is indicated by Allah’s words:

“So whoever hopes to meet his Lord, let him do righteous deeds and associate no one in the worship of his Lord.”

Righteous deeds are those that Allah and His Messenger have commanded, whether as obligations or recommendations. Associating no one in the worship of one’s Lord means devoting one’s religion sincerely to Allah alone.

Likewise, Allah says:

“Yes, whoever submits himself to Allah and does good will have his reward with his Lord,”

to the end of the verse. And He says:

“And who is better in religion than one who submits himself

---

## Page 9387

to Allah and does good, and follows the faith of Abraham, inclining to the truth?” And He says:

“And whoever submits himself to Allah and does good has certainly grasped the firmest handhold.”

Submitting oneself to Allah entails devoting one’s deeds sincerely to Him. Doing good means performing one’s deeds well for Allah, by doing what He has commanded concerning them. As He, the Exalted, says:

“Indeed, We do not allow the reward of anyone who does good deeds to be lost.”

Performing a righteous deed poorly entails treating lightly the command to perform it, the deed itself, and the reward Allah has promised for it. Thus, when a servant devotes his religion sincerely to Allah and performs his deeds well for Him, he is among those who submit themselves to Allah and do good. He is therefore among those who will have their reward with their Lord, and who will have no fear, nor will they grieve.

Section

In Arabic usage, the word niyya (intention) is akin to qasd (purpose), irāda (will), and similar expressions. The Arabs say, “Nawāka Allāhu bi-khayr,” meaning, “May Allah will good for you.” They also say, “Nawā manwiyyahu” (he intended his destination), referring to the place he intends to reach. They call it nawā (an intended destination), just as they use qabd (seizure) to mean maqbūd (what is seized).

The word niyya is used to express a kind of willing, and also the very object intended. Thus, the Arabs say, “This is my niyya,” meaning, “This is the place I intended to visit.” They also say, “His niyya is near or far,” meaning the place that

---

## Page 9388

he intended to reach. Some people, however, say that intention is narrower in scope than will: a person’s will may concern his own actions or those of another, whereas his intention concerns only his own actions. Thus, you may say, “I wanted such-and-such from so-and-so,” but not, “I intended such-and-such from so-and-so.”

Section

People have differed over the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Deeds depend solely on intentions.”

Does it contain an ellipsis, or is its scope restricted? Or does it retain its apparent meaning and general scope?

A group of later scholars adopted the first view. They said that what is meant by “intentions” is the obligatory or recommended acts prescribed by religious law, and that not all deeds require these intentions to be valid. When obligations are fulfilled by returning usurped property, borrowed items and deposits, or by repaying debts, the person fulfilling them is discharged of liability even if he has no religiously prescribed intention in doing so. Indeed, he may be discharged of liability without any action on his part—for example, if the rightful owner takes possession of his very property, or if the wind carries a garment that was deposited for safekeeping or usurped and drops it into its owner’s hands, and similar cases.

Some of these scholars then said that the implied wording is: “The reward accruing from deeds depends solely on intentions,” or, “Deeds are accepted only with intentions.” Others said that the implied wording is: “Only acts prescribed by religious law

---

## Page 9389

, or only their validity, or only their sufficiency in discharging an obligation,” and the like.

The majority, however, said:

Rather, the hadith retains its apparent meaning and general scope. The intentions mentioned in it are not confined to righteous deeds alone; rather, it encompasses praiseworthy and blameworthy intentions, and praiseworthy and blameworthy deeds. Hence, he said later in the hadith:

“So whoever’s migration is to Allah and His Messenger…”

Thus, he mentioned the praiseworthy intention of migrating solely to Allah and His Messenger, and the blameworthy intention of migrating for a woman or wealth. He gave these details after first stating the general principle:

“Deeds depend solely on intentions, and each person shall have only what he intended.”

Then he explained this in detail by saying:

“So whoever’s migration…”

It has been reported that the occasion for this hadith was that a man migrated from Mecca to Medina for the sake of a woman he loved, named Umm Qays. Since he migrated for her sake, he became known as “the emigrant of Umm Qays.” Hence, the hadith mentions “or a woman to marry”—or, in another narration, “to wed.” A woman was specifically mentioned because the occasion of the hadith called for it. And Allah knows best.

By unanimous agreement, the particular occasion that gave rise to a generally worded statement cannot be excluded from its scope. Outwardly, migration is travel from one place to another. Travel is a general category comprising various kinds, which differ according to the traveller’s intention. It may be obligatory, as with Hajj or jihad that is an individual obligation; or it may be forbidden, such as an aggressor’s journey to commit robbery on

---

## Page 9390

the highway, or the journey of someone rebelling against the Muslim community, a runaway slave, or a wife defying her husband.

For this reason, the jurists discussed the distinction between someone whose journey is itself sinful and someone who commits a sin during his journey. They said:

If someone undertakes a permissible journey, such as one for Hajj, Umrah, or jihad, he may shorten his prayers and break his fast during it, according to the agreement of the four imams, even if he commits a sin during that journey. But if the journey itself is sinful, as when travelling to commit highway robbery or the like, may he avail himself of the concessions granted to travellers, such as breaking the fast and shortening prayers?

There is disagreement on this. The position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad is that he may not shorten his prayers or break his fast, whereas the position of Abū Hanīfah is that he may do so.

Since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, mentioned both kinds of journey, it is clear that he intended to speak of deeds in general, not merely deeds that are intrinsically acts of devotion, such as prayer and fasting. Likewise, he intended to speak of intention in general. It thus becomes clear that his saying:

“Deeds depend solely on intentions”

is an instance of the concise, comprehensive speech with which Allah Most High specially endowed him, as he said:

“I have been sent with concise, comprehensive speech.”

This hadith is among the most comprehensive of the sayings with which he was sent. Every deed a person performs, whether good or evil, depends on what he intended. If he pursues a good end through his deed, he shall have that good end; and if he pursues an evil end through it, he shall have what he intended.

---

## Page 9391

Section

The word “intention” may denote a mode of intending, or it may denote what is intended. Its use in this latter sense is perhaps more common in Arabic speech. Thus, the meaning is that deeds depend solely on what the doer intends—that is, on the object of his intention. Hence, the Prophet said in the remainder of the hadith:

“Whoever’s emigration is to Allah and His Messenger, his emigration is to Allah and His Messenger.”

He thus mentioned what the doer intends and seeks through his deed: the end he pursues. For every being that acts voluntarily must have an intended aim. Hence, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The names most beloved to Allah are ‘Abd Allāh and ‘Abd al-Rahmān; the ugliest are Harb and Murrah; and the truest are Hārith and Hammām.”

For every human being is a hārith and a hammām: a hārith is one who works and earns, while a hammām is one who forms intentions and wills.

Allah Most High says:

“Whoever desires the harvest of the Hereafter, We increase for him his harvest; and whoever desires the harvest of this world, We give him some of it, but he will have no share in the Hereafter.”

The expression “the harvest of this world” means its earnings and the work done for it. This is why al-Harīrī composed his Maqāmāt (Assemblies) in the voice of al-Hārith ibn Hammām, since this description holds true of everyone.

---

## Page 9392

Section

Scholars use the word “intention” in two senses. Sometimes they mean distinguishing one deed from another, or one act of worship from another. At other times, they mean distinguishing whom one worships and for whose sake one acts.

The first sense appears in their discussions of intention: Is it a condition for purification from ritual impurity? Does fasting require an intention specifying the fast and formed during the preceding night? If someone purifies himself intending an act for which purification is recommended, does that suffice for obligatory purification? Must the intention in prayer specify which prayer is being offered? And other such questions.

The second sense concerns, for example, distinguishing sincere devotion to Allah in one’s deeds from acting for show and reputation. Thus, they asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about a man who fights out of bravery, partisan zeal, or a desire to show off: which of these is fighting in Allah’s cause? He replied:

“Whoever fights so that the word of Allah may be supreme is fighting in Allah’s cause.”

This hadith applies to all other deeds. This kind of intention distinguishes someone who seeks Allah and the abode of the Hereafter through his deeds from someone who seeks worldly things: wealth, status, praise, commendation, veneration, and so forth. The hadith directly indicates this kind of intention, although it may be said that its general wording encompasses

---

## Page 9393

both kinds. For it distinguishes between someone who seeks Allah and His Messenger and someone who seeks worldly gain or a woman. It thus distinguishes between the ends for which one acts, not between one deed and another.

Allah Most High mentions sincerity in His Book in several places, as in His words:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah with sincere devotion to Him.”

And His words:

“So worship Allah with sincere devotion to Him.”

“Surely, sincere devotion is due to Allah alone.”

And His words:

“Say: It is Allah I worship, devoting my religion sincerely to Him.”

There are other such verses as well.

Sincere devotion is the foundation of Islam. Thus, He condemns showing off in such passages as:

“So woe to those who pray, yet are heedless of their prayer—those who show off.”

And His words:

“When they stand for prayer, they stand lazily, showing off before people and remembering Allah only a little.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Like one who spends his wealth to be seen by people”—and the rest of the verse.

And His words:

“And those who spend their wealth to be seen by people”—and the rest of the verse.

Section

Scholars agree that an act of worship intended for its own sake, such as prayer, fasting, or Hajj, is valid only with an intention. They disagree, however, concerning purification: for example, someone who is in a state of major ritual impurity, forgets this, and bathes merely to become clean. Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī, and Ahmad said: Intention

---

## Page 9394

is a condition for purification from all states of ritual impurity. Abū Hanīfa said: It is not required for purification with water, unlike dry ablution. Zufar said: It is required for neither. Some later scholars of the schools of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad said: It is required for removing physical impurity.

This last view is anomalous, for removing physical impurity does not require a person’s action: it may be removed by falling rain, a flowing river, and the like. How, then, could intention be required for its removal? Moreover, removing physical impurity is a matter of avoidance rather than affirmative action. Thus, if a person never consciously thinks during prayer that he is avoiding impurity, his prayer is nevertheless valid, provided he is keeping clear of it. Accordingly, Mālik, Ahmad—in the well-known position reported from him—and al-Shāfi'ī, in one of his two opinions, said: If someone prays with impurity on him and discovers it only after the prayer, he need not repeat it, because this is a matter of avoidance.

Allah relates that the believers say:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake.”

It is authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that Allah Most High said:

“I have done so.”

Whoever does something prohibited out of forgetfulness or by mistake incurs no sin. This differs from someone who omits something commanded: someone who misses a prayer, for example, must make it up. Thus, most scholars distinguish, in matters of prayer, fasting, and the state of consecration for pilgrimage, between someone who forgetfully commits a prohibited act and someone who forgetfully omits an obligation. Examples include someone who forgetfully speaks during prayer, eats while fasting, or applies perfume or puts on clothing while in the state of consecration.

Those who require intention for purification from states of ritual impurity cite this hadith as evidence against

---

## Page 9395

Abū Hanīfa. Abū Hanīfa concedes that purification performed without intention is not an act of worship and carries no reward. The disagreement concerns only whether prayer is valid with such purification. Thus, the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“Actions are only by intentions,”

does not establish the point at issue unless another premise is added: purification can only be an act of worship, and an act of worship is valid only with intention. Yet if this premise is accepted, there is no need to invoke this hadith as evidence. Everyone agrees that whatever can only be an act of worship is valid only with intention, unlike acts that may or may not constitute worship, such as returning entrusted property and repaying debts. The question therefore hinges on whether ablution can occur without being an act of worship.

The majority cite the texts concerning its reward, such as his saying:

“When a Muslim servant performs ablution, his sins depart with the water, or with the last drop of water.”

They cite this and similar reports, arguing that ablution carries a reward because of the general scope of these texts. Since there can be no reward without intention, ablution can only be performed with intention.

Abū Hanīfa says: Purification is one of the conditions of prayer, so intention is not required for it, just as it is not required for wearing clothing or removing physical impurity. The others reply: Wearing clothing and removing impurity may or may not constitute acts of worship. Thus, no text promises a person reward for wearing clothing or removing impurity as such, whereas texts do promise reward for ablution as such.

Abū Hanīfa says: The texts promise reward for ablution as ordinarily performed.

---

## Page 9396

and Muslims generally perform ablution only with intention. Ablution without intention is rare, occurring only when, for example, someone wishes to teach another person, or in similar circumstances. The majority say:

This ablution customarily performed by Muslims is the ablution prescribed by Islamic law, with which prayer is valid. No other form falls within the scope of the Lawgiver’s statements, such as the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The prayer of any one of you who breaks his state of ritual purity is not accepted until he performs ablution.”

Those addressed recognize the commanded ablution only as the ablution he praised and encouraged. They recognize no other form, so he does not intend to include any other form within the general scope of his words, nor does the text encompass it.

Section

As for intention in the sense of sincere devotion to Allah in religion, people have discussed its definition and that of sincerity. For example, some have said:

“The sincere person is one who would not mind losing all standing in people’s hearts for the sake of keeping his heart sound before Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and who does not wish people to know of even the tiniest amount of his deeds.”

They have made other fine statements of this kind. However, their words imply sincerity in all deeds, and this is not attained by everyone—indeed, not by most people. Rather, most Muslims are sincere towards Allah in many of their deeds, as they are in the deeds they have in common

---

## Page 9397

such as fasting the month of Ramadan. Most Muslims fast it for Allah. Likewise, whoever consistently observes the prayers prays only for Allah, Mighty and Majestic, unlike someone who does not maintain them, who prays only out of embarrassment, to show off, or for some worldly reason.

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, as reported by al-Tirmidhī:

“When you see a man regularly attending the mosque, testify to his faith, for Allah Most High says: ‘The mosques of Allah are only maintained by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakah, and fear none but Allah’—to the end of the verse.”

Whoever never prays without the requisite ablution or ritual bath does so only for Allah. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, as reported by Ahmad and Ibn Mājah from Thawbān:

“Remain steadfast, though you will never attain perfection. Know that the best of your deeds is prayer. Only a believer consistently maintains ablution, for ablution is a secret between the servant and Allah, Mighty and Majestic.”

A person’s ablution may be invalidated without anyone knowing. Thus, if he consistently maintains it, he does so only for Allah, glory be to Him. Someone like this can only be a believer.

Sincerity is less common in deeds whose benefit extends to others than in physical acts of worship. Hence he said in the hadith whose authenticity is agreed upon:

“Seven are those whom Allah will shade with His shade on the Day when there is no shade but His”—and the hadith continues.

---

## Page 9398

Section

The place of intention is the heart, by scholarly agreement. If someone forms an intention in his heart without expressing it verbally, that intention suffices, by their agreement. Some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī inferred from his words a legal opinion that they mistakenly attributed to him. Al-Shāfi‘ī merely distinguished prayer from entering the state of consecration for pilgrimage by noting that prayer begins with spoken words. Some mistakenly thought that he meant verbally expressing the intention, whereas he meant the opening declaration of Allah’s greatness.

Intention follows knowledge: anyone who knows what he wishes to do necessarily intends it. This is like someone who places food before himself to eat: if he knows that he wishes to eat, he necessarily intends to do so. The same applies to riding and other actions. Indeed, if people were required to perform an action without any intention, they would be required to do something beyond their ability. Whenever anyone wishes to perform an action, whether prescribed by religious law or not, awareness of it is already present in his heart, and that is intention.

If a person knows that he wishes to purify himself, pray, or fast, that awareness necessarily entails the intention to do so. An absence of intention is conceivable only when he is unaware of what he wishes to do. Examples include someone who forgets that he is in a state of major ritual impurity and bathes to clean or cool himself; someone who wishes to teach another person how to perform ablution without intending to perform ablution himself; or someone who does not know that the following day is in Ramadan and reaches the morning without having intended to fast.

---

## Page 9399

As for a Muslim who knows that tomorrow is in Ramadan and wishes to fast Ramadan, he necessarily has the intention to do so and need not express it verbally. Failure to form the intention overnight or to specify the fast as Ramadan most often occurs when there is uncertainty. For example, someone may not know whether tomorrow is in Ramadan, so he intends to fast Ramadan in general, or intends a voluntary fast, only to discover that the day is in Ramadan.

If someone verbally expresses one thing while intending something else in his heart, what counts is what is in his heart, not what he says. If he believes that the prayer time has not ended and intends to perform the prayer within its prescribed time, then discovers that its time has ended—or believes that its time has ended and intends to make it up, then discovers that its time remains—his prayer is valid by scholarly agreement.

Anyone who understands this will see that, when one knows what one is doing, intention is exceedingly simple: it requires neither obsessive doubts nor burdens and shackles. Hence some scholars said:

“Obsessive doubts arise in a person only from ignorance of religious law or impairment of the intellect.”

Scholars have disagreed over whether expressing the intention verbally is recommended. Some followers of Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad said that it is recommended, to make the intention more emphatic. Some followers of Mālik and Ahmad said that it is not recommended for that purpose; rather, expressing it verbally is a religious innovation. Nothing has been transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his Companions, or their Successors indicating that any of them verbally expressed the intention for prayer, purification, or fasting.

They argued that intention necessarily accompanies awareness of the act, so expressing it verbally is a form of mental disturbance, futility, and senseless talk. The intention is already in a person’s heart, yet he believes that it is not there, so he wishes

---

## Page 9400

to bring it about by speaking it. Yet bringing into being what already exists is impossible. This is why many people fall prey to various forms of obsessive doubt.

The scholars agree that it is not permissible to pronounce the intention aloud, whether one is leading the prayer, following an imam, or praying alone, and that repeating it is not recommended. Their disagreement concerns only whether pronouncing it quietly is disliked or recommended.

Section

The word innamā conveys restriction according to the overwhelming majority of scholars. This is something self-evident in Arabic, just as the meanings of particles of negation, interrogation, condition, and the like are understood. Scholars have, however, differed over whether it conveys restriction through its explicit wording or its implied meaning. There are two views. The majority hold that it does so through its explicit wording. The other view is held by some who recognize implied meaning as authoritative, such as al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā in one of his two positions, and by some of its more extreme opponents. These maintained that it conveys restriction, citing expressions such as Allah’s words:

“The believers are only”

Some scholars of legal theory have argued that it conveys restriction because the particle in expresses affirmation and the particle mā expresses negation; when combined, they convey both negation and affirmation.

---

## Page 9401

But this is mistaken according to scholars of Arabic, for mā here is the particle that blocks grammatical governance, not the negative particle. This blocking mā attaches to anna and its sister particles, preventing them from exercising grammatical governance. This is because governing particles are, in principle, restricted to a particular class of words: when they are restricted to nouns or verbs, without effectively forming part of the word itself, they govern those words. Thus, anna and its sister particles are restricted to nouns and therefore govern them. They are called “verb-like particles” because they assign accusative and nominative case and consist of several letters. Prepositions are restricted to nouns and therefore govern them, while conditional particles are restricted to verbs and therefore govern them. Interrogative particles, by contrast, introduce both nominal and verbal sentences and exercise no grammatical governance. The same applies to nominalizing mā.

Accordingly, grammatical analogy would also require negative mā not to exercise governance, as in the dialect of Tamīm. It does, however, exercise governance in the Hijāzī dialect, in which the Quran was revealed, as in His words, exalted is He:

“They are not their mothers,”

and:

“This is no human being.”

This usage is accepted because of its resemblance to laysa (“is not”). Here, when blocking mā attaches to inna, it removes its restriction to nouns, allowing it to introduce both nominal and verbal sentences; its grammatical governance therefore ceases. Examples include His words:

“You are only a warner,”

and His words:

“You are only being repaid for what you used to do.”

The mā following anna may also be a noun rather than a particle, as in His words:

“What they have made is a magician’s trick,”

with “trick” in the nominative. That is: what they have made is a magician’s trick. This differs from His words:

“You can only pass judgment in this worldly life.”

For the accusative reading cannot be sustained if mā means “that which.” Restriction is present in both interpretations, but if

---

## Page 9402

mā means “that which,” restriction arises from the fact that definite nouns are among the forms expressing generality. Nouns are either definite or indefinite: definite nouns express generality, as does an indefinite noun in a non-affirmative context, such as negation and the like. Thus, His words:

“What they have made is a magician’s trick”

mean: that which they have made is a magician’s trick. As for the restriction expressed by innamā (“only”), it is of the same kind as restriction through negation and exception, as in His words, exalted is He:

“You are nothing but a human being like us,”

and:

“Muhammad is no more than a messenger.”

Restriction is sometimes described as the first term being restricted to the second, and sometimes the reverse description is used. The meaning is the same: the second is affirmed of the first, while other things that might be thought to apply to it are not affirmed. This does not mean that you deny of the first everything other than the second. Thus, His words:

“You are only a warner”

mean: you are not their Lord, nor the one who calls them to account or requites them, nor their guardian. As He says:

“You are not a controller over them,”

and:

“Your duty is only to convey the message.”

“The Messiah, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger; messengers had passed away before him. His mother was a woman of truth.”

He is not a god, nor is his mother a goddess. Rather, his highest rank is that of a messenger, just as Muhammad’s highest rank is that of a messenger, and Mary’s highest rank is that of a woman of truth.

This is among the passages cited to refute the claim of some later scholars that she was a prophet. Consensus that no woman was a prophet was reported by al-Qādī Abū Bakr

---

## Page 9403

ibn al-Tayyib, al-Qādī Abū Ya'lā, the scholar Abū al-Ma'ālī al-Juwaynī, and others. Likewise, His words, exalted is He:

“Muhammad is no more than a messenger; messengers had passed away before him.”

This means that he will not remain in this world forever, immune to death or being killed. Rather, he may die or be killed, just as his fellow messengers could:

“If he dies or is killed, will you turn back on your heels?”

This verse was revealed on the day of Uhud, when it was said that Muhammad had been killed. Al-Siddīq recited it on the day the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, died. He said: “Whoever worshipped Muhammad, Muhammad has died. But whoever worshipped Allah, Allah is Ever-Living and never dies.” He then recited this verse. It was as though the people had never heard it until Abū Bakr, may Allah be pleased with him, recited it; thereafter, everyone could be heard reciting it.

Section

As for His words, exalted is He:

“The believers are only those whose hearts tremble when Allah is mentioned,”

and the rest of the verse, Allah affirms faith for these people and denies it to others, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, denied it to those mentioned in the hadiths, such as his saying:

“The adulterer is not a believer while committing adultery; the thief is not a believer while stealing; and one who drinks wine is not a believer while drinking it. So beware—beware!”

Likewise, his saying:

“There is no faith in one who cannot be trusted, and no religion in one who does not keep his covenant.”

Also in this

---

## Page 9404

category are His words, exalted is He:

“The believers are only those who believe in Allah and His Messenger, then do not doubt,”

and the rest of the verse. Likewise, His words:

“The believers are only those who believe in Allah and His Messenger and, when they are with him on a matter of common concern,”

and the rest of the verse.

People have differed over the negation expressed in these passages. The position of the great majority of the early generations, the scholars of hadith, and others is that faith is negated because some of its obligatory requirements are absent. The Lawgiver never denies a religious designation unless an obligatory requirement of it is absent.

If it is said that what is meant is the negation of perfection, then perfection is of two kinds: obligatory and recommended. Recommended perfection is illustrated by the statement of some jurists that the full ritual bath is either complete or sufficient—that is, complete in its recommended elements. This is not the perfection negated in the Lawgiver’s wording; rather, what is negated is obligatory perfection. The Lawgiver has never negated faith, prayer, fasting, purification, or other such religious designations merely because some of their recommended elements are absent. Were that so, faith would be negated for the great majority of believers. Rather, He negates it only because obligatory requirements are absent, as in the Prophet’s sayings, peace and blessings be upon him:

“There is no fast for one who has not formed the intention during the night,”

and:

“There is no prayer without the Mother of the Quran.”

Other wordings have been reported from him whose authenticity people have disputed, such as:

“There is no fast for one who has not resolved during the night to fast.”

“There is no prayer without ablution, and no ablution for one who does not mention Allah’s name over it.”

“There is no prayer for one who lives near the mosque except in the mosque.”

Anyone who considers these wordings authentically established must accept what they entail

---

## Page 9405

and thus deem obligatory what they prescribe: forming the intention during the night, mentioning Allah’s name, responding to the call to prayer, and the like.

Then, if a person omits some obligatory elements of an act of worship, should it be said that the entire act is invalid and earns him no reward? Or should it be said that he is rewarded for what he performed and punished for what he omitted? Must he repeat the act? This is determined by the scriptural evidence. Some obligatory elements of worship do not invalidate the act when omitted, nor must the person repeat it; rather, he must compensate for the omission. This applies to obligations of Hajj that are not pillars, such as stoning the pillars, or entering the state of consecration somewhere other than the prescribed station, and similar matters.

Likewise, according to the majority, including Mālik, Ahmad, and others, prayer contains obligatory elements whose omission does not invalidate it. Abū Hanīfa holds this concerning the recitation of al-Fātiha and remaining still in the prayer’s postures, as do Mālik and Ahmad concerning the first tashahhud (testimony of faith).

However, Mālik and Ahmad maintain that someone who omits any such element forgetfully must perform the prostrations of forgetfulness, whereas deliberately omitting it invalidates his prayer. Thus, deliberately omitting the first tashahhud invalidates the prayer according to the well-known position in their two schools. Mālik’s followers, however, call this an emphatic sunnah, by which they mean an obligation.

Abū Hanīfa, on the other hand, holds that whoever deliberately omits an obligatory element (*wājib*) that is not an essential requirement (*fard*) has acted wrongly but need not repeat the act. The majority say: We know of no obligatory element of worship that a person may omit without providing a substitute and without being required to repeat the act. There must therefore be an obligatory substitute for repeating it. Nevertheless, the imams agree that whoever omits

---

## Page 9406

an obligatory element of Hajj that is not a pillar, and fails to compensate for it with the sacrificial offering he owes, still has a valid Hajj and is not required to repeat it. Likewise, the majority of the early Muslims and scholars of hadith maintain that whoever omits one of faith’s obligations, where its omission does not contradict the foundations of faith, must remedy the deficiency in his faith through either repentance or good deeds that expiate sins. Major sins require repentance, while minor sins are expiated by prayer, fasting, charity, enjoining what is right, and forbidding what is wrong. If he does not do so, his faith is not nullified altogether.

Their underlying principle is that faith has constituent parts: some may be lost while others remain, as indicated by the Prophet’s words, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Whoever has an atom’s weight of faith in his heart will come out of the Fire.”

Thus, they hold that faith varies in degree and has constituent parts. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others.

As for those who denied that faith has constituent parts or varies in degree, it is as though they said: If any part of it is lost, all the rest is lost as well. They then divided into two groups. The Khārijites and the Muʿtazila said: Performing obligations and abstaining from forbidden acts are part of faith; if any of that is lost, all faith is lost. Thus, a sinful person possesses no faith whatsoever.

The Khārijites then said that he is a disbeliever, whereas the Muʿtazila said that he is neither a disbeliever nor a believer. Rather, he is a sinful person whom we place in an intermediate position between the two. They therefore differed with the Khārijites over his designation but agreed with them regarding his fate, saying that he will remain in the Fire forever and will not come out of it

---

## Page 9407

through intercession or by any other means.

The second group agreed with Ahl al-Sunnah that none of those who affirm Allah’s oneness will remain in the Fire forever. Yet they supposed that this could only be so if their faith were complete, because they believed that faith cannot be divided into parts. They therefore said: Every sinful person has complete faith, and people’s faith is identical, without differences in degree. Differences in degree concern only deeds, which are outside faith. They also said: Deeds are not part of faith, because Allah distinguishes between faith and deeds in His Book.

The recognized jurists who held this position said: Faith consists of the heart’s assent and the tongue’s profession. This is what is reported from Hammād ibn Abī Sulaymān and those who agreed with him, such as Abū Hanīfa and others. Jahm, al-Sālihī, and the speculative theologians who agreed with them, such as Abū al-Hasan and others, said that it consists solely of the heart’s assent.

The decisive distinction in this matter is that the term “faith” is sometimes mentioned on its own and sometimes alongside deeds or Islam. When mentioned on its own, it includes deeds, as in the two Sahīh collections:

“Faith has sixty-odd or seventy-odd branches. The highest is the declaration that there is no god but Allah, and the lowest is removing something harmful from the road.”

Both collections also report that he said to the delegation of ‘Abd al-Qays:

“I command you to have faith in Allah. Do you know what faith in Allah is? To testify that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, to establish prayer, to give zakat, and to pay one-fifth of the spoils you acquire.”

When faith is mentioned alongside Islam—as in the hadith in which “Gabriel asked the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, about

---

## Page 9408

faith, Islam, and excellence”—he distinguished between the two, saying: “Faith is to believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers,” and so on.

In the Musnad, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is reported to have said:

“Islam is outward, while faith is in the heart.”

Thus, when he mentioned them together, he explained that faith is in the heart, while Islam consists of outward deeds. When he mentioned faith alone, he included outward deeds within it, because they necessarily follow from what is in the heart. Whenever faith and assent to what the Messenger has conveyed become established in the heart, their necessary effects must inevitably follow. No one conceals anything within himself without Allah revealing it in his facial expressions and unguarded words. Once assent is established in the heart, action in accordance with it can never fail to follow. Complete knowledge and genuine love cannot be firmly established without producing an outward effect.

This is why Allah denies faith to those who lack its necessary consequences: the absence of a necessary consequence entails the absence of that which necessitates it. Thus, the Most High says:

“Had they believed in Allah, the Prophet, and what was revealed to him, they would not have taken them as allies.”

He also says:

“You will not find a people who believe in Allah and the Last Day maintaining affection for those who oppose Allah and His Messenger.”

And so the verse continues; other verses convey the same meaning. The outward and the inward are inseparably linked: outward conduct cannot be upright unless the inward state is upright, and when the inward state is upright, outward conduct must also be upright. This is why the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Indeed, in the body there is a piece of flesh: if it is sound, the rest of the body is sound, and if it is corrupt, the rest of the body is corrupt. Indeed, it is the heart.”

---

## Page 9409

On seeing someone fidgeting during prayer, Umar said:

“Had this man’s heart been humbly submissive, his limbs would have been likewise.”

A hadith states:

“A servant’s faith will not be upright until his tongue is upright, and his tongue will not be upright until his heart is upright.”

Thus, outward conduct is a necessary consequence of the inward state in one respect, and entails it in another. It serves as evidence of the inward state insofar as it entails it, not insofar as it is its necessary consequence. For evidence entails what it indicates: the existence of the evidence necessarily implies the existence of what it indicates, whereas the existence of a thing does not necessarily imply the existence of evidence indicating it. Evidence holds in the forward direction, but not in reverse, whereas a definition holds in both directions.

They disagreed over whether a cause must invariably hold, such that an exception or counterexample invalidates it. The correct position is that the term “cause” may refer to the complete cause—the totality of what necessarily entails the ruling. In this sense, it must invariably hold. The term may also refer to a factor that gives rise to the ruling, but whose efficacy depends upon the fulfilment of conditions and the absence of impediments. In this sense, if the ruling fails to follow from it for any other reason, the cause is invalidated.

They likewise disagreed over whether it must hold in reverse—that is, whether the absence of the ruling necessarily implies the absence of its cause.

Some said:

“It need not hold in reverse, since a ruling may have two causes.”

Others said:

“It must hold in reverse, because whenever the ruling is established in the absence of that cause, the cause has no effect upon it. Rather, the ruling is independent of it, and the absence of any effect invalidates the cause.”

Many people say

---

## Page 9410

that lack of effect invalidates a cause, while also saying that holding in reverse is not a condition for it. Others say, “This is a contradiction.”

The precise distinction is this: when a cause is absent, the particular ruling attached to it is absent—as in the position of someone who allows a similar ruling to arise from another cause. If that ruling exists without another cause, the original cause is known to have no effect and is invalidated. But if an equivalent ruling arises from another cause, that type of ruling has two causes. This is permissible.

For example, someone may argue concerning a woman who apostatizes: “She disbelieved after embracing Islam, so she is to be put to death by analogy with a man, because the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

‘It is not lawful to shed the blood of a Muslim who testifies that there is no deity but Allah except in one of three cases: a man who disbelieves after embracing Islam, commits adultery after having been married, or kills someone and is put to death in retaliation.’”

If he is then told, “Your qualification, ‘He disbelieved after embracing Islam,’ has no effect, for a man is put to death for disbelief alone, whereas a woman is not put to death for disbelief alone,” he replies:

“This cause is established by the explicit text, and by his saying:

‘Whoever changes his religion, put him to death.’

“As for the man, I do not put him to death merely for his disbelief, but for his disbelief and his boldness. That is why I do not put to death someone incapable of fighting, such as a decrepit old man or the like. Disbelief after embracing Islam, however, is another cause that makes shedding blood lawful. That is why someone incapable of fighting, such as an elderly man, is put to death for apostasy.”

This is the position of Mālik and Ahmad. If, however, he is among those who hold that disbelief alone

---

## Page 9411

permits fighting, as al-Shāfiʿī does, he says: “Disbelief alone is one cause, and disbelief after embracing Islam is another.” This is not the place to discuss these matters at length; we are merely drawing attention to them.

The point is that the meaning of the word “faith” varies depending on whether it appears alone or alongside another term. When mentioned together with deeds, it means the foundation of faith that entails deeds; when mentioned alone, it includes the necessary consequences of that foundation. Likewise, when faith is mentioned without Islam, Islam forms part of it, and every Muslim is a believer. But when Islam is mentioned together with faith, the two are distinguished from one another, as in the hadith of Gabriel and in the words of Allah Most High:

“Indeed, Muslim men and Muslim women, believing men and believing women…”

There are parallels to this in expressions such as “what is right,” “what is wrong,” “justice,” “excellence,” and others. In His words:

“He commands them to do what is right and forbids them from what is wrong,”

“what is right” includes everything commanded, and “what is wrong” includes everything prohibited. But in the words of Allah Most High:

“Indeed, prayer restrains from immorality and wrongdoing,”

He distinguishes immorality from wrongdoing. And in His words:

“And He forbids immorality, wrongdoing, and oppression,”

He distinguishes immorality and oppression from wrongdoing.

If it is said, “This is an instance of conjoining the specific to the general and the general to the specific,”

---

## Page 9412

then there are two views on this:

Some say that the specific is included in the general and is also singled out for mention, so it has been mentioned twice. Others say that singling it out for mention implies that it was not included in the general. The specific may be conjoined to the general, as in His words:

“and His angels, His messengers, and Gabriel,”

and His words:

“And when We took from the prophets their covenant, and from you,”

to the end of the verse. The general may also be conjoined to the specific, as in the words of Allah Most High:

“And He caused you to inherit their land, their homes, their wealth, and land you had not trodden.”

The root of the misconception concerning faith is that those who hold it to be indivisible say: “When a reality is composed of several things, the loss of any of its parts means that this reality ceases to exist—just as ten is composed of individual units. If we said that faith was divisible, this would entail that part of the reality could cease to exist while another part remained.”
