The answer to them is this: when some parts of a composite whole are lost, the combined form produced by their composition ceases to exist. But it does not follow that all the remaining parts must cease to exist. Faith, composed of obligatory statements and obligatory deeds, both inward and outward, is the complete whole that is required. This combined form ceases to exist when some of its parts are lost. It is this that is negated in the Quran and Sunnah, as in his saying:

“The adulterer does not commit adultery…”

The words of Allah Most High convey the same meaning:

“The believers are only those who believe in Allah and His Messenger and then do not doubt,”

to the end of the passage. Yet it does not follow that all the other parts cease to exist, or that the remaining parts are no longer part of faith once some of it has been lost. Likewise, the obligatory acts of Hajj are part of Hajj in its complete, obligatory form; and if they are lost, so is

---

## Page 9413

this completeness, though the rest of Hajj is not lost. Likewise, the term “complete human being” encompasses all a person’s limbs. Yet if his hands and feet were amputated, he would still be called a human being, even though he had lost some of what the term encompasses in its complete sense. Similarly, terms such as “tree,” “door,” “house,” “wall,” and others apply to what they designate both when all its parts are intact and after some of its parts have been lost.

This resolves the objection raised against al-Shāfiʿī by al-Rāzī and those who followed him, such as al-Asbahānī and others. Al-Shāfiʿī’s position on this is that of the majority of the scholars of hadith and the early generations. These critics have raised this same flawed objection against the early generations.

Faith also varies in degree according to what the Lawgiver prescribes. What Allah commands of one servant is not identical to what He commands of another, nor is the faith required of one servant identical to that required of another. In the early days of Islam, a man could be a believer whose faith was complete and who deserved reward if he fulfilled what Allah and His Messenger had made obligatory upon him—even though he had not attained the higher degree of assent to what had not yet been revealed of the Quran, had not fasted Ramadan, and had not performed Hajj to the House. Likewise, someone in our own time who embraces faith fully and dies before the time for any prayer becomes due upon him dies having fulfilled all the faith required of him, and deserves reward for that faith.

---

## Page 9414

But once further Quranic revelation has come down, Allah and His Messenger have imposed additional obligations, and a person is able to fulfil them, he no longer deserves reward merely on the basis of what previously entitled him to it. Hence these scholars say: “He is no longer a believer merely by virtue of what previously made him a believer.” This is because the faith prescribed for one person is greater than that prescribed for another. Likewise, someone able to perform Hajj has an obligation that someone unable to perform it does not, and a person of wealth owes zakat that a poor person does not. There are many similar examples.

As for variations in faith on the servant’s part, one person may surpass another in affirmation and deeds. Everyone knows that the states of the heart vary in degree. Indeed, a person finds within himself, at some times more than at others, greater love for Allah and His Messenger, greater fear of Allah, greater hope in His mercy, greater reliance upon Him, and greater sincerity.

Likewise, knowledge and assent vary in degree, according to the sounder of the two views. This is also the sounder of the two positions reported from Ahmad. More than one Companion, including ʿUmar ibn Habīb al-Khatmī and others, said:

“Faith increases and decreases. When we remember Allah, praise Him, and glorify Him, that is its increase; when we become heedless, forget, and neglect our duties, that is its decrease.”

For this reason, qualifying one’s profession of faith is recommended. Many of the early generations—the Companions, the Successors, and others—qualified their professions of faith, while others rejected such qualification

---

## Page 9415

and said: “This is doubt.” Among those who qualified their professions of faith, some considered it obligatory, while others did not, allowing its omission according to the distinction between two situations. This is the soundest view. Both positions are found in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

Someone who qualifies his profession because he does not know that he is failing to fulfil the obligations as Allah and His Messenger have commanded has done well. The same applies to someone who qualifies it because he does not know his ultimate outcome, or who does so to make the matter dependent upon Allah’s will, not out of doubt. Someone who makes a definite statement about his present inner state has also done well—for example, someone who knows that he has testified that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, and thus affirms what he knows with certainty to be present within himself.

Many disputes concerning faith, religious designations, and rulings are merely verbal disputes. Once the matter is clearly distinguished, uncertainty disappears. Allah, glorified be He, knows best what is correct.

Section

The Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“Whoever’s migration is to Allah and His Messenger, his migration is to Allah and His Messenger,”

are not a mere tautology. Rather, they explain that whoever intends something by his deed receives what he intended. That is, whoever seeks Allah and His Messenger through his migration obtains what he sought, whereas whoever intends to migrate for worldly gain or a woman receives only that. This elaborates on his words:

“Deeds are only according to intentions.”

---

## Page 9416

And when he stated that each person would have what he intended, he explained that one would have what he intended, and the other would have what he intended.

The word hijra (migration) derives from hajr (abandonment). It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The migrant is one who abandons what Allah has forbidden, and the one who strives in Allah’s cause is one who strives against his own self for Allah’s sake.”

Likewise, he said:

“The Muslim is one from whose tongue and hand Muslims are safe, and the believer is one whom people trust with their lives and property.”

Here he explains what it means to fulfil this designation completely, as when he said:

“The truly needy person is not this one who goes around begging,”

and so on. This may resemble his saying:

“Whom do you consider bankrupt among you?”

They replied, “Someone who has neither a dirham nor a dinar.”

He said, “That is not the bankrupt person. Rather, the bankrupt person is someone who comes on the Day of Resurrection with good deeds like mountains, but who has struck this person, insulted that person, and taken another’s property. This person is then given some of his good deeds, and that person some of his good deeds. When he has no good deeds left, some of their sins are taken and cast upon him; then he is cast into the Fire.”

He also said:

“Whom do you consider childless among you?”

They replied, “Someone who has no children.”

He said, “The childless person is one who has not had any of his children precede him in death.”

Similar to this is his saying:

“The strong person is not the one who overpowers others in wrestling; rather, the strong person is one who controls himself when angry.”

These hadiths, however, have a particular purpose: to explain what is more deserving of designations of praise and blame than the things people suppose. Bankruptcy entails need, which is something disliked. He therefore explained that true need is that experienced on the Day of Resurrection. Likewise, people dislike being without children because they lack a child who can benefit them. He therefore explained that true benefit from one’s children is only attained in the Hereafter, by one who

---

## Page 9417

has had his children precede him in death. Likewise, strength and power are valued, so he explained that strength of character is more deserving of praise than bodily strength: it consists in controlling oneself when angry. As one of the Arab leaders was asked:

“Why do your slaves show greater endurance than you in war and at work?”

He replied, “They have greater bodily endurance, while we have greater self-control.”

As for his statement concerning the designation “Muslims,” it is of the same kind as his statements concerning the Muslim, the believer, the migrant, and the one who strives in Allah’s cause. This accords with what was explained earlier: the Lawgiver negates a designation established by the Sharia only when the completeness required for it is lacking. Abandoning what Allah has forbidden is obligatory, and keeping Muslims safe from one’s aggression, whether by tongue or hand, is obligatory. As for the believer whom people trust with their lives and property, people can only trust someone if he is trustworthy, and trustworthiness is obligatory.

The needy person who does not beg and whose circumstances are unknown is more deserving of assistance than someone who makes his need known and asks for help. Giving to him is obligatory, and it is impermissible to give exclusively to the beggar while leaving this person out. Rather, giving preference to the one who does not beg is more fitting, more binding, and more beloved.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no migration after the conquest, but striving in Allah’s cause and intention remain. When you are called upon to mobilize, mobilize.”

He also said:

“Migration will not cease as long as the enemy is fought.”

Both statements are true. In the first, he meant the migration familiar in his time: migration to Medina from Mecca and elsewhere in Arabia. This migration was prescribed when Mecca and other places were lands of disbelief and war, while Medina was the home of faith. Migration from the land of disbelief to the land of Islam was therefore obligatory for anyone able to undertake it. Then, when Mecca was conquered and became a land of Islam, and the Arabs embraced Islam,

---

## Page 9418

all this land became a land of Islam, so he said:

“There is no migration after the conquest.”

A land’s being a land of disbelief, a land of faith, or a land of sinful people is not an inherent attribute of it. Rather, it is a contingent attribute determined by its inhabitants. Any land inhabited by believers who fear Allah is, at that time, the land of Allah’s allies. Any land inhabited by disbelievers is, at that time, a land of disbelief. Any land inhabited by sinful people is, at that time, a land of sin. If people other than those we have mentioned settle there, replacing its former inhabitants, it becomes their land.

Likewise, if a mosque is converted into a tavern, becomes a place of sin or oppression, or becomes a church in which partners are associated with Allah, its status is determined by its occupants. Similarly, if a place of drinking, sin, or the like is made into a mosque in which Allah, the Majestic and Mighty, is worshipped, its status changes accordingly. Likewise, a righteous man may become sinful, a disbeliever may become a believer, or a believer may become a disbeliever, and so forth. In each case, the status changes as circumstances shift from one state to another. Allah Most High has said:

“Allah presents the example of a town that was safe and secure.”

The verse was revealed concerning Mecca when it was a land of disbelief, although Mecca itself remained the best of Allah’s lands and the most beloved of them to Him. The reference was only to its inhabitants.

Al-Tirmidhī narrated a report traced back to the Prophet, stating that he addressed Mecca while standing at al-Hazwarah:

“By Allah, you are the best of Allah’s lands and the most beloved of Allah’s lands to Allah. Had my people not driven me out of you, I would not have left.”

Another version reads:

“The best of Allah’s lands and the most beloved of Allah’s lands to me.”

Thus he made clear that it is the land most beloved to Allah and His Messenger. Yet his residence in Medina and that of

---

## Page 9419

the believers with him were better than their residence in Mecca, because Medina was the land to which they had migrated. Thus, standing guard at the frontiers was better than residing in Mecca or Medina, as established in the authentic hadith collection:

“Standing guard for a day and a night in Allah’s cause is better than fasting and praying at night for a month. Whoever dies while standing guard dies striving in Allah’s cause; the reward of his deeds continues to accrue to him, his provision from Paradise continues to be granted, and he is safe from the one who tests him.”

The Sunan collections also relate from ‘Uthmān that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“One day spent standing guard in Allah’s cause is better than a thousand days spent in any other place.”

Abū Hurayrah said: “I would rather stand guard for one night in Allah’s cause than spend the Night of Decree praying beside the Black Stone.”

Thus, the best land for each individual is the land in which he is most obedient to Allah and His Messenger. This varies according to circumstances; there is no single land in which residing is best for everyone. Rather, what is best for each individual depends on his fear of Allah, obedience, humility, submission, and attentiveness of heart.

Abū al-Dardā’ once wrote to Salmān: “Come to the Holy Land.” Salmān wrote back: “The land does not sanctify anyone; rather, it is a servant’s deeds that sanctify him.”

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had established a bond of brotherhood between Salmān and Abū al-Dardā’.”

Salmān had a deeper understanding than Abū al-Dardā’ in several matters, including this one. Allah Most High said to Moses, peace be upon him:

“I will show you the abode of the defiantly disobedient.”

This was the land inhabited by those Amalekites, which subsequently became the land of the believers. It is the land referred to in the Quran, comprising the Holy Land

---

## Page 9420

and the land of Egypt, which Allah caused the Children of Israel to inherit. The conditions of lands are like those of people: a man may be a Muslim at one time and an unbeliever at another; a believer at one time and a hypocrite at another; righteous and God-fearing at one time, defiantly disobedient at another, and wicked and wretched at yet another.

Likewise, places take their character from their inhabitants. A person’s migration from a place of unbelief and sin to a place of faith and obedience is like his repentance and his transition from unbelief and sin to faith and obedience. This continues until the Day of Resurrection. Allah Most High said:

“And those who believed afterwards, emigrated, and strove alongside you—they are of you.”

A group of the early generations said: This includes everyone who believes, emigrates, and strives until the Day of Resurrection. Likewise, His words, Most High:

“For those who emigrated after being put to trial, then strove and remained steadfast—your Lord thereafter is surely All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

The meaning of this verse includes everyone whom Satan has tempted away from his religion or caused to fall into sin, but who then abandons evil deeds, strives against his own self and other enemies, strives against the hypocrites by enjoining what is right, forbidding what is wrong, and other means, and patiently endures whatever befalls him in words or deeds. Allah—glorified and exalted is He—knows best.

---

## Page 9421

He also said:

Section

The three expressions of remembrance contained in the address narrated by Ibn Mas‘ūd and others—“Praise be to Allah; we seek His help and His forgiveness”—are those reportedly described by Shaykh ‘Abd al-Qādir, and later by Abū al-Hasan al-Shādhilī, as comprehensive expressions of beneficial speech. They are: “Praise be to Allah,” “I seek Allah’s forgiveness,” and “There is no might or power except through Allah.”

This is because the servant is concerned with two kinds of action: what Allah does for him—His blessings bestowed upon him, which call for gratitude—and what he himself does, whether good or evil. Doing good requires Allah’s assistance, so he needs to seek His help; wrongdoing requires seeking forgiveness to erase its effects.

The hadith of Dimād al-Azdī contains only:

“Praise be to Allah; we praise Him and seek His help.”

This accords with the Opening of the Book, which is divided into two halves: one for the Lord and one for the servant. The Lord’s half opens with praise of Allah, while the servant’s half opens with seeking His help. Hence the words: “We praise Him and seek His help.”

Praise may also be coupled with seeking forgiveness, as in the report narrated by Ahmad in al-Zuhd (Asceticism): “A man lived in the time of

---

## Page 9422

al-Hasan, and was told: ‘We received this address from our father, who received it from his father, as many people recite it: “Praise be to Allah; we praise Him, seek His help, seek His guidance, and seek His forgiveness. We seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds.”’”

The words “we praise Him and seek His help” occur in the hadith of Dimād, while “we seek His help and His forgiveness” occur in the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd. As for “we seek His guidance,” this is found in the Opening of the Book: half of it belongs to the Lord and consists of praise, while half belongs to the servant and consists of seeking help and guidance. It contains no request for forgiveness, since that arises only in connection with sin. This surah is the foundation of faith, and al-Fātihah is the gateway to happiness that protects against sins. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, prayer restrains one from shameful and wrongful deeds.”

“Ibn ‘Abbās reported that Dimād came to Mecca. He belonged to Azd Shanū’ah and used to perform healing recitations for this affliction. He heard some foolish people in Mecca saying, ‘Muhammad is mad.’ He said, ‘If only I could see this man, perhaps Allah would heal him through me.’

He met him and said, ‘O Muhammad, I perform healing recitations for this affliction, and Allah heals through me whomever He wills. Would you like me to do so?’

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘All praise be to Allah; we praise Him and seek His help. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. To proceed.’

Dimād said, ‘Repeat these words of yours to me.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, repeated them to him three times. Then Dimād said:

---

## Page 9423

‘I have heard the words of soothsayers, sorcerers, and poets, but I have never heard anything like these words of yours. Indeed, I have reached the nāʿūs (depths) of the sea.’ He then said, ‘Give me your hand so that I may pledge allegiance to you upon Islam.’ He pledged allegiance to him, and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘And on behalf of your people?’ He replied, ‘And on behalf of my people.’” Muslim narrated this in his Sahīh.

For this reason, beginning with this prescribed Prophetic address is recommended and has been practised when addressing people on matters of knowledge, in both general and specific settings: teaching the Book and the Sunnah and fostering an understanding of them, exhorting people, and debating with them.

The scholars of our time whom we met and studied under, as well as others, would begin their lessons in Quranic commentary or jurisprudence, in congregational mosques, schools, and elsewhere, with a different address, such as:

“Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless Muhammad, the Seal of the Messengers, and all his family and Companions. May Allah be pleased with us, with you, with our teachers, and with all Muslims”—or, “with the esteemed people present and all Muslims.”

Likewise, I have seen people deliver marriage addresses other than the prescribed address, each group having a form different from that of others. Yet the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd did not single out marriage. Rather, this is an address for every occasion on which people need to address one another, and marriage is one such occasion.

Observing the prescribed Sunnah in speech and action, in all acts of worship and everyday practices, constitutes complete adherence to the straight path. As for anything else, even if it is not

---

## Page 9424

forbidden, it is nevertheless deficient and inferior, for the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The precise point is that the words:

“Praise be to Allah. We seek His help and His forgiveness”

are comprehensive, as in the Prophetic hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd, which mentions this and that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was granted comprehensive expressions, their conclusions and their openings, as in Ubayy’s two surahs. Seeking guidance is included in seeking help, while repeating “We praise Him” is unnecessary after saying “Praise be to Allah.” Elaborating, however, is permissible, as in the qunūt supplication:

“O Allah, we seek Your help, Your guidance and Your forgiveness. We believe in You and rely upon You. We offer You every good word of praise. We thank You and are not ungrateful to You. We renounce and abandon whoever disobeys You.”

This is one of Ubayy’s two surahs. It opens with seeking help, which, together with what follows it in the Opening of the Book, constitutes the servant’s half. The second surah reads:

“O Allah, You alone we worship; to You we pray and prostrate. Towards You we strive, hastening to serve You. We hope for Your mercy and fear Your punishment. Indeed, Your severe punishment will overtake the disbelievers.”

This opens with worship, which, together with what precedes it in the Opening of the Book, constitutes the Lord’s half. Thus, the two surahs of qunūt correspond to the Opening of the Book, and both correspond to the address for occasions of need. All of this belongs to the openings, comprehensive expressions and conclusions of speech.

As for his words:

“We seek refuge in Allah from the evils within ourselves and from our evil deeds,”

there are two kinds of things from which refuge is sought. One is something already present: refuge is sought from its harm, which has not

---

## Page 9425

yet occurred. The other is something not yet present, from whose coming into existence refuge is sought, since its very existence is harmful. An example of the first is:

“I seek refuge in Allah from Satan, the accursed.”

Examples of the second are:

“My Lord, I seek refuge in You from the promptings of the devils, and I seek refuge in You, my Lord, lest they be present with me.”

And:

“O Allah, I seek refuge in You lest I go astray or be led astray, slip or be made to slip.”

As for His words:

“Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of daybreak, from the evil of what He has created, and from the evil of darkness when it falls, and from the evil of those who blow on knots, and from the evil of an envier when he envies,”

they encompass both kinds: refuge is sought from existing evil so that it may cause no harm, and from harmful evil not yet present so that it may not come into existence. Thus, the words in the hadith:

“We seek refuge in Allah from the evils within ourselves”

admit both meanings: “We seek refuge in Allah lest any evil arise from ourselves,” and “We seek refuge in Allah lest their evil afflict us.” The latter is more likely, and Allah knows best.

As for his words:

“And from the evil consequences of our deeds,”

the evils referred to are the punishments for those deeds, as in His words:

“The evil consequences of what they plotted.”

For good things and evil things often mean blessings and afflictions, just as they can mean acts of obedience and disobedience. If, however, the expression is understood to mean evil deeds—that is, acts of disobedience—then he has sought refuge lest he commit evil deeds or lest they harm him. On the former interpretation, which is more likely, he has sought refuge lest the punishment for his deeds befall him. This is the more likely meaning.

---

## Page 9426

Thus, the hadith encompasses seeking refuge from harm both at its source and in its ultimate outcome. For the source of harm is the evil within the soul, while its ultimate outcome is the punishment for sin. On this understanding, he sought refuge from harm that has not yet occurred, though its cause is already present, asking that it not come to pass. For the soul has a propensity for evil, and deeds warrant punishment. He therefore sought refuge lest the evil within his soul come to pass or the punishment for his deeds befall him.

It may, however, be said that evil is the disposition inherent in the soul that gives rise to sins. This disposition already exists, just as Satan exists. He therefore sought refuge from it lest it harm or afflict him, just as one says:

“I seek refuge in Allah from Satan, the accursed.”

If, however, it is understood as the evils that actually occur—namely, the sins that proceed from the soul—then this constitutes a third category.

---

## Page 9427

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Concerning the Prophet’s statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) in the authentic hadith:

“Islam began as something strange and will return to being strange as it began, so glad tidings to the strangers.”

This does not imply that when Islam becomes strange, it is permissible to abandon it—Allah forbid! Rather, the matter is as Allah Most High says:

“Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, the religion in the sight of Allah is Islam.”

And Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah as He should be feared, and do not die except as Muslims.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Who would turn away from the religion of Abraham except one who makes a fool of himself? We certainly chose him in this world, and in the Hereafter he will surely be among the righteous.”

“When his Lord said to him, ‘Submit,’ he said, ‘I have submitted to the Lord of all worlds.’”

“Abraham enjoined this upon his sons, as did Jacob: ‘O my sons, Allah has chosen this religion for you, so do not die except as Muslims.’”

---

## Page 9428

We have discussed this at length elsewhere and explained that the religion of all the prophets, from Noah to the Messiah, was Islam. Thus, when Islam began as something strange, no other religion was acceptable. Indeed, in the authentic hadith narrated by ‘Iyād ibn Himār, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Allah looked upon the people of the earth and detested them—Arabs and non-Arabs alike—except for some remaining followers of the Scripture.”

The hadith continues. Nor does this imply that, when Islam becomes strange, those who hold fast to it will fare badly. Rather, such a person is the most fortunate of people, as the Prophet said at the end of the hadith:

“So glad tidings to the strangers.”

Tūbā (“glad tidings”) is derived from the word for goodness. Allah Most High says:

“Bliss shall be theirs, and a beautiful place of return.”

For such a person will be like the earliest forerunners who followed Islam when it was strange. They are the most fortunate of people. In the Hereafter, they hold the highest ranks after the prophets, peace be upon them. As for this world, Allah Most High says:

“O Prophet, Allah is sufficient for you and for the believers who follow you.”

That is, Allah is sufficient for you and sufficient for your followers. Allah Most High also says:

“Indeed, my Protector is Allah, who sent down the Book, and He protects the righteous.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Is not

---

## Page 9429

Allah sufficient for His servant?” And He says:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him and provide for him from where he does not expect. And whoever puts his trust in Allah, He is sufficient for him.”

Thus, for the Muslim who follows the Messenger, Allah Most High is sufficient: He meets his needs and is his Protector, wherever and whenever he may be. Hence, Muslims who hold fast to Islam in lands of unbelief are found to enjoy greater happiness the more fully they adhere to Islam. If harm befalls them, it is because of their sins. Indeed, when polytheists and the People of the Scripture see a Muslim who faithfully practices Islam, they revere and honor him and exempt him from the tasks they impose on those who profess Islam outwardly without putting its reality into practice; the latter are not honored.

Such was the situation of Muslims in the early days of Islam, and so it has been in every age. People inevitably experience harm in this world, and Allah bestows blessings upon His servants. Yet the harm that befalls the Muslim is less, and the blessings he receives are greater. Thus, although Muslims in the early days of Islam were tried by the harm inflicted by the unbelievers and by being driven from their homes, the destruction that befell the unbelievers was far greater. Whatever honor or wealth the unbelievers attained, the Muslims attained more—even from outsiders.

---

## Page 9430

Thus, although the polytheists sought every means of harming the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Allah defended him, honored him, protected him, and granted him victory, so that he was the most honored of Quraysh. Each of the others faced someone who harmed and humiliated him whom he could not fend off, for every leading man had another who rivaled him, opposed him, and showed him enmity. Such is the condition of those who do not follow Islam: they fear one another and pin their hopes on one another.

His followers who emigrated to Abyssinia were received by its king with the utmost honor and esteem. Those who emigrated to Medina enjoyed still greater honor and strength. Whatever worldly harm they suffered, they were immediately compensated for it with faith, its sweetness, and its delight, which enabled them to endure that harm. Their enemies suffered many times as much harm and evil, without compensation either in the Hereafter or in this life, for they were being punished for their sins. The believers, by contrast, were being tested so that their faith might be purified and their sins expiated.

This is because the believer acts for Allah’s sake. If he suffers harm, he seeks Allah’s reward for enduring it. If he expends effort or wealth, he does so for Allah’s sake and seeks his reward from Allah.

---

## Page 9431

Faith has a sweetness and delight in the heart that nothing whatsoever can equal. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever possesses three qualities will experience through them the sweetness of faith: that Allah and His Messenger are dearer to him than all else; that he loves a person solely for Allah’s sake; and that he hates to return to disbelief, after Allah has rescued him from it, as much as he hates to be cast into the Fire.”

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim both recorded this in their Sahīh collections. In Sahīh Muslim, it is narrated:

“He has tasted the sweetness of faith who is content with Allah as his Lord, Islam as his religion, and Muhammad as his Prophet.”

Just as Allah forbade His Prophet to feel grief or distress over those who did not enter Islam in its early days, the same applies in its later days. The believer is forbidden to grieve over them or be distressed by their plotting. Many people, when they see wrongdoing or widespread changes in the condition of Islam, become distraught, lose heart, and wail as those stricken by calamity do. Yet this is forbidden to them. Rather, the believer is commanded to be patient, put his trust in Allah, remain steadfast upon the religion of Islam, and believe in Allah together with those who fear Him and those who do good. He is also to believe that the final outcome favors the God-fearing, and that whatever befalls him is because of his sins. Let him therefore be patient, for Allah’s promise is true; let him seek forgiveness for his sin and glorify his Lord with praise in the evening and the morning.

The Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“Then it will return to being a stranger, just as it began,”

allow for two interpretations:

---

## Page 9432

The first is that, in certain places and at certain times, it will again become a stranger among people, then prevail, just as it was a stranger at the outset and then prevailed. This is why he said:

“It will return to being a stranger, just as it began.”

When it began, it was a stranger, unknown; then it prevailed and became known. Likewise, it will again become unknown, then prevail and become known. Thus, during the intervening periods, those who recognize it will be few, just as they were at the beginning.

It may also mean that, toward the end of this world, only a few Muslims will remain. This will happen only after the Dajjāl and Gog and Magog, when the Hour draws near. Allah will then send a wind that takes the soul of every believing man and woman, after which the Resurrection will come. Before that, however, he said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“A group of my community will continue to prevail, upholding the truth, unharmed by those who oppose them or those who abandon them, until the Hour comes.”

This hadith is in the two Sahīh collections, and similar reports are transmitted through several chains. The truthful and trustworthy one has thus informed us that a group of his community will continue to uphold the truth, unassailable and strong, unharmed by their opponents or by the opposition of those who abandon them. As for Islam remaining a humiliated stranger throughout the entire earth before the Hour, this will not happen.

As for his words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“Then it will return to being a stranger, just as it began,”

the greatest

---

## Page 9433

estrangement it can experience occurs when those who have embraced it renounce it. Allah Most High has said:

“Whoever among you renounces his religion, Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble toward the believers, firm against the disbelievers, striving in Allah’s cause and fearing no one’s reproach.”

These people uphold it when those others renounce it. Likewise, it began as a stranger and continued to grow stronger until it spread. Thus, it becomes a stranger in many places and times, then prevails until Allah, Mighty and Majestic, establishes it. When Umar ibn Abd al-Azīz came to power, for example, much of Islam had become unfamiliar to many people, to the point that some did not know that wine was forbidden. Through him, Allah restored to prominence those aspects of Islam that had become unfamiliar.

The Sunan collections relate:

“Allah will send this community, at the beginning of every hundred years, someone who will renew its religion for it.”

Renewal takes place only after the teachings have faded, and that is what Islam’s estrangement means. This hadith teaches the Muslim not to grieve over how few people understand the true nature of Islam, not to be distressed by that, and not to doubt the religion of Islam—just as was the case when it first began. Allah Most High said:

“If you are in doubt about what We have revealed to you, ask those who have been reading the Scripture before you.”

There are other such verses

---

## Page 9434

and proofs demonstrating the truth of Islam. Likewise, when Islam becomes a stranger, those who adhere to it need evidence and proofs like those needed at its beginning. Allah said to him:

“Shall I seek a judge other than Allah, when it is He who has sent down to you the Book, explained in detail? Those to whom We gave the Scripture know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth, so never be among the doubters.”

“The word of your Lord has been fulfilled in truth and justice. None can change His words, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing.”

“If you obey most of those on earth, they will lead you away from Allah’s path. They follow nothing but conjecture and do nothing but guess.”

Allah Most High also said:

“Or do you think that most of them hear or understand? They are only like cattle; rather, they are even further astray.”

This estrangement may affect some of Islam’s prescribed teachings, and it may occur in certain places. In many places, some of its teachings are so little known that Islam becomes a stranger among the people there, recognized by only a few isolated individuals. Yet blessed is whoever holds fast to those teachings as Allah and His Messenger have commanded. Making them manifest, enjoining them, and reproving those who contravene them depends on one’s strength and the support available.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand. If he is unable, then with his tongue; if he is unable, then with his heart

---

## Page 9435

; beyond that there is not even a mustard seed of faith.”

If it should be the case that someone suffers harm in this world and the Hereafter, contrary to what Allah promised His Messenger and his followers, this is due to his sins and the deficiency of his Islam—as with the defeat that befell them at Uhud. For Allah Most High has said:

“We will surely support Our messengers and those who believe, both in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.”

And He Most High said:

“Our word has already gone forth to Our servants, the messengers:

They will surely be victorious,

And Our army will surely prevail.”

In Allah Most High’s accounts of the prophets and their followers, their victory and deliverance, and the destruction of their enemies, there is a lesson. And Allah knows best.

If it is said:

His words, blessed and exalted is He,

“Whoever among you turns back from his religion—Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him,”

are addressed to that generation, as are His words:

“Allah has promised those among you who believe and do righteous deeds that He will surely grant them succession in the land, just as He granted it to those before them.”

That is why the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, explained that they were the people of Yemen who embraced Islam when some of the Arabs apostatized. This is further indicated by the fact that, at the very end, no believer will remain.

The answer is:

His words, blessed and exalted is He,

“O you who believe,”

are addressed to everyone who

---

## Page 9436

is a believer and has received the Quran, just like all other addresses of this kind, such as His words, Most High:

“O you who believe, when you rise to pray,”

and similar passages. The same applies to His words, Most High:

“Allah has promised those among you who believe.”

Both promises have been fulfilled and continue to be fulfilled, just as Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has foretold. No group has ever apostatized from Islam without Allah bringing forth a people whom He loves, who strive in its defense. They are the group granted victory until the Hour comes.

This is made clear by the fact that He mentioned this in the context of prohibiting allegiance to the disbelievers. He, Most High, said:

“O you who believe, do not take the Jews and the Christians as allies. They are allies of one another. Whoever among you takes them as allies is indeed one of them. Indeed, Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.

“You see those in whose hearts is a disease hastening to ally themselves with them, saying, ‘We fear that a turn of fortune may strike us.’ But perhaps Allah will bring victory or a decree from Him, and then they will become regretful over what they concealed within themselves.”

Then, further on, He says:

“O you who believe, whoever among you turns back from his religion—Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him.”

Thus, those addressed by the prohibition against taking the Jews and Christians as allies are the very same people addressed by the verse concerning apostasy. It is well known that this encompasses every generation of the Muslim community. Having forbidden allegiance to the disbelievers and explained that whoever among those addressed gives them allegiance is one of them, He then explained that whoever gives them allegiance and apostatizes from the religion of Islam does not harm Islam in the least.

---

## Page 9437

Rather, Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him. They will give their allegiance to the believers rather than the disbelievers and strive in the way of Allah, fearing no one’s reproach, as He said at the outset:

“But if these disbelieve in it, We have entrusted it to a people who do not disbelieve in it.”

Neither those who never entered Islam nor those who left it after entering it harm Islam in the least. Rather, Allah raises up those who believe in what His Messenger brought and support His religion until the Hour comes. The people of Yemen were among those whom Allah brought forth when some people apostatized in those days. The verse is not restricted to them, nor does the hadith contain anything requiring such a restriction. Indeed, Allah has foretold that He will bring forth people other than the people of Yemen, such as the sons of Persia; the promise is not exclusive to them.

Indeed, He, Most High, said:

“O you who believe, what is the matter with you that, when you are told to go forth in the way of Allah, you cling heavily to the earth? Are you content with the life of this world rather than the Hereafter? Yet the enjoyment of this worldly life is but little compared with the Hereafter.”

“If you do not go forth, He will punish you with a painful punishment and replace you with another people. You will not harm Him at all, and Allah has power over all things.”

This too addresses every generation. In it, He declares that whoever shrinks from the jihad he has been commanded to undertake will be punished and replaced by someone who will undertake it. This is precisely what happens.

Likewise, He says in the other verse:

“Here you are, called upon to spend in the way of Allah, yet some of you are miserly. Whoever is miserly is so only to his own detriment. And Allah

---

## Page 9438

is free of need, while you are in need. If you turn away, He will replace you with another people, and they will not be like you.”

The Most High has thus declared that whoever turns away from striving personally in jihad or from spending in the way of Allah will be replaced. Such is the case of the cowardly miser: Allah replaces him with someone who supports Islam and spends in its cause. What, then, when someone abandons Islam itself? Allah brings forth a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble towards the believers and mighty against the disbelievers, striving in the way of Allah and fearing no one’s reproach.

This is found among people devoted to knowledge, worship, fighting, and wealth. Among all four groups are believers who strive and are granted victory until the Hour comes, just as among them are those who apostatize or shrink from jihad and spending. Likewise, the Most High says:

“Allah has promised those of you who believe and do righteous deeds that He will surely grant them succession in the land.”

This promise applies to everyone who possesses these qualities. When the earliest Muslims possessed them, Allah granted them succession as He had promised. People after them have also possessed these qualities in proportion to their faith and righteous deeds. The more complete a person’s faith and righteous conduct, the more fully this promised succession is realized for him. If there is deficiency and weakness in him, there will be deficiency and weakness in his establishment in power. That is because this is the reward for such deeds: whoever performs them deserves that reward.

---

## Page 9439

But no later generation has equalled the first, so naturally none has attained the same degree of power. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The best generation is the one among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

Yet some members of a generation may attain this, just as some Muslims attain it in certain regions, as is well known in every age.

As for his statement, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Allah will send a wind that takes the soul of every believer,”

this involves no apostasy, but rather the death of the believers. He did not say that whenever a believer dies, Allah will replace him with another; rather, He promised this if some of them apostatize from their religion. This is among the proofs that the Muslim community will neither unanimously agree upon misguidance nor apostatize in its entirety. Rather, Allah will surely preserve believers who remain triumphant until the Hour comes. When every believer has died, the Hour will have come.

This is like the hadith concerning knowledge:

“Allah does not take away knowledge by stripping it from people. Rather, He takes away knowledge by taking away the scholars. When no scholar remains, people take ignorant men as leaders. They are asked questions and give religious rulings without knowledge, going astray themselves and leading others astray.”

This hadith is well known in the collections of authentic hadith, narrated by ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.

---

## Page 9440

If it is objected: In the report from Ibn Masʿūd and others, he says:

“The Quran will be taken away overnight, so that not a single verse of it remains in the written copies or in people’s hearts.”

This contradicts what has been stated.

The answer is: That is not so. Taking away knowledge is not the same as taking away the Quran, as demonstrated by the other hadith:

“This is the time when knowledge will be taken away.”

One of the Ansār asked, “How can it be taken away when we have recited the Quran and taught our wives and children to recite it?”

He replied, “May your mother be bereaved of you! I had certainly thought you among the most knowledgeable people of Medina. Do not the Jews and Christians possess the Torah and the Gospel? Yet what benefit does that bring them?”

Thus, merely continuing to preserve the Scripture in memory does not necessarily entail possessing this knowledge. In particular, the Quran is recited by hypocrites and believers alike, and by the unlettered person who knows nothing of the Scripture beyond reciting it.

Al-Hasan al-Basrī said:

“Knowledge is of two kinds: knowledge in the heart and knowledge on the tongue. Knowledge in the heart is beneficial knowledge, while knowledge on the tongue is Allah’s proof against His servants.”

When Allah takes away the scholars, those who recite the Quran without knowledge will remain. It will then be taken away overnight from the written copies and from people’s hearts.

If it is objected: In the hadith of Hudhayfah recorded in the two Sahīh collections, he told them about the removal of the trust, saying:

“A man will sleep, and the trust will be taken from his heart, leaving a trace like a small spot. Then he will sleep again, and the trust will be taken from his heart, and there remains

---

## Page 9441

a trace like the mark of a blister, as when you roll an ember over your foot and see a swelling, yet there is nothing inside it.”

The answer is:

The removal of trust and faith is not the same as the removal of knowledge. A person may be granted faith while lacking knowledge. Such faith may be taken from his heart, as happened to the faith of the Children of Israel when they saw the calf. But whoever has been granted knowledge together with faith will not have that knowledge taken from his heart. Such a person never apostatizes from Islam, unlike someone who merely possesses the Quran or faith alone, for these may be taken away. This is what actually occurs.

Yet we most often find apostasy among those who possess the Quran without knowledge and faith, or those who possess faith without knowledge and the Quran. As for someone who has been granted the Quran and faith, and thereby acquired knowledge, this will not be taken from his heart. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 9442

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

As for the Prophet’s saying (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him):

“My nation is like rain: one cannot tell whether its beginning or its end is better.”

Ahmad narrated this in al-Musnad. Some scholars declared it weak, while others did not, but explained its meaning as follows: among the later members of this nation will be people who approach its earliest members in virtue, even if they are not of their number. Their virtue will be so close that an observer may be uncertain which group is better, even though Allah knows that the earliest are better. Similarly, concerning a garment whose two ends look alike, one might say, “One cannot tell which end of this garment is better,” despite knowing that one end is better than the other.

This is because he said, “One cannot tell whether its beginning or its end is better.” It is well known that Allah knows which is better, if that is the case. The lack of knowledge is attributed only to the created being, not to the Creator. What is meant is their resemblance and closeness in virtue; when two things are so alike, a created being may be uncertain which is better.

---

## Page 9443

And he was asked about the hadith reported by Anas ibn Mālik from the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), who said:

“Seven things will neither die nor perish nor taste extinction: Hell and its inhabitants, the Tablet, the Pen, the Footstool, and the Throne.”

Is this hadith authentic or not?

He answered:

This report, in this wording, is not a statement of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him), but rather a statement of certain scholars. The early generations of this nation, its leading scholars, and all the people of the Sunnah and the community have agreed that some created things will neither cease to exist nor perish altogether, such as Paradise, Hell, the Throne, and others.

Only a group of speculative theologians who espoused religious innovations maintained that all created things would perish, such as al-Jahm ibn Safwān and those among the Mu'tazilah and others who agreed with him. This is a false view that contradicts the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and the consensus of this nation’s early generations and leading scholars. These contain evidence that Paradise and its inhabitants will endure, as will other created things—evidence too extensive to mention on this sheet.

Various groups of speculative theologians and philosophers have also advanced rational arguments demonstrating the impossibility of all created things perishing. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 9444

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

Section

The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said:

“I have been given concise yet comprehensive speech”—with the additional wording, in one narration, “and its endings,” and in another, “and its beginnings and endings.”

He also said in a hadith:

“Your Prophet has been given concise yet comprehensive speech, together with its beginnings and endings.”

This is a noble and comprehensive hadith. Speech is of two kinds: performative utterances, involving requests, will, and action; and informative utterances, involving belief and knowledge. Knowledge and will, expressed respectively through statements and requests, each have many branches and encompassing principles.

These principles are of two kinds: universal, comprehensive, and general; and primary and causal. Universal and primary knowledge, together with universal and primary intentions, arrangements, and commands, encompass the whole order of existence. All the information sought concerns existing truth and intended truth.

Thus reasoning—whether rational, scriptural, or otherwise—is of two kinds: subsumptive reasoning and causal reasoning. Analogical reasoning falls within one or the other: if the attribute shared by the two analogous cases is the subject of the judgment, this is subsumptive reasoning;

---

## Page 9445

and if it is the basis of the judgment, this is causal reasoning. For when knowledge, intentions, and the informative and requestive utterances that express them are general, comprehensive, and universal, they encompass everything sought, leaving nothing that one seeks to know outside their scope. They also encompass every purpose of informative speech, leaving nothing that one seeks to intend outside their scope. This, however, is knowledge and intention concerning the things themselves, whether simple or composite.

Two causes must then be associated with them: the first is the cause, namely the efficient cause; the second is the underlying wisdom, namely the final cause. This is knowledge and intention concerning primary matters. The cause and agent are more indicative in the realm of concrete existence, while the wisdom and end are more indicative in the realm of knowledge and will. Hence the final cause is an efficient cause of the efficient cause. It is, in fact, the cause of causes, because it precedes them in knowledge and intention; because it may have no need of the effect, whereas the effect cannot dispense with it; because the agent acts only by virtue of it; and because it is the perfection and completion of existence.

For this reason, it is given precedence in His words:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

Thus, when the wise sayings that express knowledge and requests contain both beginnings and endings, they bring together the two kinds of primary causes. And when they are comprehensive, they constitute a general cause.

---

## Page 9446

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

His words in the hadith concerning distress, narrated by Ahmad on the authority of Ibn Mas‘ūd:

“‘O Allah, I am Your servant, the son of Your servant and Your maidservant. My forelock is in Your hand. I ask You by every name belonging to You, by which You have named Yourself, or which You have revealed in Your Book, or taught to any of Your creation, or kept exclusively within Your knowledge of the unseen, to make the Quran the spring rain of my heart, the light of my breast, the dispeller of my grief, and the remover of my anxiety and sorrow’—except that Allah will remove his anxiety and sorrow and replace them with joy.”

*Rabīʿ* (spring rain) is the rain that brings forth spring vegetation. Hence his words in the supplication for rain:

“O Allah, send us rain that brings relief, spring rain that makes the land flourish.”

This is the early-season rain that marks the earth with vegetation. Hence his words:

“The Quran is spring rain for the believer.”

Thus, he asked Allah to make it water through which He would revive his heart, just as He revives the earth with spring rain, and a light for his breast.

Life and light together encompass all perfection, as He said:

“Is one who was dead, whom We brought to life and gave a light by which to walk among people”

And in Ahmad ibn Hanbal’s introductory address:

“Through Allah’s Book they bring the dead to life, and through Allah’s light they give sight to the blind”; for

---

## Page 9447

through life one emerges from death, and through light one emerges from the darkness of ignorance, becoming alive, knowledgeable, and capable of speech. This constitutes the perfection of a created being’s attributes. Something similar has also been said concerning the Creator; even the Christians interpreted the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit as the Existent, the Living, and the Knowing.

Al-Ghazālī reduced Allah’s attributes to being Living and Knowing. In meaning, this agrees with the philosophers’ statement: “Intellective, intelligible, and intellect.” For knowledge follows declarative speech and entails will and directive speech, since every living being has knowledge and therefore possesses will and speech; it also entails hearing and sight. This, however, is unsatisfactory, for it may be said that being Living itself entails all the attributes and is their foundation. This is why the greatest verse in the Quran is:

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all existence.”

This is also the Greatest Name, for every living being possesses awareness and will; thus, being Living entails all the attributes. If mutual entailment were sufficient when discussing the attributes, it would suffice to mention the Living. This is useful in questions of signification and existence, but it is not valid to make the meaning of being Knowing identical to that of having Will: that which entails something is not identical to what it entails. Otherwise, the Holy Essence itself entails all the attributes.

If it is asked, “Why, then, did he combine only what brings life and light in what we are to seek, rather than confining it to life or adding ability and other attributes?” the answer is: Among living human beings, some are guided to the truth and others are not. Guidance, then, is the perfection of life. As for ability, it is a prerequisite for

---

## Page 9448

being subject to religious obligation, not for attaining felicity; thus, its absence does no harm. The light within the breast prevents one from desiring anything else.

Then, his words:

“the springtime of my heart and the light of my breast”

are explained by the fact—and Allah knows best—that rain does not extend beyond where it falls; rather, when spring rain falls upon a land, it brings it to life. Light, however, radiates beyond its source. Since the breast contains the heart, he placed the springtime in the heart and the light in the breast because its radiance spreads, as illustrated by the niche in His words:

“The likeness of His light is that of a niche in which there is a lamp; the lamp is in a glass”—which is the heart.

---

## Page 9449

Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section

As for the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“A person will be with those he loves,”

it is among the most authentic hadiths. Anas said: “After embracing Islam, nothing brought the Muslims greater joy than this hadith. I love the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Abū Bakr, and ‘Umar, and I hope that Allah will gather me with them, even though my deeds do not match theirs.” Likewise: (*)

“The strongest bonds of Islam are loving for Allah’s sake and hating for Allah’s sake.”

This, however, means that a person loves what Allah loves and those whom Allah loves. Thus, he loves all of Allah’s prophets because Allah loves them, and he loves everyone whom he knows to have died in faith and piety, for these are the friends of Allah, and Allah loves them. They include those whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, testified would enter Paradise, as well as others who participated in the Battle of Badr and the Pledge of Divine Pleasure.

Whoever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, testified would enter Paradise, we too testify will enter Paradise. As for anyone for whom he gave no such testimony, a group of scholars have said: “We do not testify that he will enter Paradise
